One-point distribution of the geodesic in directed last passage percolation
Zhipeng Liu

Abstract
We consider the geodesic of the directed last passage percolation with iid exponential weights. We find the explicit one-point distribution of the geodesic location joint with the last passage times, and its limit as the parameters go to infinity under the KPZ scaling.



1
Introduction

In recent twenty years, there has been a huge progress towards to understanding a universal class of random growth models, the so-called Kardar-Parisi-Zhang (KPZ) universality class
[
BDJ99
,
Joh00
,
Joh03
,
BFPS07
,
TW08
,
TW09
,
BC14
,
MQR17
,
DOV18
,
JR19
,
Liu19
]
. Very recently, studies about the geodesics of these models started to appear
[
BSS17
,
Ham20
,
HS20
,
BHS18
,
BGH21
,
BGH19
,
BF20
,
DSV20
,
CHHM21
,
DV21
]
. However, the explicit distributions of the geodesic are still not well understood. As far as we know, the only known related results are the distribution of the geodesic endpoint location
[
MFQR13
,
Sch12
,
BLS12
]
.

This is the first paper of an ongoing project to investigate the limiting distributions of the geodesics in one representative model, the directed last passage percolation with exponential weights, using the methods in integrable probability. We obtain the finite time one-point distribution of the geodesic location joint with the last passage times, see Theorem
1.1
. We are also able to find the large time limit of this distribution function, see Theorem
1.3
. We remark that our results are for the point-to-point geodesic. In the follow-up papers, we will consider the point-to-point and point-to-line geodesics using a different approach, and the multi-point distributions of the point-to-point geodesic.

The limiting distributions obtained in this paper are expected to be universal for all models in the KPZ universality class. See
[
DV21
]
for more discussions related to the geodesics.

Below we introduce the main results of the paper. We start from a short description of the model.

The directed last passage percolation is defined on the lattice set
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. We assign to each integer site
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
an i.i.d. exponential random variable
w(\mathbf{p})
with mean
1
. Assume that
\mathbf{p}
and
\mathbf{q}
are two lattice points satisfying
\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}
, i.e., the point
\mathbf{q}
lies in the upper right direction of
\mathbf{p}
. The last passage time from
\mathbf{p}
to
\mathbf{q}
is

L_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{q}):=\max_{\pi}\sum_{\mathbf{r}\in\pi}w(\mathbf{r}),

(1.1)

where the maximum is over all possible up/right lattice paths from
\mathbf{p}
to
\mathbf{q}
.

Since the random variables
w(\mathbf{r})
’s are continuous, the last passage time
L_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{q})
in (
1.1
) is almost surely obtained at a unique up/right lattice path, which we call the geodesic from
\mathbf{p}
to
\mathbf{q}
and denote
{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{q})
.

Note that the two neighboring sites
\mathbf{r}
and
\mathbf{r}_{+}
with
\mathbf{r}_{+}-\mathbf{r}\in\{(0,1),(1,0)\}
are on the geodesic
{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{q})
, if and only if the sites
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{r},\mathbf{r}_{+},\mathbf{q}
satisfy
\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{r}_{+}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}
, and the last passage times
L_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{r})
and
L_{\mathbf{r}_{+}}(\mathbf{q})
satisfy

L_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{r})+L_{\mathbf{r}_{+}}(\mathbf{q})=L_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{q}).

(1.2)

Throughout this paper, we always use
\mathbf{r}_{+}
to denote the lattice point following
\mathbf{r}
in the geodesic.

1.1
Finite time joint probabilities of geodesic location and last passage times

The first main result of this paper is about the joint probability that a fixed pair of neighboring sites
\mathbf{r}
and
\mathbf{r}^{\prime}
are on the geodesic
{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{q})
, and the two last passage times
L_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{r})
,
L_{\mathbf{r}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{q})
lie in some intervals.

Theorem 1.1
.

Set
\mathbf{p}=(1,1)
,
\mathbf{q}=(M,N)
. Suppose
\mathbf{r}=(m,n)
and
\mathbf{r^{\prime}}=(m+1,n)
, with
m,n
satisfying
1\leq m\leq M-1
and
1\leq n\leq N
. Assume that
t_{1},t_{2},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}
are all positive real numbers. We have

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{r}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{q}),L_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{r})\in[t_{1},t_{1}+\epsilon_{1}],L_{\mathbf{r^{\prime}}}(\mathbf{q})\in[t_{2},t_{2}+\epsilon_{2}]\right)=\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{1}+\epsilon_{1}}\int_{t_{2}}^{t_{2}+\epsilon_{2}}p(s_{1},s_{2};m,n,M,N)\mathrm{d}s_{2}\mathrm{d}s_{1},

(1.3)

where the function
p(s_{1},s_{2};m,n,M,N)
is defined in (
1.7
). Similarly, if
\mathbf{r}=(m,n)
and
\mathbf{r^{\prime}}=(m,n+1)
, with
m,n
satisfying
1\leq m\leq M
and
1\leq n\leq N-1
, the formula (
1.3
) holds with
p(s_{1},s_{2};m,n,M,N)
replaced by
p(s_{1},s_{2};n,m,N,M)
.

Remark 1.2
.

By setting
t_{1}=t_{2}=0
and
\epsilon_{1}=\epsilon_{2}=\infty
, one can derive a formula for the probability of
\mathbf{r},\mathbf{r}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{q})
without the double integral with respect to the last passage times. See (
1.11
). However, we are not able to directly perform the asymptotics analysis of this formula since the summand (
1.12
) diverges when the parameters go to infinity under the KPZ scaling, the scaling of most interests to us. Moreover, it is not very surprising that the geodesic information is intertwisted with the last passage times. In fact, it has been proved that the geodesic
{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{q})
becomes more rigid (or localized) around its expected location when the last passage time
L_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{q})
becomes very large
[
BG19
,
Liu21
]
. On the other hand, it is not concentrated around any deterministic curve when the last passage time becomes very small
[
BGS19
]
.

The proof of Theorem
1.1
is given in Section
2
.

1.2
The probability density function
p(s_{1},s_{2};m,n,M,N)

We first introduce three notations. Suppose
W=(w_{1},\cdots,w_{k})\in\mathbb{C}^{k}
is a vector, we denote

\Delta(W):=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq k}(w_{j}-w_{i}).

(1.4)

If
W=(w_{1},\cdots,w_{k})\in\mathbb{C}^{k}
and
W^{\prime}=(w^{\prime}_{1},\cdots,w^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}})\in\mathbb{C}^{k^{\prime}}
are two vectors, we denote

\Delta(W;W^{\prime}):=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\prod_{i^{\prime}=1}^{k^{\prime}}(w_{i}-w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}).

(1.5)

Finally, if
f:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
is a function and
W=(w_{1},\cdots,w_{k})\in\mathbb{C}^{k}
is a vector, or
W=\{w_{1},\cdots,w_{k}\}
with each element
w_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
, we write

f(W):=\prod_{i=1}^{k}f(w_{i}).

(1.6)

Throughout this paper, we allow the empty product and define it to be
1
.

We need to introduce six contours. Suppose
\Sigma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{out}},\Sigma_{\mathrm{L}}
and
\Sigma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{in}}
are three nested contours, from outside to inside, enclosing
-1
but not
0
. Similarly,
\Sigma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{out}},\Sigma_{\mathrm{R}}
and
\Sigma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{in}}
are three nested contours, from outside to inside, enclosing
0
but not
-1
. We further assume that the contours enclosing
-1
are disjoint from those enclosing
0
. In other words, the two outermost contours
\Sigma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{out}}
and
\Sigma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{out}}
do not intersect. All the closed contours throughout this paper are counterclockwise oriented. See Figure
1
for an illustration of these contours.

\mathbb{R}
\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}
0
-1

Figure 1:
Illustration of the contours: The three contours around
-1
from outside to inside are
\Sigma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{out}},\Sigma_{\mathrm{L}}
and
\Sigma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{in}}
respectively, and the three contours around
0
from outside to inside are
\Sigma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{out}},\Sigma_{\mathrm{R}}
and
\Sigma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{in}}
respectively.

We also introduce the notation of an integral along a small loop around a point
z_{0}
in the complex plane

\oint_{z_{0}}f(z)\mathrm{d}z:=\int_{|z-z_{0}|=\epsilon}f(z)\mathrm{d}z,

where
f(z)
is an arbitrary meromorphic function defined in a neighborhood of
z_{0}
and
\epsilon
is a sufficiently small constant.

The probability density function
p(s_{1},s_{2};m,n,M,N)
is defined to be

p(s_{1},s_{2};m,n,M,N):=\oint_{0}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\mathrm{z}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}\mathrm{(1-z)^{2}}}\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}\geq 1}\frac{1}{(k_{1}!k_{2}!)^{2}}T_{k_{1},k_{2}}(\mathrm{z};s_{1},s_{2};m,n,M,N)

(1.7)

with

\begin{split}&T_{k_{1},k_{2}}(\mathrm{z};s_{1},s_{2};m,n,M,N)\\
&:=\prod_{i_{1}=1}^{k_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{in}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}u^{(1)}_{i_{1}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}-\frac{\mathrm{z}}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{out}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}u^{(1)}_{i_{1}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)\left(\frac{1}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{in}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}v^{(1)}_{i_{1}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}-\frac{\mathrm{z}}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{out}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}v^{(1)}_{i_{1}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)\\
&\quad\cdot\prod_{i_{2}=1}^{k_{2}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{L}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}u^{(2)}_{i_{2}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{R}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}v^{(2)}_{i_{2}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\cdot\left(1-\mathrm{z}\right)^{k_{2}}\left(1-\frac{1}{\mathrm{z}}\right)^{k_{1}}\cdot\frac{f_{1}(U^{(1)};s_{1})f_{2}(U^{(2)};s_{2})}{f_{1}(V^{(1)};s_{1})f_{2}(V^{(2)};s_{2})}\cdot H(U^{(1)},U^{(2)};V^{(1)},V^{(2)})\\
&\quad\cdot\prod_{\ell=1}^{2}\frac{\left(\Delta(U^{(\ell)})\right)^{2}\left(\Delta(V^{(\ell)})\right)^{2}}{\left(\Delta(U^{(\ell)};V^{(\ell)})\right)^{2}}\cdot\frac{\Delta(U^{(1)};V^{(2)})\Delta(V^{(1)};U^{(2)})}{\Delta(U^{(1)};U^{(2)})\Delta(V^{(1)};V^{(2)})},\end{split}

(1.8)

where the vectors
U^{(\ell)}=(u_{1}^{(\ell)},\cdots,u_{k_{\ell}}^{(\ell)})
and
V^{(\ell)}=(v_{1}^{(\ell)},\cdots,v^{(\ell)}_{k_{\ell}})
for
\ell\in\{1,2\}
, the functions
f_{1},f_{2}
are defined by

\begin{split}f_{1}(w;s)&:=(w+1)^{-m}w^{n}e^{sw},\\
f_{2}(w;s)&:=(w+1)^{-M+m}w^{N-n}e^{sw},\end{split}

(1.9)

and the function
H
is defined by

\begin{split}&H(U^{(1)},U^{(2)};V^{(1)},V^{(2)})\\
&:=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{k_{1}}(u^{(1)}_{i_{1}}-v^{(1)}_{i_{1}})-\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{k_{2}}(u^{(2)}_{i_{2}}-v^{(2)}_{i_{2}})\right)^{2}\left(1+\prod_{i_{1}=1}^{k_{1}}\frac{v_{i_{1}}^{(1)}}{u_{i_{1}}^{(1)}}\prod_{i_{2}=1}^{k_{2}}\frac{u_{i_{2}}^{(2)}}{v_{i_{2}}^{(2)}}\right)\\
&\quad+\frac{1}{2}\left(-\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{k_{1}}\left((u^{(1)}_{i_{1}})^{2}-(v^{(1)}_{i_{1}})^{2}\right)+\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{k_{2}}\left((u^{(2)}_{i_{2}})^{2}-(v^{(2)}_{i_{2}})^{2}\right)\right)\left(1-\prod_{i_{1}=1}^{k_{1}}\frac{v_{i_{1}}^{(1)}}{u_{i_{1}}^{(1)}}\prod_{i_{2}=1}^{k_{2}}\frac{u_{i_{2}}^{(2)}}{v_{i_{2}}^{(2)}}\right).\end{split}

(1.10)

We remark that the formula (
1.7
) has a very similar structure with the two-point distribution formula of TASEP in
[
Liu19
]
(with step initial condition), except that we have different
\mathrm{z}
factors in the integral, and that we have an extra factor
H(U^{(1)},U^{(2)};V^{(1)},V^{(2)})
. See equations (2.1) and (2.14) in
[
Liu19
]
. It is not hard to prove that
T_{k_{1},k_{2}}
becomes zero when
k_{1}
or
k_{2}
becomes large, hence the formula (
1.7
) only involves finite many nonzero terms in the summation and is well defined.
1
1

1

In fact, we can view the integrand of (
1.8
) as a function of
V^{(1)}
and
V^{(2)}
, which equals to the product of the following three terms:
\Delta(V^{(1)})\Delta(V^{(2)})
, a Cauchy-type factor
\mathcal{C}(V^{(1)};V^{(2)})=\Delta(V^{(1)})\Delta(V^{(2)})/\Delta(V^{(1)};V^{(2)})
(see the definition in (
2.47
)), and some function which is meromorphic for each
v_{i_{\ell}}^{(\ell)}
with a possible pole at
0
but the degree of this pole is at most
\max\{n,N-n+1\}
. Note that expanding the first term
\Delta(V^{(1)})\Delta(V^{(2)})
gives a sum of terms
\prod_{1\leq\ell_{1}\leq k_{1}}(v_{\sigma(\ell_{1})}^{(1)})^{k_{1}-\ell_{1}}\prod_{1\leq\ell_{2}\leq k_{2}}(v_{\pi(\ell_{2})}^{(2)})^{k_{2}-\ell_{2}}
over permutations
\sigma\in S_{k_{1}}
and
\pi\in S_{k_{2}}
, here
S_{k}
denotes the permutation group of
\{1,2,\cdots,k\}
. If
k_{1}
is large enough (the case when
k_{2}
is large is similar), for example if
k_{1}>N
, the integrand is analytic for
v_{\sigma(1)}^{(1)}
at
0
by checking the degrees. So when we integrate
v_{\sigma(1)}^{(1)}
, the only possible nontrivial contribution is from the residues
v_{\sigma(1)}^{(1)}=v_{j}^{(2)}
if
v_{j}^{(2)}
lies inside the contour of
v_{\sigma(1)}^{(1)}
due to the Cauchy-type factor. However, if we further integrate
v_{j}^{(2)}
we find each residue contribution is also zero by checking the degree of
v_{j}^{(2)}
which is
k_{1}-1-n-(N-n+1)=k_{1}-N>0
. We remark that the proof does not rely on the explicit formula of
H
or the variable
\mathrm{z}
, and it is similar to the argument for the two-point distribution formula of TASEP (see Remark 2.8 of
[
Liu19
]
) where they do not have the factor
H
.

Finally, by exchanging the integral and summations, and using the identity
\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{f_{\ell}(U^{(\ell)};s_{\ell})}{f_{\ell}(V^{(\ell)};s_{\ell})}\mathrm{d}s_{\ell}=\frac{f_{\ell}(U^{(\ell)};0)}{f_{\ell}(V^{(\ell)};0)}\cdot\frac{1}{\sum_{i_{\ell}=1}^{k_{\ell}}(v_{i_{\ell}}^{(\ell)}-u_{i_{\ell}}^{(\ell)})}
since
\mathrm{Re}(v_{i_{\ell}}^{(\ell)}-u_{i_{\ell}}^{(\ell)})<0
due to the locations of the contours, we obtain

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{r}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{q})\right)&=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}p(s_{1},s_{2};m,n,M,N)\mathrm{d}s_{1}\mathrm{d}s_{2}\\
&=\oint_{0}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\mathrm{z}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}\mathrm{(1-z)^{2}}}\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}\geq 1}\frac{1}{(k_{1}!k_{2}!)^{2}}\mathcal{T}_{k_{1},k_{2}}(\mathrm{z};m,n,M,N),\end{split}

(1.11)

where

\begin{split}&\mathcal{T}_{k_{1},k_{2}}(\mathrm{z};m,n,M,N)\\
&:=\prod_{i_{1}=1}^{k_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{in}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}u^{(1)}_{i_{1}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}-\frac{\mathrm{z}}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{out}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}u^{(1)}_{i_{1}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)\left(\frac{1}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{in}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}v^{(1)}_{i_{1}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}-\frac{\mathrm{z}}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{out}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}v^{(1)}_{i_{1}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)\\
&\quad\cdot\prod_{i_{2}=1}^{k_{2}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{L}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}u^{(2)}_{i_{2}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{R}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}v^{(2)}_{i_{2}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\cdot\left(1-\mathrm{z}\right)^{k_{2}}\left(1-\frac{1}{\mathrm{z}}\right)^{k_{1}}\cdot\frac{f_{1}(U^{(1)};0)f_{2}(U^{(2)};0)}{f_{1}(V^{(1)};0)f_{2}(V^{(2)};0)}\cdot\frac{1}{\prod_{\ell=1}^{2}\sum_{i_{\ell}=1}^{k_{\ell}}(u_{i_{\ell}}^{(\ell)}-v_{i_{\ell}}^{(\ell)})}\\
&\quad\cdot H(U^{(1)},U^{(2)};V^{(1)},V^{(2)})\cdot\prod_{\ell=1}^{2}\frac{\left(\Delta(U^{(\ell)})\right)^{2}\left(\Delta(V^{(\ell)})\right)^{2}}{\left(\Delta(U^{(\ell)};V^{(\ell)})\right)^{2}}\cdot\frac{\Delta(U^{(1)};V^{(2)})\Delta(V^{(1)};U^{(2)})}{\Delta(U^{(1)};U^{(2)})\Delta(V^{(1)};V^{(2)})}.\end{split}

(1.12)

1.3
Limiting joint distribution of geodesic location and last passage times

For any two lattice points
\mathbf{p}=(\mathrm{p}_{1},\mathrm{p}_{2})
and
\mathbf{q}=(\mathrm{q}_{1},\mathrm{q}_{2})
satisfying
\mathrm{p}_{1}\leq\mathrm{q}_{1}
and
\mathrm{p}_{2}\leq\mathrm{q}_{2}
, we define

d(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}):=\left(\sqrt{\mathrm{q}_{1}-\mathrm{p}_{1}}+\sqrt{\mathrm{q}_{2}-\mathrm{p}_{2}}\right)^{2}.

(1.13)

We say a geodesic
{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{q})
exits a set
A
at a point
\mathbf{r}
, if and only if the geodesic intersects
A
and
\mathbf{r}
is the last point of the intersection, i.e.,
\mathbf{r}\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{q})\cap A
and
\mathbf{r}_{+}\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{q})\setminus A
.

Theorem 1.3
.

Suppose
\alpha>0
,
\gamma\in(0,1)
are fixed constants. Assume
x_{1},x_{2},x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2}
are four real numbers satisfying
x_{1}>x^{\prime}_{1}
and
x_{2}<x^{\prime}_{2}
. Let

\begin{split}M&=[\alpha N],\\
m&=[\gamma\alpha N+x_{1}\alpha^{2/3}(1+\sqrt{\alpha})^{2/3}N^{2/3}],\\
n&=[\gamma N+x_{2}\alpha^{-1/3}(1+\sqrt{\alpha})^{2/3}N^{2/3}],\\
m^{\prime}&=[\gamma\alpha N+x^{\prime}_{1}\alpha^{2/3}(1+\sqrt{\alpha})^{2/3}N^{2/3}],\\
n^{\prime}&=[\gamma N+x^{\prime}_{2}\alpha^{-1/3}(1+\sqrt{\alpha})^{2/3}N^{2/3}],\end{split}

(1.14)

where
[x]
denotes the largest integer which is smaller than or equal to
x
.
Suppose
\pi
is an up/left lattice path from
(m,n)
to
(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})
. Then

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathcal{G}}_{(1,1)}{(M,N)}\text{ intersects $\pi$,}\\
\mbox{and }L_{(1,1)}(\mathbf{p})\geq d((1,1),\mathbf{p})+\mathrm{t}_{1}\cdot\alpha^{-1/6}(1+\sqrt{\alpha})^{4/3}N^{1/3},\\
\mbox{and }L_{\mathbf{p}_{+}}(M,N)\geq d(\mathbf{p}_{+},(M,N))+\mathrm{t}_{2}\cdot\alpha^{-1/6}(1+\sqrt{\alpha})^{4/3}N^{1/3},\\
\mbox{where $\mathbf{p}$ denotes the exit point of ${\mathcal{G}}_{(1,1)}{(M,N)}$ on $\pi$}\\
\end{array}\right)

(1.15)

exists and is independent of the choice of
\pi
. The limit equals to

\int_{x_{2}-x_{1}}^{x^{\prime}_{2}-x^{\prime}_{1}}\int_{\mathrm{t}_{1}}^{\infty}\int_{\mathrm{t}_{2}}^{\infty}\mathrm{p}(\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{2},\mathrm{x};\gamma)\mathrm{d}\mathrm{s}_{2}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{s}_{1}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x},

(1.16)

where the joint probability density function
\mathrm{p}(\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{2},\mathrm{x};\gamma)
is defined in (
1.22
).

(m,n)
(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})
(1,1)
(M,N)

Figure 2:
The thick path denotes the geodesic
{\mathcal{G}}_{(1,1)}(M,N)
. The spring-shaped lattice path denotes
\pi
. The star-shaped point is the exit point of
{\mathcal{G}}_{(1,1)}(M,N)
on
\pi
, and the square-shaped point is the next point on
{\mathcal{G}}_{(1,1)}(M,N)
after the exit point.

See Figure
2
for an illustration. The proof of Theorem
1.3
is provided in Section
3
.

We expect that the geodesic is around a straight line from
(1,1)
to
(M,N)
. The line is of slope
\alpha^{-1}\approx N/M
. Then
x_{2}-x_{1}
and
x^{\prime}_{2}-x^{\prime}_{1}
can be viewed as (after appropriate scaling) the shifts of moving
(m,n)
and
(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})
to the line. Similarly, in the density function
\mathrm{p}(\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{2},\mathrm{x};\gamma)
,
\mathrm{x}
can be viewed as the shift of moving the exit point
\mathbf{p}
to the line. See Figure
4
at the beginning of Section
3
for an illustration.

It might look surprising at a first glance that the limiting distribution is independent of
\pi
, but only depends on the locations of the endpoints. Here we provide an intuitive explanation. Suppose we have a different up/left lattice path
\pi^{\prime}
from
(m,n)
to
(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})
. For any point
\mathbf{q}\in\pi
, we can find a unique point
\mathbf{q^{\prime}}\in\pi^{\prime}
such that
\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{q}^{\prime}\in\{(\alpha y,y):y\in\mathbb{R}\}
. Note that the distance between
\mathbf{q}
and
\mathbf{q}^{\prime}
is at most of order
O(N^{2/3})\ll o(N)
. By the uniform slow decorrelation of the directed last passage percolation
[
CFP12
,
CLW16
]
,
N^{-1/3}({L_{(1,1)}(\mathbf{q})-d((1,1),\mathbf{q})})-N^{-1/3}({L_{(1,1)}(\mathbf{q}^{\prime})-d((1,1),\mathbf{q}^{\prime})})
converges to
0
in probability as
N\to\infty
. Moreover, with appropriate scaling, the limiting process of the last passage times from
(1,1)
(and from
(M,N)
similarly) to the points of
\pi
has the same law as that to the points of
\pi^{\prime}
. Therefore we expect the limit of (
1.15
) is independent of
\pi
. This probabilistic argument is heuristic but it might be possible to make it rigorous. In this paper, we will use an analytical way to show this independence instead. See the argument after Proposition
3.1
in Section
3
.

Note that the geodesic
{\mathcal{G}}_{(1,1)}{(M,N)}
intersects a rectangle with vertices
(m,n),(m,n^{\prime}),(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})
and
(m^{\prime},n)
if and only if
{\mathcal{G}}_{(1,1)}{(M,N)}
intersects a lattice path from
(m,n)
to
(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})
. Thus by setting
\mathrm{t}_{1},\mathrm{t}_{2}\to-\infty
we immediately have

\begin{split}&\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left({\mathcal{G}}_{(1,1)}{(M,N)}\text{ intersects the rectangle with vertices $(m,n),(m,n^{\prime}),(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})$ and $(m^{\prime},n)$}\right)\\
&=\int_{x_{2}-x_{1}}^{x^{\prime}_{2}-x^{\prime}_{1}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{p}(\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{2},\mathrm{x};\gamma)\mathrm{d}\mathrm{s}_{2}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{s}_{1}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}.\end{split}

(1.17)

Now we discuss an application of Theorem
1.3
.

Corollary 1.4
.

Let
\mathcal{A}^{(1)}
and
\mathcal{A}^{(2)}
be two independent Airy
2
processes. Denote the parabolic Airy
2
processes
\mathcal{\hat{A}}^{(\ell)}(\mathrm{x})=\mathcal{A}^{(\ell)}(\mathrm{x})-\mathrm{x}^{2}
,
\ell=1,2
. Suppose
\gamma\in(0,1)
is a fixed constant. Denote

\mathcal{T}=\mathrm{argmax}_{\mathrm{x}}\left(\gamma^{1/3}\mathcal{\hat{A}}^{(1)}\left(\frac{\mathrm{x}}{2\gamma^{2/3}}\right)+(1-\gamma)^{1/3}\mathcal{\hat{A}}^{(2)}\left(\frac{\mathrm{x}}{2(1-\gamma)^{2/3}}\right)\right).

Then
\mathrm{p}(\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{2},\mathrm{x};\gamma)
is the joint probability density function of
\gamma^{1/3}\mathcal{A}^{(1)}\left(\frac{\mathcal{T}}{2\gamma^{2/3}}\right),(1-\gamma)^{1/3}\mathcal{A}^{(2)}\left(\frac{\mathcal{T}}{2(1-\gamma)^{2/3}}\right)
and
\mathcal{T}
.

Proof.

Denote
\pi
the line
\{(x,y):x+y=2\gamma N\}
.
It is known
[
Joh03
]
that the processes of the last passage times from
(1,1)
(or
(N,N)
) to the points on
\pi
after appropriate scaling converge to two independent parabolic Airy
2
processes as
N\to\infty
. More explicitly, for any constant
K
,

\frac{L_{(1,1)}(\gamma N-2^{-1/3}\mathrm{x}N^{2/3},\gamma N+2^{-1/3}\mathrm{x}N^{2/3})-4\gamma N}{2^{4/3}N^{1/3}}\to\gamma^{1/3}\mathcal{\hat{A}}^{(1)}\left(\frac{\mathrm{x}}{2\gamma^{2/3}}\right),\quad|\mathrm{x}|\leq K

(1.18)

and

\frac{L_{(\gamma N-2^{-1/3}\mathrm{x}N^{2/3},\gamma N+2^{-1/3}\mathrm{x}N^{2/3})}(N,N)-4(1-\gamma)N}{2^{4/3}N^{1/3}}\to(1-\gamma)^{1/3}\mathcal{\hat{A}}^{(2)}\left(\frac{\mathrm{x}}{2(1-\gamma^{2/3})}\right),\quad|\mathrm{x}|\leq K

(1.19)

as
N\to\infty
. Both processes are tight in the space of continuous functions on
[-K,K]
(see
[
FO18
, Theorem 2.3]
for example). Note that the geodesic passes through a point
\mathbf{q}
on the line
\pi
if and only if
L_{(1,1)}(\mathbf{q})+L_{\mathbf{q}}(N,N)
reaches the maximum. And the probability that this intersection point
\mathbf{q}
lies outside of
\{(\gamma N-2^{-1/3}\mathrm{x}N^{2/3},\gamma N+2^{-1/3}\mathrm{x}N^{2/3}):|\mathrm{x}|\leq K\}
decays exponentially as
N\to\infty
and
K
becomes large (see
[
BL16
, Proposition 2.1]
for example). Also note that the argmax
\mathcal{T}
is unique since it represents the geodesic location in the limiting directed landscape and the geodesic is unique (see
[
DV21
]
). Using the above facts, we conclude that the location of the intersection of
{\mathcal{G}}_{(1,1)}(N,N)
and
\pi
, the argmax of the left hand side of (
1.18
)
+
(
1.19
), converges to
\mathcal{T}
. Now we apply Theorem
1.3
with
\alpha=1
and use the facts that

d_{(1,1)}(\gamma N-2^{-1/3}\mathrm{x}N^{2/3},\gamma N+2^{-1/3}\mathrm{x}N^{2/3})=4\gamma N+\frac{\mathrm{x}^{2}}{2^{2/3}\gamma}N^{1/3}+o(1)

and

d_{(\gamma N-2^{-1/3}\mathrm{x}N^{2/3},\gamma N+2^{-1/3}\mathrm{x}N^{2/3})}(N,N)=4(1-\gamma)N+\frac{\mathrm{x}^{2}}{2^{2/3}(1-\gamma)}N^{1/3}+o(1).

Corollary
1.4
follows immediately.
∎

The explicit distribution of
\mathcal{T}
was an interesting open problem in the community before, see
[
DOV18
, Problem 14.4(a)]
for example. Our result above resolves this problem. It is also possible to apply this result and the formula of
\mathrm{p}(\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{2},\mathrm{x};\gamma)
to obtain some properties of the directed landscape, the limiting four-parameter random field of the directed last passage percolation. For example, in a follow-up paper
[
Liu21
]
we proved that when the height of the directed landscape at a point is sufficiently large, the geodesic to this point is rigid and the location has a Gaussian distribution under appropriate scaling.

We remark that the density function
\mathrm{p}(\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{2},\mathrm{x};\gamma)
can be related to the well-known GUE Tracy-Widom distribution. Note that the max of
\gamma^{1/3}\mathcal{\hat{A}}^{(1)}\left(\frac{\mathrm{x}}{2\gamma^{2/3}}\right)+(1-\gamma)^{1/3}\mathcal{\hat{A}}^{(2)}\left(\frac{\mathrm{x}}{2(1-\gamma)^{2/3}}\right)
satisfies

\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{\mathrm{x}\in\mathbb{R}}\left\{\gamma^{1/3}\mathcal{\hat{A}}^{(1)}\left(\frac{\mathrm{x}}{2\gamma^{2/3}}\right)+(1-\gamma)^{1/3}\mathcal{\hat{A}}^{(2)}\left(\frac{\mathrm{x}}{2(1-\gamma)^{2/3}}\right)\right\}\leq\mathrm{s}\right)=F_{GUE}(\mathrm{s}),

where
F_{GUE}(\mathrm{s})
is the GUE Tracy-Widom distribution. See
[
BL14
,
BL13
]
for more details. By applying the Corollary
1.4
and noting
\mathcal{\hat{A}}^{(\ell)}(\mathrm{x})=\mathcal{A}^{(\ell)}(\mathrm{x})-\mathrm{x}^{2}
, we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{x}\iint_{\mathrm{s}_{1}+\mathrm{s_{2}}\leq\mathrm{s}}\mathrm{ds_{1}ds_{2}}\mathrm{p}\left(\mathrm{s}_{1}+\frac{\mathrm{x}^{2}}{4\gamma},\mathrm{s}_{2}+\frac{\mathrm{x}^{2}}{4(1-\gamma)},\mathrm{x};\gamma\right)=F_{GUE}(\mathrm{s}).

(1.20)

One might be able to obtain the tail estimates for the geodesic using the formula (
1.3
). After a preliminary calculation, we have the following conjecture.

Conjecture 1.5
.

Let
M,N
and
m,n
be numbers satisfying the scaling (
3.1
) in Theorem
1.3
, then

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left({\mathcal{G}}_{(1,1)}(M,N)\text{ lies above }(m,n)\right)=e^{-c\mathrm{x}^{3}+o(\mathrm{x})}\quad\text{with}\quad c=\frac{1}{6(\gamma(1-\gamma))^{3/2}},

(1.21)

when
\mathrm{x}=x_{2}-x_{1}
becomes large.

It also might be possible to obtain a more accurate estimate from this formula. We leave it as a future project.

1.4
The limiting density function
\mathrm{p}(\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{2},\mathrm{x};\gamma)

The limiting density function
\mathrm{p}(\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{2},\mathrm{x};\gamma)
has a similar structure as the finite time probability density function
p(s_{1},s_{2};m,n,M,N)
. Before we write down the formula, we introduce some contours. Suppose
\Gamma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{in}},\Gamma_{\mathrm{L}}
and
\Gamma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{out}}
are three disjoint contours on the left half plane each of which starts from
e^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}/3}\infty
and ends to
e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}/3}\infty
. Here
\Gamma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{in}}
is the leftmost contour and
\Gamma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{out}}
is the rightmost contour. The index “
\mathrm{in}
” and “
\mathrm{out}
” refer to the relative location compared with
-\infty
. Similarly, suppose
\Gamma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{in}},\Gamma_{\mathrm{R}}
and
\Gamma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{out}}
are three disjoint contours on the right half plane each of which starts from
e^{-\pi\mathrm{i}/3}\infty
and ends to
e^{\pi\mathrm{i}/3}\infty
. Here the index “
\mathrm{in}
” and “
\mathrm{out}
” refer to the relative location compared with
+\infty
, hence
\Gamma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{in}}
is the rightmost contour and
\Gamma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{out}}
is the leftmost contour. See Figure
3
for an illustration of these contours.

\mathbb{R}
\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}
0

Figure 3:
Illustration of the contours: The three contours in the left half plane from left to right are
\Gamma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{in}},\Gamma_{\mathrm{L}}
and
\Gamma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{out}}
respectively, and the three contours in the right half plane from left to right are
\Gamma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{out}},\Gamma_{\mathrm{R}}
and
\Gamma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{in}}
respectively.

The probability density function
\mathrm{p}(\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{2},\mathrm{x};\gamma)
is defined to be

\mathrm{p}(\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{2},\mathrm{x};\gamma):=\oint_{0}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\mathrm{z}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}\mathrm{(1-z)^{2}}}\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}\geq 1}\frac{1}{(k_{1}!k_{2}!)^{2}}\mathrm{T}_{k_{1},k_{2}}(\mathrm{z};\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{2},\mathrm{x};\gamma)

(1.22)

with

\begin{split}&\mathrm{T}_{k_{1},k_{2}}(\mathrm{z};\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{2},\mathrm{x};\gamma)\\
&:=\prod_{i_{1}=1}^{k_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Gamma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{in}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\xi^{(1)}_{i_{1}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}-\frac{\mathrm{z}}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Gamma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{out}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\xi^{(1)}_{i_{1}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)\left(\frac{1}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Gamma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{in}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\eta^{(1)}_{i_{1}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}-\frac{\mathrm{z}}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Gamma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{out}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\eta^{(1)}_{i_{1}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)\\
&\quad\cdot\prod_{i_{2}=1}^{k_{2}}\int_{\Gamma_{\mathrm{L}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\xi^{(2)}_{i_{2}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\int_{\Gamma_{\mathrm{R}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\eta^{(2)}_{i_{2}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\cdot\left(1-\mathrm{z}\right)^{k_{2}}\left(1-\frac{1}{\mathrm{z}}\right)^{k_{1}}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{f}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(1)};\mathrm{s}_{1})\mathrm{f}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(2)};\mathrm{s}_{2})}{\mathrm{f}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(1)};\mathrm{s}_{1})\mathrm{f}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(2)};\mathrm{s}_{2})}\cdot\mathrm{H}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(2)})\\
&\quad\cdot\prod_{\ell=1}^{2}\frac{\left(\Delta(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(\ell)})\right)^{2}\left(\Delta(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(\ell)})\right)^{2}}{\left(\Delta(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(\ell)};\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(\ell)})\right)^{2}}\cdot\frac{\Delta(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(2)})\Delta(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(2)})}{\Delta(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(2)})\Delta(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(2)})},\end{split}

(1.23)

where the vectors
\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(\ell)}=(\xi_{1}^{(\ell)},\cdots,\xi_{i_{\ell}}^{(\ell)})
and
\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(\ell)}=(\eta_{1}^{(\ell)},\cdots,\eta^{(\ell)}_{i_{\ell}})
for
\ell\in\{1,2\}
, the functions
\mathrm{f}_{1},\mathrm{f}_{2}
are defined by

\begin{split}\mathrm{f}_{1}(\zeta;\mathrm{s})&:=\exp\left(-\frac{\gamma}{3}\zeta^{3}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{x}\zeta^{2}+\left(\mathrm{s}-\frac{\mathrm{x}^{2}}{4\gamma}\right)\zeta\right),\\
\mathrm{f}_{2}(\zeta;\mathrm{s})&:=\exp\left(-\frac{(1-\gamma)}{3}\zeta^{3}+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{x}\zeta^{2}+\left(\mathrm{s}-\frac{\mathrm{x}^{2}}{4(1-\gamma)}\right)\zeta\right),\end{split}

(1.24)

and the function
\mathrm{H}
is defined by

\mathrm{H}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(2)})=\frac{1}{12}\mathrm{S}_{1}^{4}+\frac{1}{4}\mathrm{S}_{2}^{2}-\frac{1}{3}\mathrm{S}_{1}\mathrm{S}_{3}

(1.25)

with

\mathrm{S}_{\ell}=\mathrm{S}_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(2)})=\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{k_{1}}\left(\left(\xi_{i_{1}}^{(1)}\right)^{\ell}-\left(\eta_{i_{1}}^{(1)}\right)^{\ell}\right)-\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{k_{2}}\left(\left(\xi_{i_{2}}^{(2)}\right)^{\ell}-\left(\eta_{i_{2}}^{(2)}\right)^{\ell}\right).

(1.26)

Remark 1.6
.

It can be directly verified that
\mathrm{T}
is symmetric on
\mathrm{x}
, i.e., it satisfies
\mathrm{T}_{k_{1},k_{2}}(\mathrm{z};\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{2},\mathrm{x};\gamma)=\mathrm{T}_{k_{1},k_{2}}(\mathrm{z};\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{2},-\mathrm{x};\gamma)
. In fact, one can see it clearly by changing variables
\xi_{i_{\ell}}^{(\ell)}=-\tilde{\eta}_{i_{\ell}}^{(\ell)}
and
\eta_{i_{\ell}}^{(\ell)}=-\tilde{\xi}_{i_{\ell}}^{(\ell)}
for
1\leq i_{\ell}\leq k_{\ell}
and
\ell=1,2
.

One can prove that the summation is absolutely convergent in (
1.22
) due to the super-exponential decay of
\mathrm{f}_{\ell}
along the integral contours. The proof is similar to that of Lemma
3.3
so we omit it.

2
Finite time formulas and proof of Theorem
1.1

2.1
Outline of the proof

Theorem
1.1
states two formulas for different locations of
\mathbf{r}^{\prime}
. The equation (
1.3
) holds when
\mathbf{r}^{\prime}=(m+1,n)
, i.e., when
\mathbf{r}^{\prime}
is at the same row as
\mathbf{r}
. The case when
\mathbf{r}^{\prime}
is at the same column as
\mathbf{r}
follows by switching the rows and columns of the model. Thus it is sufficient to show the equation (
1.3
) with
\mathbf{r}^{\prime}=(m+1,n)
.

The proof involves a few computations and identities. We would like to split the proof into three steps, each of which ends with an identity about the probability density function
p(s_{1},s_{2};m,n,M,N)
. We will outline the steps and state these main identities in this subsection and leave their proofs in subsequent subsections.

In the first step, we obtain a formula for
p(s_{1},s_{2};m,n,M,N)
. The main idea is to convert the desired probability to a sum of the product of two transition probabilities, and evaluate the sum explicitly. There are two types of transition probabilities for the exponential directed last passage percolation. One is the transition probability by viewing its equivalent model, the so-called TASEP, as a Markov process with respect to time
[
Sch97
]
. The second one is the transition probability by viewing the model as a Markov chain along one dimension on the space
[
Joh10
]
. It turns out that only the later one can be used to find an exact formula for
p(s_{1},s_{2};m,n,M,N)
. If one uses the transition probabilities of TASEP instead, there will be an
\mathcal{O}(1)
error on the finite time formulas but the resulting limit probability densities
\mathrm{p}(\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{2},\mathrm{x};\gamma)
is the same. We will consider this approach in a follow-up paper.

Using the transition probability formula of
[
Joh10
]
and an summation identity for the product of two eigenfunctions, we obtain the following proposition.

Proposition 2.1
.

We have the following formula for
p(s_{1},s_{2};m,n,M,N)

\begin{split}&p(s_{1},s_{2};m,n,M,N)\\
&=\frac{(-1)^{N(N-1)/2}}{(N!)^{2}}\oint_{0}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\mathrm{z}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}\mathrm{z}^{n}}\prod_{i_{1}=1}^{N}\int_{|w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}|=R_{1}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\prod_{i_{2}=1}^{N}\int_{|w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}|=R_{2}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\Delta\left(W^{(1)}\right)\Delta\left(W^{(2)}\right)\\
&\quad\cdot\tilde{f}_{1}\left(W^{(1)}\right)\tilde{f}_{2}\left(W^{(2)}\right)\cdot\sum_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}=1}^{N}(-1)^{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}}\frac{\left(w_{\ell_{1}}^{(1)}\right)^{n}e^{s_{1}w_{\ell_{1}}^{(1)}}}{\left(w_{\ell_{2}}^{(2)}\right)^{n-1}e^{s_{1}w_{\ell_{2}}^{(2)}}}\det\left[C_{\mathrm{z}}\left(w_{i_{1}}^{(1)},w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}\right)+D_{\mathrm{z}}\left(w_{i_{1}}^{(1)},w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}\right)\right]_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1}\neq\ell_{1},\\
i_{2}\neq\ell_{2}\end{subarray}}.\end{split}

(2.1)

Here the radii of the contours satisfy
R_{1}>R_{2}>1
. The vectors
W^{(1)}
and
W^{(2)}
are defined by

W^{(1)}=(w_{1}^{(1)},\cdots,w_{N}^{(1)}),\quad W^{(2)}=(w_{1}^{(2)},\cdots,w_{N}^{(2)}).

Recall our conventions
\Delta(W)
and
f(W)
as in (
1.4
) and (
1.6
). The functions
\tilde{f}_{1}
and
\tilde{f}_{2}
are defined by

\tilde{f}_{1}(w):=w^{-N}(w+1)^{-m},\quad\tilde{f}_{2}(w):=(w+1)^{-M+m}e^{(s_{1}+s_{2})w}.

(2.2)

The functions
C
and
D
appearing in the determinant are defined by

C_{\mathrm{z}}(w_{1},w_{2}):=\frac{\mathrm{z}}{w_{1}-w_{2}}\frac{w_{1}^{n-1}e^{s_{1}w_{1}}}{w_{2}^{n-1}e^{s_{1}w_{2}}}+\frac{1}{-w_{1}+w_{2}}\frac{w_{1}^{n+1}e^{s_{1}w_{1}}}{w_{2}^{n+1}e^{s_{1}w_{2}}},

(2.3)

and

D_{\mathrm{z}}(w_{1},w_{2}):=\frac{\mathrm{z}}{-w_{1}+w_{2}}\frac{w_{1}}{w_{2}}+\frac{1}{w_{1}-w_{2}}\frac{w_{1}^{N}e^{(s_{1}+s_{2})w_{1}}}{w_{2}^{N}e^{(s_{1}+s_{2})w_{2}}}.

(2.4)

The proof of Proposition
2.1
is provided in the next subsection
2.2
.

It seems that the formula (
2.1
) is not suitable for asymptotic analysis by the following two reasons. The first reason is that this formula involves some unneeded information. Note that the two terms in
D_{\mathrm{z}}(w_{1},w_{2})
have factors
(w_{1}/w_{2})^{1}
and
(w_{1}/w_{2})^{N}
whose exponents
1
and
N
indeed represent the bounds of the possible locations of the geodesic. However, we expect that the geodesic only fluctuates of order
N^{2/3}
around its expected location. In other words, changing the far endpoints
1
and
N
will not affect the asymptotics. Therefore,
D_{\mathrm{z}}(w_{1},w_{2})
should not appear in the limit and we need to reformulate (
2.1
) and remove the term
D_{\mathrm{z}}(w_{1},w_{2})
. The second reason is that the formula (
2.1
) contains some determinants of size
\mathcal{O}(N)
, such as the Vandermonde determinants
\Delta\left(W^{(1)}\right)
and
\Delta\left(W^{(2)}\right)
, and the determinant
\det(C_{\mathrm{z}}+D_{\mathrm{z}})
. It is typically hard to find the asymptotics of these determinants when the size
N\to\infty
. We will need to rewrite it to a formula which is more suitable for asymptotic analysis.

In the second step, we take the term
D_{\mathrm{z}}(w_{1},w_{2})
away at the cost of changing the integral contours, and then evaluate the summation over
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}
. We obtain

Proposition 2.2
.

The equation (
2.1
) is equivalent to

\begin{split}&p(s_{1},s_{2};m,n,M,N)=\frac{1}{(N!)^{2}}\oint_{0}\frac{(1-\mathrm{z})^{N-2}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{z}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}\mathrm{z}^{n}}\prod_{i_{1}=1}^{N}\left(\frac{-\mathrm{z}}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}+\frac{1}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)\prod_{i_{2}=1}^{N}\int_{\Sigma}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\\
&\quad\hat{f}_{1}\left(W^{(1)}\right)\hat{f}_{2}\left(W^{(2)}\right)\frac{\left(\Delta\left(W^{(1)}\right)\right)^{2}\left(\Delta\left(W^{(2)}\right)\right)^{2}}{\Delta\left(W^{(2)};W^{(1)}\right)}\cdot\left(\hat{H}\left(W^{(1)};W^{(2)}\right)+\mathrm{z}\frac{\prod_{i_{2}=1}^{N}w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}}{\prod_{i_{1}=1}^{N}w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}}\hat{H}\left(W^{(2)};W^{(1)}\right)\right),\end{split}

(2.5)

where the contours
\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
,
\Sigma
, and
\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
are three nested closed contours, from outside to inside, all of which enclose both
0
and
-1
. The vectors
W^{(1)}:=(w_{1}^{(1)},\cdots,w_{N}^{(1)})
and
W^{(2)}:=(w_{1}^{(2)},\cdots,w_{N}^{(2)})
. The functions

\hat{f}_{1}(w):=(w+1)^{-m}w^{-N+n}e^{s_{1}w},\quad\hat{f}_{2}(w):=(w+1)^{-M+m}w^{-n}e^{s_{2}w},

(2.6)

and

\hat{H}\left(W;W^{\prime}\right):=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{i}w_{i}-\sum_{i^{\prime}}w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}\right)^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{i}w_{i}^{2}-\sum_{i^{\prime}}\left(w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}\right)^{2}\right)

(2.7)

for any vectors
W=(\cdots,w_{i},\cdots)
and
W^{\prime}=(\cdots,w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}},\cdots)
of finite sizes.

We remark that the idea of changing the integral contours is constructive. It results in a compact formula which effectively removes the terms including the information of the geodesic bounds. Formulas from similar summations (for product of two eigenfunctions in TASEP as we did in the proof of Proposition
2.1
) without including the information of the summation bounds were also obtained in the periodic version of the directed last passage percolation
[
BL18
,
BL19
,
BL21
]
and its large period limit
[
Liu19
]
. Heuristically, in the periodic model it turned out that the upper bound (in the previous period) cancels out the lower bound (in the current period) in the summation. While in this paper, we construct contours
\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
and
\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
which play similar roles as different periods: integral of the terms involving the upper bound along one contour cancels that involving the lower bound along the other contour.

The proof of Proposition
2.2
is provided in subsection
2.3
.

In the last step, we rewrite the formula (
2.5
) in the form with a structure similar to a Fredholm determinant expansion, which is the formula (
1.7
).

Proposition 2.3
.

The formula (
2.5
) is equivalent to (
1.7
).

The proof of Proposition
2.3
is provided in subsection
2.4
. It involves an extension of a Cauchy-type summation formula in
[
Liu19
]
. We first convert the integral into discrete summations over a so-called Bethe roots, then reformulate the summation as a Fredholm-determinant-like expansion, and finally convert the discrete summation back into integrals. It would be nice to see a more direct proof for Proposition
2.3
but it seems quite complicated considering the differences between the two formulas.

2.2
Proof of Proposition
2.1

As we mentioned in the previous subsection, we need a transition probability formula by viewing the directed last passage percolation as a Markov chain. Such a formula was obtained in
[
Joh10
]
for the geometric directed last passage percolation, which is a discrete version of the model we are considering in this paper. We will introduce the model below. Then we will show how to compute an analogous probability for the geodesic in the geometric model, and take the limit to get the results for exponential directed last passage percolation.

The geometric last passage percolation model is defined as follows. We assign to each site
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
an i.i.d. geometric random variables
\tilde{w}(\mathbf{p})
with parameter
q\in(0,1)

\mathbb{P}\left(\tilde{w}(\mathbf{p})=i\right)=(1-q)q^{i},\qquad i=0,1,2,\cdots

(2.8)

for each integer site
\mathbf{p}
. Note that if we take
q=1-\epsilon
and let
\epsilon\to 0
,
\epsilon\tilde{w}(\mathbf{p})
converges to an exponential random variable.

Similar to (
1.1
), if a lattice point
\mathbf{q}
lies in the upper right direction of another lattice point
\mathbf{p}
, we define the last passage time from
\mathbf{p}
to
\mathbf{q}
as

G_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{q}):=\max_{\pi}\sum_{\mathbf{r}\in\pi}\tilde{w}(\mathbf{r}),

(2.9)

where the maximum is over all possible up/right lattice paths from
\mathbf{p}
to
\mathbf{q}
. We remark that the maximal path is not necessary unique in this model. We call these maximal paths the geodesics from
\mathbf{p}
to
\mathbf{q}
.

We consider the following event

A=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}G_{(1,1)}(m,n)+G_{(m+1,n)}(M,N)=G_{(1,1)}(M,N),\\
G_{(1,1)}(m,n)=x,\\
G_{(m+1,n)}(M,N)=y.\end{array}\right\}.

(2.10)

Here
x
and
y
are nonnegative integers. As we mentioned before, there may be more than one geodesic. The event
A
means that there is one geodesic that passes through the two points
(m,n)
and
(m+1,n)
, and these two points split the last passage time
G_{(1,1)}(M,N)
into two parts
G_{(1,1)}(m,n)=x
and
G_{(m+1,n)}(M,N)=y
. Later we will show

Lemma 2.4
.

We have

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}\left(A\right)\\
&=(-1)^{N(N-1)/2}\frac{(1-q)^{MN}}{(N!)^{2}}\oint_{0}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\mathrm{z}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}\mathrm{z}^{n}}\prod_{i_{1}=1}^{N}\int_{|w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}|=R_{1}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\prod_{i_{2}=1}^{N}\int_{|w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}|=R_{2}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\Delta\left(W^{(1)}\right)\Delta\left(W^{(2)}\right)\\
&\quad\tilde{F}_{1}\left(W^{(1)}\right)\tilde{F}_{2}\left(W^{(2)}\right)\sum_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}=1}^{N}(-1)^{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}}\frac{\left(w_{\ell_{1}}^{(1)}+1\right)^{x}\left(w_{\ell_{1}}^{(1)}\right)^{n}}{\left(w_{\ell_{2}}^{(2)}+1\right)^{x+1}\left(w_{\ell_{2}}^{(2)}\right)^{n-1}}\det\left[\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{z}}(w_{i_{1}}^{(1)},w_{i_{2}}^{(2)})+\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{z}}(w_{i_{1}}^{(1)},w_{i_{2}}^{(2)})\right]_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1}\neq\ell_{1}\\
i_{2}\neq\ell_{2}\end{subarray}},\end{split}

(2.11)

where the radii
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
are distinct and both larger than
1
. The functions
\tilde{F}_{1}
and
\tilde{F}_{2}
are defined by

\tilde{F}_{1}(w):=(w+1)^{m-1}w^{-N}(w+1-q)^{-m},\quad\tilde{F}_{2}(w):=(w+1)^{x+y+M-m}(w+1-q)^{-M+m}.

(2.12)

Recall the conventions
\tilde{F}_{\ell}\left(W^{(\ell)}\right)
and
\Delta\left(W^{(\ell)}\right)=\prod_{i>j}\left(w_{i}^{(\ell)}-w_{j}^{(\ell)}\right)=\det\left[\left(w_{i}^{(\ell)}\right)^{j-1}\right]_{i,j=1}^{N}
as introduced in (
1.4
) and (
1.6
). Finally, the functions
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{z}}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{z}}
are given by

\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{z}}(w_{1},w_{2}):=\frac{\mathrm{z}}{w_{1}-w_{2}}\cdot\frac{w_{1}^{n-1}(w_{1}+1)^{x+1}}{w_{2}^{n-1}(w_{2}+1)^{x}}+\frac{1}{-w_{1}+w_{2}}\cdot\frac{w_{1}^{n+1}(w_{1}+1)^{x}}{w_{2}^{n+1}(w_{2}+1)^{x-1}}

(2.13)

and

\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{z}}(w_{1},w_{2}):=\frac{\mathrm{z}}{-w_{1}+w_{2}}\cdot\frac{w_{1}(w_{2}+1)}{w_{2}}+\frac{1}{w_{1}-w_{2}}\cdot\frac{w_{1}^{N}(w_{1}+1)^{x+y+1}}{w_{2}^{N}(w_{2}+1)^{x+y}}.

(2.14)

We postpone the proof of this lemma later in this subsection. Assuming Lemma
2.4
, we are ready to prove Proposition
2.1
. Below we write
A
as
A(x,y)
in (
2.10
) to emphasize the parameters
x
and
y
. As we mentioned before, if we take
q=1-\epsilon
and let
\epsilon\to 0
, the geometric directed last passage percolation becomes an exponential one. More explicitly,
\epsilon\tilde{w}(\mathbf{p})
converges to an exponential random variable in distribution as
\epsilon\to 0
. Moreover, for any fixed interval
I_{1}=[t_{1},t_{1}+\epsilon_{1}]
and
I_{2}=[t_{2},t_{2}+\epsilon_{2}]
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\bigcup_{s_{1}\in I_{1}}\bigcup_{s_{2}\in I_{2}}A\left(\frac{s_{1}}{\epsilon},\frac{s_{2}}{\epsilon}\right)\right)=\mathbb{P}\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}G_{(1,1)}(m,n)+G_{(m+1,n)}(M,N)=G_{(1,1)}(M,N),\\
\epsilon G_{(1,1)}(m,n)\in I_{1},\\
\epsilon G_{(m+1,n)}(M,N)\in I_{2}.\end{array}\right\}

(2.15)

converges as
\epsilon\to 0
to the analogous probability that in the exponential directed last passage percolation, the geodesic
{\mathcal{G}}_{(1,1)}(M,N)
passes through two points
(m,n)
and
(m+1,n)
, and the analogous last passage times satisfy
L_{(1,1)}(m,n)\in I_{1}
and
L_{(m+1,n)}(M,N)\in I_{2}
. In other words, the limit of (
2.15
) is the left hand side of (
1.3
). We remark that although it is possible that there are more than one geodesics in the geometric last passage percolation, after taking the small
\epsilon
limit the chance of getting more geodesics becomes zero.

Now we evaluate the limit of (
2.15
). The left hand side of (
2.15
) is

\sum_{i\epsilon\in I_{1},j\epsilon\in I_{2}}\mathbb{P}(A(i,j))=\int_{I_{1}}\int_{I_{2}}\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\mathbb{P}\left(A\left(\frac{s_{1}}{\epsilon},\frac{s_{2}}{\epsilon}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\epsilon}(s_{2})\mathrm{d}\mu_{\epsilon}(s_{1}),

(2.16)

where
\mathrm{d}\mu_{\epsilon}(s)=\epsilon\delta_{\frac{s}{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{Z}}
. We will prove

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\mathbb{P}(A(s_{1}/\epsilon,s_{2}/\epsilon))=p(s_{1},s_{2};m,n,M,N)

(2.17)

uniformly on
I_{1}\times I_{2}
, with
p(s_{1},s_{2};m,n,M,N)
defined in (
2.1
). Then by using the continuity of the function
p(s_{1},s_{2};m,n,M,N)
we immediately obtain that the limit of (
2.15
) equals to
\int_{I_{1}}\int_{I_{2}}p(s_{1},s_{2};m,n,M,N)\mathrm{d}s_{2}\mathrm{d}s_{1}
. Hence we prove Proposition
2.1
.

Now we prove (
2.17
). We insert
q=1-\epsilon
,
x=s_{1}/\epsilon
, and
y=s_{2}/\epsilon
in (
2.11
). Note that all other parameters are fixed, and
s_{1}\in I_{1},s_{2}\in I_{2}
are nonnegative. We observe that the exponents of
(w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}+1)
for each
1\leq i_{1}\leq N
in the integrand are at least
m-1+\min\{x,1\}\geq m-1\geq 0
, and the exponents of
(w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}+1)
for each
1\leq i_{2}\leq N
are at least
x+y+M-m-\max\{x+1,x+y\}\geq M-m-1\geq 0
. Therefore the integrand is analytic at
-1
for each
w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}
and
w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}
. There are possible poles at
0
and
q-1=-\epsilon
both of which are close to
0
as
\epsilon\to 0
. We hence can deform the contours sufficiently close to the origin. More precisely, we replace
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
by
\epsilon\hat{R}_{1}
and
\epsilon\hat{R}_{2}
where
\hat{R}_{1},\hat{R}_{2}
are distinct constants and both larger than
1
, and change variables
w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}=\epsilon\hat{w}_{i_{1}}^{(1)}
and
w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}=\epsilon\hat{w}_{i_{2}}^{(2)}
. Then

\begin{split}&\Delta\left(W^{(1)}\right)\Delta\left(W^{(2)}\right)=\epsilon^{N(N-1)}\Delta\left(\hat{W}^{(1)}\right)\Delta\left(\hat{W}^{(2)}\right),\\
&\tilde{F}_{1}(w)=\epsilon^{-N-m}(\hat{w}^{-N}(\hat{w}+1)^{-m}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon))=\epsilon^{-N-m}(\tilde{f}_{1}(\hat{w})+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)),\\
&\tilde{F}_{2}(w)=\epsilon^{-M+m}((\hat{w}+1)^{-M+m}e^{(s_{1}+s_{2})\hat{w}}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon))=\epsilon^{-M+m}(\tilde{f}_{2}(\hat{w})+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)),\\
&\left(w+1\right)^{x}w^{n}=\epsilon^{n}(\hat{w}^{n}e^{s_{1}\hat{w}}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)),\quad\left(w+1\right)^{x}w^{n-1}=\epsilon^{n-1}(\hat{w}^{n-1}e^{s_{1}\hat{w}}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)),\\
&\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{z}}(w_{1},w_{2})=\frac{\mathrm{z}}{\epsilon(\hat{w}_{1}-\hat{w}_{2})}\cdot\frac{\hat{w}_{1}^{n-1}e^{s_{1}\hat{w}_{1}}}{\hat{w}_{2}^{n-1}e^{s_{1}\hat{w}_{2}}}+\frac{1}{\epsilon(-\hat{w}_{1}+\hat{w}_{2})}\cdot\frac{\hat{w}_{1}^{n+1}e^{s_{1}\hat{w}_{1}}}{\hat{w}_{2}^{n+1}e^{s_{1}\hat{w}_{2}}}+\mathcal{O}(1)=\epsilon^{-1}(C_{\mathrm{z}}(\hat{w}_{1},\hat{w}_{2})+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)),\\
&\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{z}}(w_{1},w_{2})=\frac{\mathrm{z}}{\epsilon(-\hat{w}_{1}+\hat{w}_{2})}\cdot\frac{\hat{w}_{1}}{\hat{w}_{2}}+\frac{1}{\epsilon(\hat{w}_{1}-\hat{w}_{2})}\cdot\frac{\hat{w}_{1}^{N}e^{(s_{1}+s_{2})\hat{w}_{1}}}{\hat{w}_{2}^{N}e^{(s_{1}+s_{2})\hat{w}_{2}}}+\mathcal{O}(1)=\epsilon^{-1}(D_{\mathrm{z}}(\hat{w}_{1},\hat{w}_{2})+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)).\end{split}

(2.18)

We remind that
\mathrm{d}w=\epsilon\mathrm{d}\hat{w}
. Therefore by inserting these leading terms, we heuristically obtain that

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\mathbb{P}(A(s_{1}/\epsilon,s_{2}/\epsilon))=\text{ the right hand side of~\eqref{eq:finite_time_formula01}}.

(2.19)

On the other hand, since all other parameters are fixed and the contours
|\hat{w}_{i_{1}}^{(1)}|=\hat{R}_{1}
and
|\hat{w}_{i_{2}}^{(2)}|=\hat{R}_{2}
are of finite size, if we insert the above estimates (
2.18
) with the error terms into (
2.11
), all the terms involving
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)
are uniformly bounded by
C\epsilon
for some constant
C
, and there are only finitely many such terms. Therefore the equation (
2.19
) holds uniformly. This proves (
2.17
).

The remaining part of this subsection is to prove Lemma
2.4
.

Denote

G(m)=\left(G_{(1,1)}(m,1),\cdots,G_{(1,1)}(m,N)\right)

(2.20)

the vector of the last passage times from the site
(1,1)
to
(m,i)
,
1\leq i\leq N
.

Our starting point is the following remarkable formula for the distribution of
G(m)
.

Theorem 2.5
.

[
Joh10
, Theorem 2.1]

Suppose
X=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{N})\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{N}
satisfies
x_{1}\leq x_{2}\leq\cdots\leq x_{N}
, then

\mathbb{P}\left(G(m)=X\right)=\det\left[(1-q)^{m}\int_{|w|=R}(w+1)^{x_{j}+m-1}w^{j-i}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}(w+1-q)^{m}}\right]_{i,j=1}^{N},

where
R>1
is any constant.

Note that the contour is of radius
R>1
in the above theorem. This restriction will be kept throughout the proof of Lemma
2.4
and finally lead to the requirements
R_{1}>1
and
R_{2}>1
.

The original theorem of
[
Joh10
, Theorem 2.1]
considered the finite-step transition probabilities from any column to another, and for any
x_{1}\leq\cdots\leq x_{N}
without assuming
x_{1}\geq 0
. For our purpose we only need this simpler version. The assumption that
x_{1}\geq 0
comes from the fact that all random variables
\tilde{w}(\mathbf{p})
are nonnegative. Moreover, we use the contour integral formula in the above determinant for later computations. This formula is equivalent to the original version by combining the equations (9) and (25) in
[
Joh10
]
.

Denote

\tilde{G}(m+1)=\left(G_{(m+1,1)}(M,N),\cdots,G_{(m+1,N)}(M,N)\right).

Note that, by flipping the sites
(i,j)\to(-i,-j)
and shifting the site
(-M,-N)
to
(1,1)
,
\tilde{G}(m+1)
has the same distribution as

\left(G_{(1,1)}(M-m,N),G_{(1,1)}(M-m,N-1),\cdots,G_{(1,1)}(M-m,1)\right).

Therefore, by applying Theorem
2.5
we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\tilde{G}(m+1)=Y\right)=\det\left[(1-q)^{M-m}\int_{|w|=R}(w+1)^{y_{N+1-j}+M-m-1}w^{j-i}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}(w+1-q)^{M-m}}\right]_{i,j=1}^{N}

for any
Y=(y_{1},\cdots,y_{N})\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}
satisfying
y_{1}\geq y_{2}\geq\cdots\geq y_{N}\geq 0
.

Note that
G(M)
and
\tilde{G}(m+1)
are independent since they are defined on the lattices
\mathbb{Z}_{\leq m}\times\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq m+1}\times\mathbb{Z}
respectively. Also note the event
A
is equivalent to the event that
G_{(1,1)}(m,n)=x
,
G_{(m+1,n)(M,N)}=y
, and
G_{(1,1)}(m,i)+G_{(m+1,i)}(M,N)\leq G_{(1,1)}(M,N)=x+y
for all other
i
’s. Thus by combining Theorem
2.5
and the above formula for
\tilde{G}(m+1)
, we obtain

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}(A)&=\sum\mathbb{P}\left(G(m)=X\right)\mathbb{P}\left(\tilde{G}(m+1)=Y\right)\\
&=(1-q)^{MN}\sum\det\left[\int_{|w|=R}(w+1)^{x_{j}+m-1}w^{j-i}(w+1-q)^{-m}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right]_{i,j=1}^{N}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\cdot\det\left[\int_{|w|=R}(w+1)^{y_{N+1-j}+M-m-1}w^{j-i}(w+1-q)^{-M+m}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right]_{i,j=1}^{N},\end{split}

(2.21)

where the summation is running over all possible
X=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{N})\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}
and
Y=(y_{1},\cdots,y_{N})\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}
satisfying

\begin{split}&0\leq x_{1}\leq\cdots\leq x_{N},\quad y_{1}\geq\cdots\geq y_{N}\geq 0,\\
&x_{i}+y_{i}\leq x+y,\quad\text{for all }i=1,\cdots,N,\\
&\text{and }x_{n}=x,\ y_{n}=y.\end{split}

(2.22)

We will consider the above summation in two steps. First, we fix
X
satisfying
0\leq x_{1}\leq\cdots\leq x_{N}\leq x+y
and
x_{n}=x
, and take the sum over
Y
satisfying (
2.22
). Note that only the last determinant in (
2.21
) contains
Y
. We formulate such a summation in the following lemma.

Lemma 2.6
.

Suppose
0\leq x_{1}\leq\cdots\leq x_{N}\leq x+y
,
x_{n}=x
and
y_{n}=y
. Assume that
F(w)
is a function which is analytic on
|w|\geq R
and satisfies
|F(w)|\to 0
uniformly as
|w|\to\infty
. Then

\begin{split}&\sum_{y_{N}=0}^{x+y-x_{N}}\sum_{y_{N-1}=y_{N}}^{x+y-x_{N-1}}\cdots\sum_{y_{n+1}=y_{n+2}}^{x+y-x_{n+1}}\sum_{y_{n-1}=y}^{x+y-x_{n-1}}\cdots\sum_{y_{1}=y_{2}}^{x+y-x_{1}}\det\left[\int_{|w|=R}(w+1)^{y_{j}}w^{-j+i}F(w)\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right]_{i,j=1}^{N}\\
&=\det\left[\int_{|w|=R}(w+1)^{x+y-x_{j}+1_{j\neq n}}w^{-j+i-1_{j\neq n}}F(w)\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right]_{i,j=1}^{N}.\end{split}

Proof of Lemma
2.6
.

Due to the linearity of determinant, we can take the summation of the columns inside the determinant. For each
j=1,\cdots,n-1,n+1,\cdots,N-1
, we have

\begin{split}&\sum_{y_{j}=y_{j+1}}^{x+y-x_{j}}\int_{|w|=R}(w+1)^{y_{j}}w^{-j+i}F(w)\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\\
&=\int_{|w|=R}(w+1)^{x+y-x_{j}+1}w^{-j-1+i}F(w)\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}-\int_{|w|=R}(w+1)^{y_{j+1}}w^{-j-1+i}F(w)\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}},\end{split}

where the second term matches the corresponding entry in the
(j+1)
-th column. Therefore we can remove this term without changing the determinant. For the summation over
y_{N}
, we have a similar identity where the second term becomes

\int_{|w|=R}w^{-N-1+i}F(w)\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}=0

by deforming the contour to infinity. We complete the proof by combining the above summations.
∎

Now we come back to (
2.21
). We reorder the rows and columns in the second determinant by replacing
i\to N+1-i
and
j\to N+1-j
, and apply Lemma
2.6
with
F(w)=(w+1)^{M-m-1}(w+1-q)^{-M+m}
. We have

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}(A)&=(1-q)^{MN}\sum\det\left[\int_{|w|=R}(w+1)^{x_{j}+m-1}w^{j-i}(w+1-q)^{-m}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right]_{i,j=1}^{N}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\cdot\det\left[\int_{|w|=R}(w+1)^{-x_{j}+x+y+M-m-1_{j=N}}w^{-j-1+i+1_{j=n}}(w+1-q)^{-M+m}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right]_{i,j=1}^{N},\end{split}

(2.23)

where the summation is over all
0\leq x_{1}\leq\cdots\leq x_{N}\leq x+y
with
x_{n}=x
.

In the next step, we consider the sum over
X
in (
2.23
). We first apply the following Cauchy-Binet/Andreief’s formula in (
2.23
)

\det\left[\int f_{i}(z)g_{j}(z)\mathrm{d}\mu(z)\right]_{i,j=1}^{N}=\frac{1}{N!}\int\cdots\int\det\left[f_{i}(z_{j})\right]_{i,j=1}^{N}\det\left[g_{i}(z_{j})\right]_{i,j=1}^{N}\mathrm{d}\mu(z_{1})\cdots\mathrm{d}\mu(z_{N}).

We also relabel the variables to avoid confusions. Recall the functions
\tilde{F}_{1}
and
\tilde{F}_{2}
defined in (
2.12
). We have

\begin{split}&\det\left[\int_{|w|=R}(w+1)^{x_{j}+m-1}w^{j-i}(w+1-q)^{-m}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right]_{i,j=1}^{N}\\
&=\frac{1}{N!}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\int_{|w_{i}^{(1)}|=R_{1}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{i}^{(1)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\tilde{F}_{1}\left(W^{(1)}\right)\det\left[\left(w_{i}^{(1)}+1\right)^{x_{j}}\left(w_{i}^{(1)}\right)^{j}\right]_{i,j=1}^{N}\det\left[\left(w_{i}^{(1)}\right)^{N-j}\right]_{i,j=1}^{N}\end{split}

and

\begin{split}&\det\left[\int_{|w|=R}(w+1)^{-x_{j}+x+y+M-m-1_{j=N}}w^{-j-1+i+1_{j=n}}(w+1-q)^{-M+m}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right]_{i,j=1}^{N}\\
&=\frac{1}{N!}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\int_{|w_{i}^{(2)}|=R_{2}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{i}^{(2)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\tilde{F}_{2}\left(W^{(2)}\right)\det\left[\left(w_{i}^{(1)}+1\right)^{-x_{j}-1_{j=N}}\left(w_{i}^{(1)}\right)^{-j+1_{j=N}}\right]_{i,j=1}^{N}\det\left[\left(w_{i}^{(2)}\right)^{j-1}\right]_{i,j=1}^{N}.\end{split}

Thus we write

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}(A)&=(-1)^{N(N-1)/2}\frac{(1-q)^{MN}}{(N!)^{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\int_{|w_{i}^{(1)}|=R_{1}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{i}^{(1)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\int_{|w_{i}^{(2)}|=R_{2}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{i}^{(2)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\tilde{F}_{1}\left(W^{(1)}\right)\tilde{F}_{2}\left(W^{(2)}\right)\\
&\quad\cdot\Delta\left(W^{(1)}\right)\Delta\left(W^{(2)}\right)\cdot S\left(W^{(1)},W^{(2)}\right),\end{split}

(2.24)

where
W^{(1)}=\left(w_{1}^{(1)},\cdots,w_{N}^{(1)}\right)
,
W^{(2)}=\left(w_{1}^{(2)},\cdots,w_{N}^{(2)}\right)
. We also rewrote
\det\left[\left(w_{i}^{(\ell)}\right)^{j-1}\right]_{i,j=1}^{N}=\Delta\left(W^{(\ell)}\right)
for both
\ell=1,2
. Finally, the function

S\left(W^{(1)};W^{(2)}\right):=\sum_{X}\det\left[\left(w_{i}^{(1)}+1\right)^{x_{j}}\left(w_{i}^{(1)}\right)^{j}\right]_{i,j=1}^{N}\det\left[\left(w_{i}^{(2)}+1\right)^{-x_{j}-1_{j=n}}\left(w_{i}^{(2)}\right)^{-j+1_{j=n}}\right]_{i,j=1}^{N},

(2.25)

where the summation is over all
0\leq x_{1}\leq\cdots\leq x_{N}\leq x+y
with fixed
x_{n}=x
.

Note that the summation over
X
only appears in the function
S\left(W^{(1)};W^{(2)}\right)
. Our goal in this step is to evaluate this summation explicitly. We remark that this summation without the extra
1_{j=n}
in the exponents can be simplified to a compact formula if all the coordinates of
W^{(\ell)}
satisfy a so-called Bethe equation, see
[
BL19
, Proposition 5.2]
. However, here we do not have the Bethe roots structure for the coordinates and the resulting formulas are more complicated.

To proceed, we need an identity to expand the determinants in (
2.25
). By using the Laplace expansion of the determinant along the
n
-th column and the Cauchy-Binet formula for the cofactors, we have the identity

\det\left[A_{i,j}\right]_{i,j=1}^{N}=\sum_{\ell}(-1)^{\ell+n}A_{\ell,n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I_{1}\cup I_{2}=\{1,\cdots,N\}\setminus\{\ell\}\\
|I_{1}|=n-1,|I_{2}|=N-n\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\#(I,J)}\det\left[A_{i,j}\right]_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I_{1}\\
1\leq j\leq n-1\end{subarray}}\det\left[A_{i,j}\right]_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I_{2}\\
n+1\leq j\leq N\end{subarray}},

where

\#(I_{1},I_{2}):=\text{ the number of pairs $(i_{1},i_{2})\in I_{1}\times I_{2}$ such that $i_{1}>i_{2}$}.

(2.26)

We apply the above identity in (
2.25
) and change the order of summations. This leads to

\begin{split}&S\left(W^{(1)};W^{(2)}\right)\\
&=\sum_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\geq 1}(-1)^{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}}\frac{\left(w_{\ell_{1}}^{(1)}+1\right)^{x}\left(w_{\ell_{1}}^{(1)}\right)^{n}}{\left(w_{\ell_{2}}^{(2)}+1\right)^{x+1}\left(w_{\ell_{2}}^{(2)}\right)^{n-1}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I_{1}^{(1)}\cup I_{2}^{(1)}=\{1,\cdots,N\}\setminus\{\ell_{1}\}\\
I_{1}^{(2)}\cup I_{2}^{(2)}=\{1,\cdots,N\}\setminus\{\ell_{2}\}\\
|I_{1}^{(1)}|=|I_{1}^{(2)}|=n-1\\
|I_{2}^{(1)}|=|I_{2}^{(2)}|=N-n\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\#(I_{1}^{(1)},I_{2}^{(1)})+\#(I_{1}^{(2)},I_{2}^{(2)})}\\
&\quad\frac{\prod_{i\in I_{2}^{(1)}}\left(w_{i}^{(1)}\right)^{n}}{\prod_{i\in I_{2}^{(2)}}\left(w_{i}^{(2)}\right)^{n}}\hat{S}_{0,x}\left(W^{(1)}_{I_{1}^{(1)}},W^{(2)}_{I_{1}^{(2)}}\right)\hat{S}_{x,x+y}\left(W^{(1)}_{I_{2}^{(1)}},W^{(2)}_{I_{2}^{(2)}}\right),\end{split}

(2.27)

where for simplification we use the notation
W_{I}
for the vector with coordinates
w_{i}
’s satisfying
i\in I
. More explicitly,
W_{I}=(w_{i_{1}},w_{i_{2}},\cdots,w_{i_{k}})
for any
I=(i_{1},\cdots,i_{k})
. The function

\hat{S}_{a,b}\left(W,W^{\prime}\right):=\sum_{a\leq x_{1}\leq\cdots\leq x_{k}\leq b}\det\left[(w_{i}+1)^{x_{j}}w_{i}^{j}\right]_{1\leq i,j\leq k}\det\left[(w^{\prime}_{i}+1)^{-x_{j}}(w^{\prime}_{i})^{-j}\right]_{1\leq i,j\leq k}

for any
a\leq b
and vectors
W
and
W^{\prime}
of the same size. Here
k
is the size of
W
and
W^{\prime}
,
w_{i}
’s and
w^{\prime}_{i}
’s are the coordinates of
W
and
W^{\prime}
respectively.

We have the following identity to simplify
\hat{S}_{a,b}\left(W,W^{\prime}\right)
.

Lemma 2.7
.

[
BL19
]

We have

\hat{S}_{a,b}(W,W^{\prime})=\det\left[\frac{1}{-w_{i}+w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}}\cdot\frac{w_{i}(w_{i}+1)^{a}}{w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}(w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}+1)^{a-1}}+\frac{1}{w_{i}-w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}}\cdot\frac{w_{i}^{k}(w_{i}+1)^{b+1}}{(w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})^{k}(w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}+1)^{b}}\right]_{i,i^{\prime}=1}^{k}.

(2.28)

Proof of Lemma
2.7
.

The main technical part of the summation was included in
[
BL19
]
. Here we simply mention how to arrive (
2.28
) using the known results in
[
BL19
]
.

In
[
BL19
]
, the authors introduced a similar sum
H_{a}(W;W^{\prime})
, where
W
and
W^{\prime}
both are of size
N
. See equation (5.6) in
[
BL19
]
. It reads

H_{a}(W;W^{\prime})=\sum_{a-1=x_{1}\leq\cdots\leq x_{N}\leq a+L-N-1}\det\left[(w^{\prime}_{i}+1)^{x_{j}}(w^{\prime}_{i})^{j}\right]_{1\leq i,j\leq N}\det\left[(w_{i}+1)^{-x_{j}}w_{i}^{-j}\right]_{1\leq i,j\leq N}.

Here we emphasize that
x_{1}=a-1
is fixed in this summation. We also remark that the original definition of
H_{a}(W;W^{\prime})
assumes that the coordinates of
W
and
W^{\prime}
are roots of the so-called Bethe equation, but we will only cite the identities in §5.1-5.3 in
[
BL19
]
where the Bethe roots properties are not used.

The equation (5.44) in
[
BL19
]
can be viewed as a difference of two terms. We apply Lemma 5.9 of
[
BL19
]
for each term and rewrite the equation as

\begin{split}H_{a}(W,W^{\prime})&=\det\left[\frac{1}{w_{i}-w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}}\cdot\frac{w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}(w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}+1)^{a-1}}{w_{i}(w_{i}+1)^{a-2}}+\frac{1}{-w_{i}+w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}}\cdot\frac{(w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})^{N}(w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}+1)^{a+L-N}}{w_{i}^{N}(w_{i}+1)^{a+L-N-1}}\right]_{i,i^{\prime}=1}^{N}\\
&\quad-\det\left[\frac{1}{w_{i}-w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}}\cdot\frac{w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}(w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}+1)^{a}}{w_{i}(w_{i}+1)^{a-1}}+\frac{1}{-w_{i}+w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}}\cdot\frac{(w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})^{N}(w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}+1)^{a+L-N}}{w_{i}^{N}(w_{i}+1)^{a+L-N-1}}\right]_{i,i^{\prime}=1}^{N}.\end{split}

We replace
a+L-N-1
by
b
, and then
a-1
by
a
, and get

\begin{split}&\sum_{a=x_{1}\leq\cdots\leq x_{N}\leq b}\det\left[(w^{\prime}_{i}+1)^{x_{j}}(w^{\prime}_{i})^{j}\right]_{1\leq i,j\leq N}\det\left[(w_{i}+1)^{-x_{j}}w_{i}^{-j}\right]_{1\leq i,j\leq N}\\
&=\det\left[\frac{1}{w_{i}-w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}}\cdot\frac{w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}(w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}+1)^{a}}{w_{i}(w_{i}+1)^{a-1}}+\frac{1}{-w_{i}+w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}}\cdot\frac{(w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})^{N}(w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}+1)^{b+1}}{w_{i}^{N}(w_{i}+1)^{b}}\right]_{i,i^{\prime}=1}^{N}\\
&\quad-\det\left[\frac{1}{w_{i}-w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}}\cdot\frac{w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}(w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}+1)^{a+1}}{w_{i}(w_{i}+1)^{a}}+\frac{1}{-w_{i}+w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}}\cdot\frac{(w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})^{N}(w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}+1)^{b+1}}{w_{i}^{N}(w_{i}+1)^{b}}\right]_{i,i^{\prime}=1}^{N}.\end{split}

So far
x_{1}=a
is fixed. Now by summing the above identity for all
x_{1}
from
a
to
b
, we get

\begin{split}\hat{S}_{a,b}(W^{\prime},W)&=\det\left[\frac{1}{w_{i}-w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}}\cdot\frac{w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}(w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}+1)^{a}}{w_{i}(w_{i}+1)^{a-1}}+\frac{1}{-w_{i}+w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}}\cdot\frac{(w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})^{N}(w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}+1)^{b+1}}{w_{i}^{N}(w_{i}+1)^{b}}\right]_{i,i^{\prime}=1}^{N}\\
&\quad-\det\left[\frac{1}{w_{i}-w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}}\cdot\frac{w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}(w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}+1)^{b+1}}{w_{i}(w_{i}+1)^{b}}+\frac{1}{-w_{i}+w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}}\cdot\frac{(w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})^{N}(w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}+1)^{b+1}}{w_{i}^{N}(w_{i}+1)^{b}}\right]_{i,i^{\prime}=1}^{N}.\end{split}

It is easy to see that the second determinant is zero. Therefore we obtain a formula for
\hat{S}_{a,b}(W^{\prime},W)
with a single determinant. By switching
W
and
W^{\prime}
, and replace the size
N
by
k
, we obtain (
2.28
).
∎

Now we apply Lemma
2.7
to (
2.27
). We also use the identity

\begin{split}&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I_{1}\cup J_{1}=\{1,\cdots,L\}\\
J_{1}\cup J_{2}=\{1,\cdots,L\}\\
|I_{1}|=|I_{2}|=n-1\\
|J_{1}|=|J_{2}|=L-n+1\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\#(I_{1},J_{1})+\#(I_{2},J_{2})}\det\left[A(i_{1},j_{1})\right]_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1}\in I_{1}\\
j_{1}\in J_{1}\end{subarray}}\det\left[B(i_{2},j_{2})\right]_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{2}\in I_{2}\\
j_{2}\in J_{2}\end{subarray}}\\
&=\oint_{0}\det\left[\mathrm{z}A(i,j)+B(i,j)\right]_{i,j=1}^{L}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\mathrm{z}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}\mathrm{z}^{n}},\end{split}

which follows from the multilinearity of the determinant on the rows and the Cauchy-Binet formula. It can also be derived from Lemma 5.9 of
[
BL19
]
. Then we arrive at

\begin{split}&S\left(W^{(1)};W^{(2)}\right)=\sum_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\geq 1}(-1)^{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}}\frac{\left(w_{\ell_{1}}^{(1)}+1\right)^{x}\left(w_{\ell_{1}}^{(1)}\right)^{n}}{\left(w_{\ell_{2}}^{(2)}+1\right)^{x+1}\left(w_{\ell_{2}}^{(2)}\right)^{n-1}}\\
&\oint_{0}\frac{\mathrm{dz}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}\mathrm{z}^{n}}\det\left[\frac{\mathrm{z}}{-w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}+w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}}\cdot\frac{w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}\left(w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}+1\right)}{w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}}+\frac{\mathrm{z}}{w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}-w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}}\cdot\frac{\left(w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}\right)^{n-1}\left(w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}+1\right)^{x+1}}{\left(w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}\right)^{n-1}\left(w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}+1\right)^{x}}\right.\\
&\qquad\quad\left.+\frac{1}{-w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}+w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}}\cdot\frac{\left(w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}\right)^{n+1}\left(w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}+1\right)^{x}}{\left(w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}\right)^{n+1}\left(w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}+1\right)^{x-1}}+\frac{1}{w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}-w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}}\cdot\frac{\left(w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}\right)^{N}\left(w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}+1\right)^{x+y+1}}{\left(w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}\right)^{N}\left(w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}+1\right)^{x+y}}\right]_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1}\neq\ell_{1}\\
i_{2}\neq\ell_{2}\end{subarray}}.\end{split}

(2.29)

By inserting this formula to (
2.24
), we obtain Lemma
2.4
.

2.3
Proof of Proposition
2.2

In this subsection, we prove Proposition
2.2
. There are two main steps in the proof. In the first step we will deform the contours and get rid of term
D_{\mathrm{z}}
in (
2.1
). In the second step we will evaluate the summation over
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
.

2.3.1
Step 1: Deforming the contours

We first realize that

C_{\mathrm{z}}(w_{1},w_{2})+D_{\mathrm{z}}(w_{1},w_{2})=\frac{\mathrm{z}}{w_{1}-w_{2}}\frac{w_{1}^{n-1}e^{s_{1}w_{1}}}{w_{2}^{n-1}e^{s_{1}w_{2}}}+\frac{1}{-w_{1}+w_{2}}\frac{w_{1}^{n+1}e^{s_{1}w_{1}}}{w_{2}^{n+1}e^{s_{1}w_{2}}}+\frac{\mathrm{z}}{-w_{1}+w_{2}}\frac{w_{1}}{w_{2}}+\frac{1}{w_{1}-w_{2}}\frac{w_{1}^{N}e^{(s_{1}+s_{2})w_{1}}}{w_{2}^{N}e^{(s_{1}+s_{2})w_{2}}}

does not have a pole at
w_{1}=w_{2}
. Hence the integrand in (
2.1
) only has poles at
0
and
-1
. Furthermore, we can rewrite the
w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}
integrals as

\int_{|w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}|=R_{1}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}=\frac{-\mathrm{z}}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}+\frac{1}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}

(2.30)

and the
w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}
integrals as

\int_{|w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}|=R_{2}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}=\int_{\Sigma}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}

(2.31)

without changing the value of (
2.1
). After we change the order of summation and integrals, we have

\begin{split}&p(s_{1},s_{2};m,n,M,N)\\
&=\frac{(-1)^{N(N-1)/2}}{(N!)^{2}}\oint_{0}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\mathrm{z}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}\mathrm{z}^{n}}\sum_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}=1}^{N}(-1)^{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}}\left(\frac{-\mathrm{z}}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{\ell_{1}}^{(1)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}+\frac{1}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{\ell_{1}}^{(1)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)\int_{\Sigma}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{\ell_{2}}^{(2)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{\left(w_{\ell_{1}}^{(1)}\right)^{n}e^{s_{1}w_{\ell_{1}}^{(1)}}}{\left(w_{\ell_{2}}^{(2)}\right)^{n-1}e^{s_{1}w_{\ell_{2}}^{(2)}}}\\
&\prod_{i_{1}\neq\ell_{1}}\left(\frac{-\mathrm{z}}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}+\frac{1}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)\prod_{i_{2}\neq\ell_{2}}\int_{\Sigma}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\\
&\quad\cdot\Delta\left(W^{(1)}\right)\Delta\left(W^{(2)}\right)\tilde{f}_{1}\left(W^{(1)}\right)\tilde{f}_{2}\left(W^{(2)}\right)\det\left[C_{\mathrm{z}}\left(w_{i_{1}}^{(1)},w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}\right)+D_{\mathrm{z}}\left(w_{i_{1}}^{(1)},w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}\right)\right]_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1}\neq\ell_{1},\\
i_{2}\neq\ell_{2}\end{subarray}}.\end{split}

(2.32)

Although this rewriting seems simple, it turns out with these changes, we can drop the term
D_{\mathrm{z}}
in the integrand, following from the lemma below.

Lemma 2.8
.

Suppose
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{\prime}
are contours on the complex plane,
\mathrm{d}\mu(w)
and
\mathrm{d}\mu^{\prime}(w^{\prime})
are two measures on these contours respectively. Suppose
C(w,w^{\prime})
and
D(w,w^{\prime})
are two complex-valued functions on
\Sigma\times\Sigma^{\prime}
, and
B(w_{1},\cdots,w_{N};w_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,w^{\prime}_{N})
is a complex-valued function defined on
\Sigma^{N}\times(\Sigma^{\prime})^{N}
. Assume that

\int_{\Sigma^{N}}\int_{(\Sigma)^{N}}\left|B(w_{1},\cdots,w_{N};w_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,w^{\prime}_{N})\right|\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{N}\left(\left|C(w_{i},w^{\prime}_{\sigma(i)})\right|+\left|D(w_{i},w^{\prime}_{\sigma(i)})\right|\right)\prod_{i=1}^{N}\left|\mathrm{d}\mu(w_{i})\right|\prod_{i^{\prime}=1}^{N}\left|\mathrm{d}\mu^{\prime}(w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})\right|<\infty

for each permutation
\sigma\in S_{N}
. We further assume that

\int_{\Sigma}\int_{\Sigma^{\prime}}B(w_{1},\cdots,w_{N};w_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,w^{\prime}_{N})D(w_{i},w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})\mathrm{d}\mu(w_{i})\mathrm{d}\mu^{\prime}(w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})=0

(2.33)

for any
1\leq i,i^{\prime}\leq N
, and any
w_{\ell}\in\Sigma,\ell\neq i
, any
w^{\prime}_{\ell^{\prime}}\in\Sigma^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}\neq i^{\prime}
. Then we have

\begin{split}&\int_{\Sigma^{N}}\int_{(\Sigma)^{N}}B(w_{1},\cdots,w_{N};w_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,w^{\prime}_{N})\cdot\det\left[C(w_{i},w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})+D(w_{i},w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})\right]_{i,i^{\prime}=1}^{N}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\mathrm{d}\mu(w_{i})\prod_{i^{\prime}=1}^{N}\mathrm{d}\mu^{\prime}(w^{\prime}_{i})\\
&=\int_{\Sigma^{N}}\int_{(\Sigma)^{N}}B(w_{1},\cdots,w_{N};w_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,w^{\prime}_{N})\cdot\det\left[C(w_{i},w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})\right]_{i,i^{\prime}=1}^{N}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\mathrm{d}\mu(w_{i})\prod_{i^{\prime}=1}^{N}\mathrm{d}\mu^{\prime}(w^{\prime}_{i}).\end{split}

(2.34)

Proof of Lemma
2.8
.

We expand the determinants on both sides of (
2.34
). It turns out all the terms that appear on the left side but not the right side have some factor
D(w_{i},w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})
in the integrand and hence these terms are zero by the assumption (
2.33
). This proves the identity.
∎

In order to apply Lemma
2.8
in (
2.32
), we need to check the assumptions. All of these assumptions are obvious except for the assumption (
2.33
), which we verify below. We need to show

\begin{split}\left(\frac{-\mathrm{z}}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}+\frac{1}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)\int_{\Sigma}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\Delta\left(W^{(1)}\right)\Delta\left(W^{(2)}\right)\tilde{f}_{1}\left(W^{(1)}\right)\tilde{f}_{2}\left(W^{(2)}\right)D_{\mathrm{z}}\left(w_{i_{1}}^{(1)},w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}\right)\end{split}

equals to zero. If we insert the formulas of
\tilde{f}_{1}
and
\tilde{f}_{2}
(see (
2.2
)) and
D_{\mathrm{z}}
(see (
2.4
)) in the above formula, we only need to prove

\begin{split}&\left(\frac{-\mathrm{z}}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{1}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}+\frac{1}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{1}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)\int_{\Sigma}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{2}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\\
&\quad\tilde{G}_{1}(w_{1})\tilde{G}_{2}(w_{2})w_{1}^{-N}(w_{1}+1)^{-m}(w_{2}+1)^{-M+m}e^{(s_{1}+s_{2})w_{2}}\left(\frac{\mathrm{z}}{-w_{1}+w_{2}}\frac{w_{1}}{w_{2}}+\frac{1}{w_{1}-w_{2}}\frac{w_{1}^{N}e^{(s_{1}+s_{2})w_{1}}}{w_{2}^{N}e^{(s_{1}+s_{2})w_{2}}}\right)=0\end{split}

(2.35)

for some polynomials
\tilde{G}_{1}
and
\tilde{G}_{2}
of degree
N-1
. Using a simple residue computation, we have

\begin{split}&\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{1}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\int_{\Sigma}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{2}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\tilde{G}_{1}(w_{1})\tilde{G}_{2}(w_{2})w_{1}^{-N}(w_{1}+1)^{-m}(w_{2}+1)^{-M+m}e^{(s_{1}+s_{2})w_{2}}\frac{\mathrm{z}}{-w_{1}+w_{2}}\frac{w_{1}}{w_{2}}=0,\\
&\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{1}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\int_{\Sigma}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{2}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\tilde{G}_{1}(w_{1})\tilde{G}_{2}(w_{2})w_{1}^{-N}(w_{1}+1)^{-m}(w_{2}+1)^{-M+m}e^{(s_{1}+s_{2})w_{2}}\frac{\mathrm{z}}{-w_{1}+w_{2}}\frac{w_{1}}{w_{2}}\\
&\quad=\mathrm{z}\int_{\Sigma}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\tilde{G}_{1}(w)\tilde{G}_{2}(w)w^{-N}(w+1)^{-M}e^{(s_{1}+s_{2})w},\\
&\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{1}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\int_{\Sigma}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{2}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\tilde{G}_{1}(w_{1})\tilde{G}_{2}(w_{2})w_{1}^{-N}(w_{1}+1)^{-m}(w_{2}+1)^{-M+m}e^{(s_{1}+s_{2})w_{2}}\frac{1}{w_{1}-w_{2}}\frac{w_{1}^{N}e^{(s_{1}+s_{2})w_{1}}}{w_{2}^{N}e^{(s_{1}+s_{2})w_{2}}}\\
&\quad=\int_{\Sigma}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\tilde{G}_{1}(w)\tilde{G}_{2}(w)w^{-N}(w+1)^{-M}e^{(s_{1}+s_{2})w},\\
&\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{1}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\int_{\Sigma}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{2}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\tilde{G}_{1}(w_{1})\tilde{G}_{2}(w_{2})w_{1}^{-N}(w_{1}+1)^{-m}(w_{2}+1)^{-M+m}e^{(s_{1}+s_{2})w_{2}}\frac{1}{w_{1}-w_{2}}\frac{w_{1}^{N}e^{(s_{1}+s_{2})w_{1}}}{w_{2}^{N}e^{(s_{1}+s_{2})w_{2}}}=0.\end{split}

(
2.35
) follows immediately.

Thus we can apply Lemma
2.8
in (
2.32
). After we remove the term
D_{\mathrm{z}}
, we exchange the integral and summation again and obtain

\begin{split}&p(s_{1},s_{2};m,n,M,N)\\
&=\frac{(-1)^{N(N-1)/2}}{(N!)^{2}}\oint_{0}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\mathrm{z}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}\mathrm{z}^{n}}\prod_{i_{1}=1}^{N}\left(\frac{-\mathrm{z}}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}+\frac{1}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)\prod_{i_{2}=1}^{N}\int_{\Sigma}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\\
&\quad\Delta\left(W^{(1)}\right)\Delta\left(W^{(2)}\right)\tilde{f}_{1}\left(W^{(1)}\right)\tilde{f}_{2}\left(W^{(2)}\right)\sum_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}=1}^{N}(-1)^{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}}\frac{\left(w_{\ell_{1}}^{(1)}\right)^{n}e^{s_{1}w_{\ell_{1}}^{(1)}}}{\left(w_{\ell_{2}}^{(2)}\right)^{n-1}e^{s_{1}w_{\ell_{2}}^{(2)}}}\det\left[C_{\mathrm{z}}\left(w_{i_{1}}^{(1)},w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}\right)\right]_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1}\neq\ell_{1},\\
i_{2}\neq\ell_{2}\end{subarray}}.\end{split}

(2.36)

2.3.2
Step 2: Evaluating the summation

Recall the formula of
C_{\mathrm{z}}
in (
2.3
). We can write

C_{\mathrm{z}}(w_{1},w_{2})=\frac{w_{1}^{n}e^{s_{1}w_{1}}}{w_{2}^{n}e^{s_{1}w_{2}}}\cdot\left(\frac{\mathrm{z}}{w_{1}-w_{2}}\frac{w_{2}}{w_{1}}+\frac{1}{-w_{1}+w_{2}}\frac{w_{1}}{w_{2}}\right).

We insert this formula in (
2.36
). Recall the formulas of
\tilde{f}_{1},\tilde{f}_{2}
in (
2.2
), and
\hat{f}_{1},\hat{f}_{2}
in (
2.6
). We arrive at

\begin{split}&p(s_{1},s_{2};m,n,M,N)\\
&=\frac{(-1)^{N(N-1)/2}}{(N!)^{2}}\oint_{0}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\mathrm{z}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}\mathrm{z}^{n}}\prod_{i_{1}=1}^{N}\left(\frac{-\mathrm{z}}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}+\frac{1}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)\prod_{i_{2}=1}^{N}\int_{\Sigma}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\Delta\left(W^{(1)}\right)\Delta\left(W^{(2)}\right)\\
&\quad\hat{f}_{1}\left(W^{(1)}\right)\hat{f}_{2}\left(W^{(2)}\right)\sum_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}=1}^{N}(-1)^{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}}w_{\ell_{2}}^{(2)}\det\left[\frac{\mathrm{z}}{w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}-w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}}\frac{w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}}{w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}}+\frac{1}{-w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}+w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}}\frac{w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}}{w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}}\right]_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1}\neq\ell_{1},\\
i_{2}\neq\ell_{2}\end{subarray}}.\end{split}

(2.37)

Compare the above formula with (
2.5
). Note the following Cauchy determinant formula

\frac{\Delta\left(W^{(1)}\right)\Delta\left(W^{(2)}\right)}{\Delta\left(W^{(2)};W^{(1)}\right)}=(-1)^{N(N-1)/2}\det\left[\frac{1}{w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}-w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}}\right]_{i_{1},i_{2}=1}^{N}.

We see that (
2.5
) follows from (
2.37
) and Lemma
2.9
below. This completes the proof of Proposition
2.2
.

The remaining part of this subsection is the next lemma and its proof.

Lemma 2.9
.

Suppose
X=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{N})
and
Y=(y_{1},\cdots,y_{N})
are two vectors in
\mathbb{C}^{N}
satisfying
x_{i}\neq y_{j}
for all
1\leq i,j\leq N
. Suppose
\mathrm{z}
is an arbitrary complex number. Then we have the following identity

\begin{split}&\sum_{a,b=1}^{N}(-1)^{a+b}y_{b}\det\left[\frac{\mathrm{z}}{x_{i}-y_{j}}\frac{y_{j}}{x_{i}}+\frac{1}{-x_{i}+y_{j}}\frac{x_{i}}{y_{j}}\right]_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\neq a\\
j\neq b\end{subarray}}\\
&=(1-\mathrm{z})^{N-2}\left(\hat{H}(X;Y)+\mathrm{z}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{y_{i}}{x_{i}}\hat{H}(Y;X)\right)\det\left[\frac{1}{y_{j}-x_{i}}\right]_{i,j=1}^{N},\end{split}

(2.38)

where
\hat{H}
is defined in (
2.7
).

Proof of Lemma
2.9
.

We first use the identity

\frac{\mathrm{z}}{x-y}\frac{y}{x}+\frac{1}{-x+y}\frac{x}{y}=(1-\mathrm{z})\frac{x}{y}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{-x+y}-\frac{\mathrm{z}}{1-\mathrm{z}}\frac{1}{x}-\frac{\mathrm{z}}{1-\mathrm{z}}\frac{y}{x^{2}}\right)

and write the left hand side of (
2.38
) as

(1-\mathrm{z})^{N-1}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{x_{i}}{y_{i}}\sum_{a,b=1}^{N}(-1)^{a+b}\frac{y_{b}^{2}}{x_{a}}\det\left[\frac{1}{-x_{i}+y_{j}}-\frac{\mathrm{z}}{1-\mathrm{z}}\frac{1}{x_{i}}-\frac{\mathrm{z}}{1-\mathrm{z}}\frac{y_{j}}{x_{i}^{2}}\right]_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\neq a\\
j\neq b\end{subarray}}.

(2.39)

Thus the equation (
2.38
) is equivalent to, by setting
u=-\mathrm{z}/(1-\mathrm{z})
,

\begin{split}&\sum_{a,b=1}^{N}(-1)^{a+b}\frac{y_{b}^{2}}{x_{a}}\det\left[\frac{1}{-x_{i}+y_{j}}+u\frac{1}{x_{i}}+u\frac{y_{j}}{x_{i}^{2}}\right]_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\neq a\\
j\neq b\end{subarray}}\\
