&=-\left((u-1)\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{y_{i}}{x_{i}}\hat{H}(X;Y)+u\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{y_{i}^{2}}{x_{i}^{2}}\hat{H}(Y;X)\right)\cdot\det\left[\frac{1}{y_{j}-x_{i}}\right]_{i,j=1}^{N}.\end{split}

(2.40)

The proof of (
2.40
) is tedious while the strategy is quite straightforward. Below we will show the proof but omit some details which are direct to check. We remark that the strategy was applied to a much simpler identity in
[
BL19
, Lemma 5.5]
, but this identity (
2.40
) is much more complicated.

Before we prove (
2.40
), we need to prepare some easier identities. We denote

X(w):=\prod_{i=1}^{N}(w-x_{i}),\quad Y(w):=\prod_{i=1}^{N}(w-y_{i}),

and introduce

C_{p,q}=\sum_{a,b=1}^{n}x_{a}^{p}y_{b}^{q}\frac{Y(x_{a})X(y_{b})}{(x_{a}-y_{b})X^{\prime}(x_{a})Y^{\prime}(y_{b})},

where
p,q
are both integers. It is not hard to verify, by using the Cauchy determinant formula, that

C_{p,q}=\sum_{a,b=1}^{N}(-1)^{a+b}x_{a}^{p}y_{b}^{q}\det\left[\frac{1}{-x_{i}+y_{j}}\right]_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\neq a\\
j\neq b\end{subarray}}/\det\left[\frac{1}{-x_{i}+y_{j}}\right]_{i,j=1}^{N}.

(2.41)

One can evaluate
C_{p,q}
by converting the sum as a residue computation of an integral on the complex plane. As an illustration, we show how to obtain
C_{-1,2}
, then we will list all the
C_{p,q}
values we will use later without providing proofs, see Table
1
.

We consider a double integral

\int_{|y|=R_{2}}\int_{|x|=R_{1}}\frac{y^{2}}{x}\frac{Y(x)X(y)}{(x-y)X(x)Y(y)}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}x}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}y}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}},

where
R_{1}>R_{2}>\max_{i}\{|x_{i}|+|y_{i}|\}
. Note that we can deform the
x
-contour to infinity and the integral becomes zero. Hence the above double integral is zero. On the other hand, we can change the order of integrals and evaluate the
y
-integral first. It gives a sum over all roots of
Y(y)
:

0=\int_{|x|=R_{1}}\sum_{b=1}^{N}\frac{y_{b}^{2}}{x}\frac{Y(x)X(y_{b})}{(x-y_{b})X(x)Y^{\prime}(y_{b})}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}x}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}.

Then we exchange the summation and integral, and evaluate the
x
-integral by computing the residues within the contour. Note that
x=y_{b}
is not a pole. We get

0=C_{-1,2}-\frac{Y(0)}{X(0)}\sum_{b=1}^{N}y_{b}\frac{X(y_{b})}{Y^{\prime}(y_{b})}.

(2.42)

We need to continue to evaluate the summation in (
2.42
). We have, by a residue computation,

\begin{split}\sum_{b=1}^{N}y_{b}\frac{X(y_{b})}{Y^{\prime}(y_{b})}&=\int_{|y|=R_{2}}y\frac{X(y)}{Y(y)}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}y}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\\
&=\int_{|y|=R_{2}}y\left(1+\frac{1}{y}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(y_{i}-x_{i})+\frac{1}{y^{2}}\hat{H}(X;Y)+O(y^{-3})\right)\frac{{\mathrm{d}}y}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}=\hat{H}(X;Y),\end{split}

where we evaluated the integral by expanding the integrand for large
y
. Here the function
\hat{H}
is defined in (
2.7
).

By inserting the above formula to (
2.42
), we obtain

C_{-1,2}=\prod_{i}\frac{y_{i}}{x_{i}}\hat{H}(X;Y).

Using similar calculations, we can find all
C_{p,q}
for small
p,q
values. In Table
1
we list some
C_{p,q}
identities we will use in the proof of (
2.40
). We remark that the proof of these identities are analogous to that of
C_{-1,2}
without adding extra difficulties.

Expression

Value

Expression

Value

C_{0,-1}

\displaystyle 1-\prod_{i}\frac{x_{i}}{y_{i}}

C_{-1,2}

\displaystyle\prod_{i}\frac{y_{i}}{x_{i}}\hat{H}(X;Y)

C_{-1,0}

\displaystyle-1+\prod_{i}\frac{y_{i}}{x_{i}}

C_{-1,1}-C_{0,0}

\displaystyle\left(1-\prod_{i}\frac{y_{i}}{x_{i}}\right)\sum_{i}(x_{i}-y_{i})

C_{1,0}

\displaystyle-\hat{H}(Y;X)

C_{0,2}-C_{1,1}

\displaystyle-\sum_{i}(x_{i}-y_{i})\hat{H}(X;Y)

C_{0,1}

\displaystyle\hat{H}(X;Y)

C_{-2,1}

\displaystyle-1+\prod_{i}\frac{y_{i}}{x_{i}}\left(1-\sum_{i}\left(\frac{1}{x_{i}}-\frac{1}{y_{i}}\right)\sum_{i}\left(x_{i}-y_{i}\right)\right)

Table 1:
Values of some
C_{p,q}
expressions.

We need to evaluate

\det\left[\frac{1}{-x_{i}+y_{j}}+u\frac{1}{x_{i}}\right]_{i,j=1}^{N}=\det\left[\frac{1}{-x_{i}+y_{j}}\right]_{i,j=1}^{N}+u\sum_{a,b=1}^{N}(-1)^{a+b}\frac{1}{x_{a}}\det\left[\frac{1}{-x_{i}+y_{j}}\right]_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\neq a\\
j\neq b\end{subarray}}.

By applying (
2.41
) and finding the
C_{-1,0}
value in Table
1
, we get

\begin{split}\det\left[\frac{1}{-x_{i}+y_{j}}+u\frac{1}{x_{i}}\right]_{i,j=1}^{N}&=\det\left[\frac{1}{-x_{i}+y_{j}}\right]_{i,j=1}^{N}\left(1+uC_{-1,0}\right)\\
&=\det\left[\frac{1}{-x_{i}+y_{j}}\right]_{i,j=1}^{N}\left(1+u\left(-1+\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{y_{i}}{x_{i}}\right)\right).\end{split}

(2.43)

Then we evaluate

\det\left[\frac{1}{-x_{i}+y_{j}}+u\frac{1}{x_{i}}+u\frac{y_{j}}{x_{i}^{2}}\right]_{i,j=1}^{N}=\det\left[\frac{1}{-x_{i}+y_{j}}+u\frac{1}{x_{i}}\right]_{i,j=1}^{N}+u\sum_{a,b=1}^{N}(-1)^{a+b}\frac{y_{b}}{x_{a}^{2}}\det\left[\frac{1}{-x_{i}+y_{j}}+u\frac{1}{x_{i}}\right]_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\neq a\\
j\neq b\end{subarray}}.

We insert (
2.43
) in the above equation and obtain

\begin{split}&\det\left[\frac{1}{-x_{i}+y_{j}}+u\frac{1}{x_{i}}+u\frac{y_{j}}{x_{i}^{2}}\right]_{i,j=1}^{N}\\
&=\det\left[\frac{1}{-x_{i}+y_{j}}\right]_{i,j=1}^{N}\left(1-u+u\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{y_{i}}{x_{i}}\right)+u(1-u)\sum_{a,b=1}^{N}(-1)^{a+b}\frac{y_{b}}{x_{a}^{2}}\det\left[\frac{1}{-x_{i}+y_{j}}\right]_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\neq a\\
j\neq b\end{subarray}}\\
&\quad+u^{2}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{y_{i}}{x_{i}}\sum_{a,b=1}^{N}(-1)^{a+b}\frac{1}{x_{a}}\det\left[\frac{1}{-x_{i}+y_{j}}\right]_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\neq a\\
j\neq b\end{subarray}}\\
&=\det\left[\frac{1}{-x_{i}+y_{j}}\right]_{i,j=1}^{N}\left(1-u+u\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{y_{i}}{x_{i}}+u(1-u)C_{-2,1}+u^{2}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{y_{i}}{x_{i}}C_{-1,0}\right).\end{split}

(2.44)

By inserting the values of
C_{-2,1}
and
C_{-1,0}
and simplifying the expression, we obtain

\begin{split}&\det\left[\frac{1}{-x_{i}+y_{j}}+u\frac{1}{x_{i}}+u\frac{y_{j}}{x_{i}^{2}}\right]_{i,j=1}^{N}=\det\left[\frac{1}{-x_{i}+y_{j}}\right]_{i,j=1}^{N}\\
&\quad\cdot\left[1-2u\left(1-\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{y_{i}}{x_{i}}\right)-(u-u^{2})\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{y_{i}}{x_{i}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\frac{1}{x_{i}}-\frac{1}{y_{i}}\right)\sum_{i=1}^{N}(x_{i}-y_{i})+u^{2}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{y_{i}}{x_{i}}-1\right)^{2}\right].\end{split}

(2.45)

Finally we are ready to prove (
2.40
). Inserting (
2.45
), we can write

\begin{split}&\sum_{a,b=1}^{N}(-1)^{a+b}\frac{y_{b}^{2}}{x_{a}}\det\left[\frac{1}{-x_{i}+y_{j}}+u\frac{1}{x_{i}}+u\frac{y_{j}}{x_{i}^{2}}\right]_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\neq a\\
j\neq b\end{subarray}}\\
&=\sum_{a,b=1}^{N}(-1)^{a+b}\frac{y_{b}^{2}}{x_{a}}\det\left[\frac{1}{-x_{i}+y_{j}}\right]_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\neq a\\
j\neq b\end{subarray}}\cdot\left[1-2u\left(1-\frac{x_{a}}{y_{b}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{y_{i}}{x_{i}}\right)+u^{2}\left(\frac{x_{a}}{y_{b}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{y_{i}}{x_{i}}-1\right)^{2}\right.\\
&\quad\left.-(u-u^{2})\frac{x_{a}}{y_{b}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{y_{i}}{x_{i}}\left(-\frac{1}{x_{a}}+\frac{1}{y_{b}}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\frac{1}{x_{i}}-\frac{1}{y_{i}}\right)\right)\left(-x_{a}+y_{b}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}(x_{i}-y_{i})\right)\right].\end{split}

We apply (
2.41
) and rewrite the above equation as

\begin{split}&\sum_{a,b=1}^{N}(-1)^{a+b}\frac{y_{b}^{2}}{x_{a}}\det\left[\frac{1}{-x_{i}+y_{j}}+u\frac{1}{x_{i}}+u\frac{y_{j}}{x_{i}^{2}}\right]_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\neq a\\
j\neq b\end{subarray}}/\det\left[\frac{1}{-x_{i}+y_{j}}+u\frac{1}{x_{i}}+u\frac{y_{j}}{x_{i}^{2}}\right]_{i,j=1}^{N}\\
&=\left((1-u)^{2}+u(1-u)\prod_{i}\frac{y_{i}}{x_{i}}\right)C_{-1,2}+u\prod_{i}\frac{y_{i}}{x_{i}}\left(1-u+u\cdot\prod_{i}\frac{y_{i}}{x_{i}}\right)C_{1,0}\\
&\quad-u(1-u)\prod_{i}\frac{y_{i}}{x_{i}}\left(\sum_{i}\frac{1}{x_{i}}-\sum_{i}\frac{1}{y_{i}}\right)\left(\sum_{i}x_{i}-\sum_{i}y_{i}\right)C_{0,1}\\
&\quad-u(1-u)\prod_{i}\frac{y_{i}}{x_{i}}\left(\sum_{i}x_{i}-\sum_{i}y_{i}\right)C_{0,0}+u(1-u)\prod_{i}\frac{y_{i}}{x_{i}}\left(\sum_{i}x_{i}-\sum_{i}y_{i}\right)C_{-1,1}\\
&\quad-u(1-u)\prod_{i}\frac{y_{i}}{x_{i}}\left(\sum_{i}\frac{1}{x_{i}}-\sum_{i}\frac{1}{y_{i}}\right)C_{0,2}+u(1-u)\prod_{i}\frac{y_{i}}{x_{i}}\left(\sum_{i}\frac{1}{x_{i}}-\sum_{i}\frac{1}{y_{i}}\right)C_{1,1}.\end{split}

(2.46)

By checking the values of Table
1
, and noting that
\left(\sum_{i}(x_{i}-y_{i})\right)^{2}=\hat{H}(X;Y)+\hat{H}(Y;X)
, we can simplify the above expression. It turns out, after a careful but straightforward calculation, the
u^{2}
term vanishes, and the remaining terms match the right hand side of (
2.40
). We hence complete the proof.
∎

2.4
Proof of Proposition
2.3

In this subsection, we prove Proposition
2.3
. Note that the equation (
2.5
) involves a Cauchy determinant factor

\frac{\Delta\left(W^{(1)}\right)\Delta\left(W^{(2)}\right)}{\Delta\left(W^{(2)};W^{(1)}\right)}=(-1)^{N(N-1)/2}\det\left[\frac{1}{w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}-w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}}\right]_{i_{1},i_{2}=1}^{N},

which is of size N, while the formula (
1.7
) is analogous to a Fredholm determinant expansion. So Proposition
2.3
can be interpreted as an identity between a Cauchy determinant of large size and a Fredholm-determinant-like expansion. Our strategy contains three steps. First, we rewrite the formula (
2.5
) to a summation on discrete spaces with summand having similar Cauchy determinant structures. This rewriting involves a generalized version of an identity in
[
Liu19
]
. In the second step, we reformulate the summation to a Fredholm-determinant-like expansion on the same discrete space. We remark that similar calculation were considered in
[
BL18
,
BL19
]
but our summand is more involved. Finally, we verify that the expansion indeed matches (
1.7
) using the identity obtained in the first step.

Below we will first introduce a generalized version of an identity in
[
Liu19
]
, the Proposition 4.3 of
[
Liu19
]
. Then we prove Proposition
2.3
using the above strategy.

2.4.1
A Cauchy-type summation identity

We introduce a few concepts before we state the results. We will mainly follow
[
Liu19
, Section 4]
but add a small generalization.

Suppose
W=(w_{1},\cdots,w_{n})\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
and
W^{\prime}=(w^{\prime}_{1},\cdots,w^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}})\in\mathbb{C}^{n^{\prime}}
are two vectors without overlapping coordinates, i.e., they satisfy
w_{i}\neq w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}
for all
i,i^{\prime}
. We define

\mathcal{C}(W;W^{\prime})=\frac{\Delta(W)\Delta(W^{\prime})}{\Delta(W;W^{\prime})}

(2.47)

and call it a
Cauchy-type factor
. Note that when
n=n^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{C}(W;W^{\prime})
equals to a Cauchy determinant
\det\left[1/(w_{i}-w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})\right]_{i,i^{\prime}=1}^{n}
multiplied by a sign factor
(-1)^{n(n-1)/2}
. We remark that we allow empty product and view it as
1
in the above definition. For example, when
n^{\prime}=0
, we have
\mathcal{C}(W;W^{\prime})=\Delta(W)
.

Similar as in (
2.27
), we use the convention that
W_{I}=(w_{i_{1}},\cdots,w_{i_{k}})
for
any index set
I=\{i_{1},\cdots,i_{k}\}
where
1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\leq n
. In other words,
W_{I}
is the vector formed by the coordinates with indices in
I
.

We denote

\mathbb{D}(r):=\{z:|z|<r\},\text{ and }\mathbb{D}_{0}(r)=\{z:0<|z|<r\}.

And we omit
r
when
r=1
, i.e.,
\mathbb{D}=\mathbb{D}(1)
and
\mathbb{D}_{0}=\mathbb{D}_{0}(1)
.

Suppose
q(w)
is a function which is analytic in a certain bounded region
\mathcal{D}
. Denote

\mathcal{R}_{z}=\left\{w\in\mathcal{D}:q(w)=z\right\}.

(2.48)

Assume that
\mathcal{R}_{0}\neq\emptyset
. In other words, there is at least one root of
q(w)
within
\mathcal{D}
. We also assume that
\mathrm{r_{max}}
is a positive constant such that
\cup_{z\in\mathbb{D}(\mathrm{r_{max}})}\mathcal{R}_{z}=\{w\in\mathcal{D}:|q(w)|\leq\mathrm{r_{max}}\}
lies within a compact subset of
\mathcal{D}
, and
\{w\in\mathcal{D}:|q(w)|=r\}
for all
0<|r|<\mathrm{r_{max}}
consists of
|\mathcal{R}_{0}|
non-intersecting simply connected contours around the points in
\mathcal{R}_{0}
. It is easy to see that with these assumptions
q^{\prime}(w)\neq 0
for all
w\in\{w\in\mathcal{D}:|q(w)|<\mathrm{r_{max}}\}
. We remark that in the original setting of
[
Liu19
]
, they assumed
\mathcal{R}_{0}=\{0\}
or
\{-1\}
. Here we drop this assumption.

We will consider a Cauchy-type summation, which involves an expression

\mathcal{H}\left(W^{(1)},\cdots,W^{(\ell)};z_{0},\cdots,z_{\ell-1}\right):=\left[\prod_{k=1}^{\ell-1}\mathcal{C}\left(W_{I^{(k)}}^{(k)};W_{J^{(k+1)}}^{(k+1)}\right)\right]\cdot\mathcal{A}\left(W^{(1)},\cdots,W^{(\ell)};z_{0},\cdots,z_{\ell-1}\right),

(2.49)

where
W^{(k)}=\left(w^{(k)}_{1},\cdots,w_{n_{k}}^{(k)}\right)\in\mathbb{C}^{n_{k}}
,
1\leq k\leq\ell
, such that
W^{(k)}
and
W^{(k+1)}
do not have overlapping coordinates for
1\leq k\leq\ell-1
.
I^{(k)}
and
J^{(k)}
are arbitrary subsets of
\{1,\cdots,n_{k}\}
for
1\leq k\leq\ell-1
and
2\leq k\leq\ell
respectively. The function
\mathcal{A}
is analytic for all
w_{j_{k}}^{(k)}\in\mathcal{D}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{0}
,
1\leq j_{k}\leq n_{k},1\leq k\leq\ell
, and for all
(z_{0},\cdots,z_{\ell-1})\in\mathbb{D}(\mathrm{r_{max}})\times\mathbb{D}^{\ell-1}
. Hence
\mathcal{H}
is also analytic on
(\mathcal{D}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{0})^{n_{1}+\cdots+n_{\ell}}\times\mathbb{D}(\mathrm{r_{max}})\times\mathbb{D}^{\ell-1}
, except for having possible poles at
w_{i_{k}}^{(k)}=w_{i_{k+1}}^{(k+1)}
for some
i_{k}\in I^{(k)}
and
i_{k+1}\in I^{(k+1)}
, which comes from the Cauchy-type factors. We remark that the function
\mathcal{H}
also depends on the index sets
I^{(k)},J^{(k+1)}
,
1\leq k\leq\ell-1
.

Now we introduce the summation. We consider

\mathcal{G}(z_{0},\cdots,z_{\ell-1})=\sum_{W^{(1)}\in\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z}_{1}}^{n_{1}}}\cdots\sum_{W^{(\ell)}\in\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z}_{\ell}}^{n_{\ell}}}\left[\prod_{k=1}^{\ell}J(W^{(k)})\right]\mathcal{H}\left(W^{(1)},\cdots,W^{(\ell)};z_{0},\cdots,z_{\ell-1}\right)

(2.50)

for
(z_{0},\cdots,z_{\ell})\in\mathbb{D}_{0}(\mathrm{r_{max}})\times\mathbb{D}_{0}^{\ell-1}
,
where the function

J(w):=\frac{q(w)}{q^{\prime}(w)}.

(2.51)

Recall our convention
J(W^{(k)})=\prod_{a=1}^{n_{k}}J(w_{a}^{(k)})
. The variables
\hat{z}_{k}
’s are defined by

\hat{z}_{k}=z_{0}z_{1}\cdots z_{k-1},\quad k=1,\cdots,\ell.

(2.52)

Note the identity

\sum_{w\in\mathcal{R}_{z}}f(w)H(w)=\left(\int_{|q(w)|=C_{1}}-\int_{|q(w)|=C_{2}}\right)\frac{f(w)q(w)}{q(w)-z}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}},

(2.53)

where
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are two positive constants satisfying
C_{2}<|z|<C_{1}
such that the function
f(w)
is analytic in
\{w:C_{2}<|q(w)|<C_{1}\}
. The right hand side is analytic as a function of
z
within
C_{2}<|z|<|C_{1}|
. This identity implies that
\sum_{w\in\mathcal{R}_{z}}f(w)H(w)
is also analytic as a function of
z
within
C_{2}<|z|<|C_{1}|
. Using this fact we obtain that
\mathcal{G}(z_{0},\cdots,z_{\ell-1})

is analytic as a function of
\hat{z}_{1},\cdots,\hat{z}_{\ell}
within
0<|\hat{z}_{\ell}|<\cdots<|\hat{z}_{1}|<\mathrm{r_{max}}
, and hence is analytic as a function of
z_{0},\cdots,z_{\ell-1}
in
\mathbb{D}_{0}(\mathrm{r_{max}})\times\mathbb{D}_{0}^{\ell-1}
. We remark that there are no poles from the Cauchy-type factor due to the order of
|\hat{z}_{k}|
.

Our goal is to analytically extend the function
\mathcal{G}
to
\mathbb{D}(\mathrm{r_{max}})\times\mathbb{D}^{\ell-1}
under certain assumption. Below we introduce two more concepts related the assumption, then we state the identity.

We call a sequence of variables
w_{i_{k}}^{(k)},w_{i_{k+1}}^{(k+1)},\cdots,w_{i_{k^{\prime}}}^{(k^{\prime})}

a Cauchy chain
with respect to the vectors
W^{(1)},\cdots,W^{(\ell)}
and index sets
I^{(1)},J^{(2)},I^{(2)},J^{(3)},\cdots,I^{(\ell-1)},J^{(\ell)}
, if

\left(w_{i_{k}}^{(k)}-w_{i_{k+1}}^{(k+1)}\right)\cdot\left(w_{i_{k+1}}^{(k+1)}-w_{i_{k+2}}^{(k+2)}\right)\cdot\cdots\cdot\left(w_{i_{k^{\prime}-1}}^{(k^{\prime}-1)}-w_{i_{k^{\prime}}}^{(k^{\prime})}\right)

appears as a factor of the denominator in
\prod_{k=1}^{\ell-1}\mathcal{C}\left(W_{I^{(k)}}^{(k)};W_{J^{(k+1)}}^{(k+1)}\right)
. We allow any single variable
w_{i_{k}}^{(k)}
to be a Cauchy chain as long as it is a coordinate of
W^{(k)}
.

We say
q(w)

dominates

\mathcal{H}\left(W^{(1)},\cdots,W^{(\ell)};z_{0},\cdots,z_{\ell-1}\right)
if and only if the following function of
w

\left.q(w)\cdot\mathcal{A}\left(W^{(1)},\cdots,W^{(\ell)};z_{0},\cdots,z_{\ell-1}\right)\right|_{w_{i_{k}}^{(k)}=w_{i_{k+1}}^{(k+1)}=\cdots=w_{i_{k^{\prime}}}^{(k^{\prime})}=w}

(2.54)

is analytic at any
w\in\mathcal{R}_{0}
when all other variables are fixed, here
w_{i_{k}}^{(k)},w_{i_{k+1}}^{(k+1)},\cdots,w_{i_{k^{\prime}}}^{(k^{\prime})}
is an arbitrary Cauchy chain with respect to
W^{(1)},\cdots,W^{(\ell)}
and
I^{(1)},J^{(2)},I^{(2)},J^{(3)},\cdots,I^{(\ell-1)},J^{(\ell)}
. We remark that in
[
Liu19
]
, this concept was only defined when
\mathcal{R}_{0}
contains one single point. Here we dropped this assumption.

Proposition 2.10
.

If
q(w)
dominates
\mathcal{H}\left(W^{(1)},\cdots,W^{(\ell)};z_{0},\cdots,z_{\ell-1}\right)
, then the function
\mathcal{G}(z_{0},\cdots,z_{\ell-1})
can be analytically extended to
\mathbb{D}(\mathrm{r_{max}})\times\mathbb{D}^{\ell-1}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{G}(z_{0}=0,z_{1}\cdots,z_{\ell-1})
is independent of
q(w)
, and it equals to

\prod_{k=2}^{\ell}\prod_{i_{k}=1}^{n_{k}}\left[\frac{1}{1-z_{k-1}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}^{(k)}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{i_{k}}^{(k)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}-\frac{z_{k-1}}{1-z_{k-1}}\int_{\Sigma^{(k)}_{\mathrm{out}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{i_{k}}^{(k)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right]\prod_{i_{1}=1}^{n_{1}}\int_{\Sigma^{(1)}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\mathcal{H}\left(W^{(1)},\cdots,W^{(\ell)};0,z_{1},\cdots,z_{\ell-1}\right),

where
\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}^{(\ell)},\cdots,\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}^{(2)},\Sigma^{(1)},\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}^{(2)},\cdots,\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}^{(\ell)}
are
2\ell-1
nested contours in
\mathcal{D}
each of which encloses all the points in
\mathcal{R}_{0}
.

Proof of Proposition
2.10
.

When
\mathcal{R}_{0}=\{0\}
, this is exactly the same as
[
Liu19
, Proposition 4.3]
. On the other hand, their proof does not use the fact
\mathcal{R}_{0}=\{0\}
, see
[
Liu19
, Section 6]
. Hence Proposition
2.10
follows from the same argument.
∎

One can similarly consider a two-region version of the above result. Assume that
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{L}}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{R}}
are two disjoint bounded regions on the complex plane. Let
q(w)
be a function analytic in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{L}}\cup\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{R}}
and define

\mathcal{R}_{z,\mathrm{L}}=\{u\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{L}}:q(u)=z\},\quad\text{and }\mathcal{R}_{z,\mathrm{R}}=\{v\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{R}}:q(v)=z\}.

Assume that both
\mathcal{R}_{0,\mathrm{L}}
and
\mathcal{R}_{0,\mathrm{R}}
are nonempty. The analog of (
2.49
) is

\begin{split}&\mathcal{H}\left(U^{(1)},\cdots,U^{(\ell)};V^{(1)},\cdots,V^{(\ell)};z_{0},\cdots,z_{\ell-1}\right)\\
&:=\left[\prod_{k=1}^{\ell-1}\mathcal{C}\left(U_{I_{\mathrm{L}}^{(k)}}^{(k)};U_{J_{\mathrm{L}}^{(k+1)}}^{(k+1)}\right)\mathcal{C}\left(V_{I_{\mathrm{R}}^{(k)}}^{(k)};V_{J_{\mathrm{R}}^{(k+1)}}^{(k+1)}\right)\right]\cdot\mathcal{A}\left(U^{(1)},\cdots,U^{(\ell)};V^{(1)},\cdots,V^{(\ell)};z_{0},\cdots,z_{\ell-1}\right),\end{split}

where
\mathcal{A}
is analytic in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{L}}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{0,\mathrm{L}}
for each coordinate of
U^{(k)}
, and in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{R}}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{0,\mathrm{R}}
for each coordinate of
V^{(k)}
,
1\leq k\leq\ell
, and analytic for all
(z_{0},\cdots,z_{\ell})\in\mathbb{D}(\mathrm{r_{max}})\times\mathbb{D}^{\ell-1}
. The analog of (
2.50
) is

\mathcal{G}(z_{0},\cdots,z_{\ell-1})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}U^{(1)}\in\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z}_{1},\mathrm{L}}^{n_{1,\mathrm{L}}}\\
V^{(1)}\in\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z}_{1},\mathrm{R}}^{n_{1,\mathrm{R}}}\end{subarray}}\cdots\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}U^{(\ell)}\in\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z}_{\ell},\mathrm{L}}^{n_{\ell,\mathrm{L}}}\\
V^{(\ell)}\in\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z}_{\ell},\mathrm{R}}^{n_{\ell,\mathrm{R}}}\end{subarray}}\left[\prod_{k=1}^{\ell}J(U^{(k)})J(V^{(k)})\right]\mathcal{H}\left(U^{(1)},\cdots,U^{(\ell)};V^{(1)},\cdots,V^{(\ell)};z_{0},\cdots,z_{\ell-1}\right)

for
(z_{0},\cdots,z_{\ell})\in\mathbb{D}_{0}(\mathrm{r_{max}})\times\mathbb{D}_{0}^{\ell-1}
. We can similarly define Cauchy chains in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{L}}
and in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{R}}
. We say
q(w)
dominates
\mathcal{H}\left(U^{(1)},\cdots,U^{(\ell)};V^{(1)},\cdots,V^{(\ell)};z_{0},\cdots,z_{\ell-1}\right)
if

\left.q(u)\cdot\mathcal{A}\left(U^{(1)},\cdots,U^{(\ell)};V^{(1)},\cdots,V^{(\ell)};z_{0},\cdots,z_{\ell-1}\right)\right|_{u_{i_{k}}^{(k)}=u_{i_{k+1}}^{(k+1)}=\cdots=u_{i_{k^{\prime}}}^{(k^{\prime})}=u}

is analytic at any
u\in\mathcal{R}_{0,\mathrm{L}}
for any Cauchy chain in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{L}}
, and

\left.q(v)\cdot\mathcal{A}\left(U^{(1)},\cdots,U^{(\ell)};V^{(1)},\cdots,V^{(\ell)};z_{0},\cdots,z_{\ell-1}\right)\right|_{v_{i_{k}}^{(k)}=v_{i_{k+1}}^{(k+1)}=\cdots=v_{i_{k^{\prime}}}^{(k^{\prime})}=v}

is analytic at any
v\in\mathcal{R}_{0,\mathrm{R}}
for any Cauchy chain in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{R}}
. The analog of Proposition
2.10
is as follows.

Proposition 2.11
.

If
q(w)
dominates
\mathcal{H}\left(U^{(1)},\cdots,U^{(\ell)};V^{(1)},\cdots,V^{(\ell)};z_{0},\cdots,z_{\ell-1}\right)
, then the function
\mathcal{G}(z_{0},\cdots,z_{\ell-1})
can be analytically extended to
\mathbb{D}(\mathrm{r_{max}})\times\mathbb{D}^{\ell-1}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{G}(z_{0}=0,z_{1}\cdots,z_{\ell-1})
is independent of
q(w)
, and it equals to

\begin{split}&\prod_{k=2}^{\ell}\prod_{i_{k}=1}^{n_{k,\mathrm{L}}}\left[\frac{1}{1-z_{k-1}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{in},\mathrm{L}}^{(k)}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}u_{i_{k}}^{(k)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}-\frac{z_{k-1}}{1-z_{k-1}}\int_{\Sigma^{(k)}_{\mathrm{out},\mathrm{L}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}u_{i_{k}}^{(k)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right]\prod_{i_{1}=1}^{n_{1,\mathrm{L}}}\int_{\Sigma^{(1)}_{\mathrm{L}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}u_{i_{1}}^{(1)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\\
&\prod_{k=2}^{\ell}\prod_{i_{k}=1}^{n_{k,\mathrm{R}}}\left[\frac{1}{1-z_{k-1}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{in},\mathrm{R}}^{(k)}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}v_{i_{k}}^{(k)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}-\frac{z_{k-1}}{1-z_{k-1}}\int_{\Sigma^{(k)}_{\mathrm{out},\mathrm{R}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}v_{i_{k}}^{(k)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right]\prod_{i_{1}=1}^{n_{1,\mathrm{R}}}\int_{\Sigma^{(1)}_{\mathrm{R}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}v_{i_{1}}^{(1)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\\
&\mathcal{H}\left(U^{(1)},\cdots,U^{(\ell)};V^{(1)},\cdots,V^{(\ell)};0,z_{1}\cdots,z_{\ell-1}\right),\end{split}

where
\Sigma_{\mathrm{out},\mathrm{L}}^{(\ell)},\cdots,\Sigma_{\mathrm{out},\mathrm{L}}^{(2)},\Sigma^{(1)}_{\mathrm{L}},\Sigma_{\mathrm{in},\mathrm{L}}^{(2)},\cdots,\Sigma_{\mathrm{in},\mathrm{L}}^{(\ell)}
are
2\ell-1
nested contours in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{L}}
each of which encloses all the points in
\mathcal{R}_{0,\mathrm{L}}
, and
\Sigma_{\mathrm{out},\mathrm{R}}^{(\ell)},\cdots,\Sigma_{\mathrm{out},\mathrm{R}}^{(2)},\Sigma^{(1)}_{\mathrm{R}},\Sigma_{\mathrm{in},\mathrm{R}}^{(2)},\cdots,\Sigma_{\mathrm{in},\mathrm{R}}^{(\ell)}
are
2\ell-1
nested contours in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{R}}
each of which encloses all the points in
\mathcal{R}_{0,\mathrm{R}}
.

Proof of Proposition
2.11
.

The case when
\mathcal{R}_{0,\mathrm{L}}=\{-1\}
and
\mathcal{R}_{0,\mathrm{R}}=\{0\}
was the same as
[
Liu19
, Proposition 4.4]
. The proof for the more general case is also the same as the proof of
[
Liu19
, Proposition 4.4]
, except that we apply Proposition
2.10
in this paper instead of
[
Liu19
, Proposition 4.2]
.
∎

2.4.2
Rewriting (
2.5
)

Now we want to apply Proposition
2.10
to equation (
2.5
) and rewrite the formula.

We first choose
q(w)=w^{N}(w+1)^{L-N}
, where
L
is any fixed integer satisfying
L\geq M+N
. Recall the formula (
2.5
). Let
\mathcal{H}\left(W^{(2)},W^{(1)};z_{1},z_{0}=\mathrm{z}\right)
be a slight modification of the integrand in (
2.5
). More precisely, let

\mathcal{H}\left(W^{(2)},W^{(1)};z_{1},z_{0}\right)=\mathcal{C}\left(W^{(2)};W^{(1)}\right)\mathcal{A}\left(W^{(2)},W^{(1)};z_{1},z_{0}\right),

(2.55)

where

\begin{split}&\mathcal{A}\left(W^{(2)},W^{(1)};z_{1},z_{0}\right)\\
&:=\Delta\left(W^{(1)}\right)\Delta\left(W^{(2)}\right)\hat{f}_{1}\left(W^{(1)}\right)\hat{f}_{2}\left(W^{(2)}\right)\left[\hat{H}\left(W^{(1)};W^{(2)}\right)+z_{0}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{w_{i}^{(2)}}{w_{i}^{(1)}}\hat{H}\left(W^{(1)};W^{(2)}\right)\right].\end{split}

(2.56)

Note that when
z_{0}=\mathrm{z}
,
\mathcal{H}\left(W^{(2)},W^{(1)};z_{1},z_{0}\right)
is exactly the integrand of (
2.5
). Assume
\mathcal{D}
is a bounded region enclosing both
0
and
-1
. It is obvious that the function
\mathcal{A}
is well defined and analytic for all
w_{i}^{(1)},w_{i}^{(2)}\in\mathcal{D}\setminus\{\-1,0\}
,
1\leq i\leq N
, and for all
(z_{1},z_{0})\in\mathbb{D}(\mathrm{r_{max}})\times\mathbb{D}
, here we choose

\mathrm{r_{max}}=N^{N}(L-N)^{L-N}/L^{L}.

(2.57)

We remark that we have a different ordering of indices compared to the original formulas (
2.49
) and (
2.50
). This is because we want to make the indices of
\hat{f}_{1}
and
\hat{f}_{2}
more natural by using
1
to label the parameters appearing in the first part of the last passage time and using
2
to label the parameters appearing in the second part of the last passage time. On the other hand, we also want to make our indices in Propositions
2.10
and
2.11
consistent with
[
Liu19
]
so the readers can compare the results easily. These different orderings might be confusing but they only appear in this technical proof. We will keep reminding readers if needed.

The sum we are considering is

\mathcal{G}(z_{1},z_{0})=\sum_{W^{(2)}\in\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z}_{2}}^{N}}\sum_{W^{(1)}\in\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z}_{1}}^{N}}J\left(W^{(1)}\right)J\left(W^{(2)}\right)\mathcal{H}\left(W^{(2)},W^{(1)};z_{1},z_{0}\right),

(2.58)

where
\hat{z}_{2}=z_{1}
and
\hat{z}_{1}=z_{1}z_{0}
. We assume that
z_{1}\in\mathbb{D}_{0}(\mathrm{r_{max}})
and
z_{0}\in\mathbb{D}_{0}
hence
0<|\hat{z}_{2}|<|\hat{z}_{1}|<\mathrm{r_{max}}
.

We need to verify that Proposition
2.10
is applicable for this function (
2.58
). All other assumptions are trivial, except for the one that
q(w)
dominates
\mathcal{H}\left(W^{(2)},W^{(1)};z_{1},z_{0}\right)
. We verify it below.

There are only three types of Cauchy chains. The chains of single element
w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}
or
w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}
, and the chain of two elements
w_{i_{2}}^{(2)},w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}
. For the first type of chains, we need to verify
q(w_{i_{1}}^{(1)})\mathcal{A}\left(W^{(2)},W^{(1)};z_{1},z_{0}\right)
is analytic at
0
and
-1
. This follows from the fact that
\hat{f}_{1}(w)q(w)w^{-1}=(w+1)^{L-N-m}w^{n-1}e^{s_{1}w}
is an entire function. Similarly we can verify it for the second type of Cauchy chains. Finally, for the chain of two elements
w_{i_{2}}^{(2)},w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}
, we need to show
\left.q(w)\mathcal{A}\left(W^{(2)},W^{(1)};z_{1},z_{0}\right)\right|_{w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}=w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}=w}
is analytic at
-1
and
0
. It follows from the fact that
\hat{f}_{1}(w)\hat{f}_{2}(w)q(w)=(w+1)^{L-N-M}e^{(s_{1}+s_{2})w}
is entire.

So we can apply Proposition
2.10
, and obtain

\begin{split}&\mathcal{G}(0,z_{0}=\mathrm{z})=\prod_{i_{1}=1}^{N}\left(\frac{-\mathrm{z}}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}+\frac{1}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)\prod_{i_{2}=1}^{N}\int_{\Sigma}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\\
&\quad\hat{f}_{1}\left(W^{(1)}\right)\hat{f}_{2}\left(W^{(2)}\right)\frac{\left(\Delta\left(W^{(1)}\right)\right)^{2}\left(\Delta\left(W^{(2)}\right)\right)^{2}}{\Delta\left(W^{(2)};W^{(1)}\right)}\cdot\left(\hat{H}\left(W^{(1)};W^{(2)}\right)+\mathrm{z}\frac{\prod_{i_{2}=1}^{N}w_{i_{2}}^{(2)}}{\prod_{i_{1}=1}^{N}w_{i_{1}}^{(1)}}\hat{H}\left(W^{(2)};W^{(1)}\right)\right).\end{split}

Hence we have an alternate expression for (
2.5
)

p(s_{1},s_{2};m,n,M,N)=\frac{1}{(N!)^{2}}\oint_{0}\mathcal{G}(0,z_{0}=\mathrm{z})\frac{(1-\mathrm{z})^{N-2}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{z}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}\mathrm{z}^{n}}.

(2.59)

2.4.3
Reformulation to a Fredholm-determinant-like expansion

In this subsubsection, we want to evaluate the summation (
2.58
) in a different way. Recall
q(w)=w^{N}(w+1)^{L-N}
and
\mathcal{R}_{z}
are the roots of
q(w)=z
. This equation is called the Bethe equation, and its roots are called the Bethe roots. It is known
[
BL18
]
that when
|z|<\mathrm{r_{max}}=N^{N}(L-N)^{L-N}/L^{L}
, the set
\mathcal{R}_{z}
can be split into two different subsets
\mathcal{R}_{z,\mathrm{L}}
and
\mathcal{R}_{z,\mathrm{R}}
satisfying
|\mathcal{R}_{z,\mathrm{L}}|=L-N
and
|\mathcal{R}_{z,\mathrm{R}}|=N
. Intuitively, each root in
\mathcal{R}_{z,\mathrm{L}}
(
\mathcal{R}_{z,\mathrm{R}}
, respectively) can be viewed as an continuous function of
z
starting from
-1
(
0
, respectively) when
z=0
. We denote

\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{L}}=\cup_{|z|<\mathrm{r_{max}}}\mathcal{R}_{z,\mathrm{L}},\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{R}}=\cup_{|z|<\mathrm{r_{max}}}\mathcal{R}_{z,\mathrm{R}},

(2.60)

and

q_{z,\mathrm{L}}(w)=\prod_{u\in\mathcal{R}_{z,\mathrm{L}}}(w-u),\quad\text{and}\quad q_{z,\mathrm{R}}(w)=\prod_{v\in\mathcal{R}_{z,\mathrm{L}}}(w-v)

(2.61)

which will be used in later computations. Note that
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{L}}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{R}}
are two disjoint bounded regions, and
q_{z,\mathrm{L}}(w)q_{z,\mathrm{R}}(w)=q(w)-z
.

We will rewrite the summation (
2.58
) by treating
w_{i_{k}}^{(k)}\in\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z}_{k},\mathrm{L}}
and
w_{i_{k}}^{(k)}\in\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z}_{k},\mathrm{R}}
separately. We first observe that, by checking the formulas (
2.55
) and (
2.56
), the summand is invariant when we permute the coordinates of
W^{(k)}
,
k=1,2
. We also observe that the summand is zero if any two coordinates of
W^{(k)}
are equal due to the Cauchy-type factor. Therefore we only need to consider the summation for
W^{(k)}
with different coordinates.

Assume that
n_{k}
coordinates in
W^{(k)}
are chosen from
\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z}_{k},\mathrm{L}}
. Then the other
N-n_{k}
coordinates are chosen from
\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z}_{k},\mathrm{R}}
. Note that
\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z}_{k},\mathrm{R}}
has exactly
N
elements, hence there are
n_{k}
elements which do not appear in
W^{(k)}
. We denote
V^{(k)}=(v_{1}^{(k)},\cdots,v_{n_{k}}^{(k)})
the vector formed by these elements with any given order. We also denote
U^{(k)}=(u_{1}^{(k)},\cdots,u_{n_{k}}^{(k)})
the vector formed by the coordinates of
W^{(k)}
in
\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z}_{k},\mathrm{L}}
. Note the invariance property we observed above. We write

\sum_{W^{(2)}\in\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z}_{2}}^{N}}\sum_{W^{(1)}\in\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z}_{1}}^{N}}=(N!)^{2}\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}=0}^{N}\frac{1}{(n_{1}!)^{2}(n_{2}!)^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}U^{(2)}\in\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z}_{2},\mathrm{L}}^{n_{2}}\\
V^{(2)}\in\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z}_{2},\mathrm{R}}^{n_{2}}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}U^{(1)}\in\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z}_{1},\mathrm{L}}^{n_{1}}\\
V^{(1)}\in\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z}_{1},\mathrm{R}}^{n_{1}}\end{subarray}},

(2.62)

where the factors
N!
,
n_{k}!
come from the number of ways to permute the coordinates of
W^{(k)}
,
U^{(k)}
(and
V^{(k)}
) respectively. Now we need to rewrite the summand in terms of
U^{(k)}
and
V^{(k)}
,
k=1,2
. Such a rewriting was mostly done in
[
BL18
,
BL19
]
except for one extra factor. We will write down the formulas without proofs except for the one involving the extra factor.

Recall the notation conventions (
1.4
), (
1.5
) and (
1.6
). We write, by simply inserting the coordinates,

\hat{f}_{k}\left(W^{(k)}\right)=\frac{\hat{f}_{k}\left(U^{(k)}\right)}{\hat{f}_{k}\left(V^{(k)}\right)}\cdot\hat{f}_{k}\left(\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z}_{k},\mathrm{R}}\right),\quad J\left(W^{(k)}\right)=\frac{J\left(U^{(k)}\right)}{J\left(V^{(k)}\right)}\cdot J\left(\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z}_{k},\mathrm{R}}\right),\quad k=1,2.

We also have (see equation (4.43) of
[
BL19
]
)

\Delta\left(W^{(k)}\right)^{2}=(-1)^{N(N-1)/2}\frac{\Delta\left(U^{(k)}\right)^{2}\Delta\left(V^{(k)}\right)^{2}}{\Delta\left(U^{(k)};V^{(k)}\right)^{2}}\frac{q_{\hat{z}_{k},\mathrm{R}}^{2}\left(U^{(k)}\right)}{\left(q^{\prime}_{\hat{z}_{k},\mathrm{R}}\left(V^{(k)}\right)\right)^{2}}\cdot q^{\prime}_{\hat{z}_{k},\mathrm{R}}\left(\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z}_{k},\mathrm{R}}\right)

and (see equation (4.44) of
[
BL19
]
)

\Delta\left(W^{(2)};W^{(1)}\right)=\Delta\left(\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z}_{2},\mathrm{R}};\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z}_{1},\mathrm{R}}\right)\frac{\Delta\left(U^{(2)};U^{(1)}\right)\Delta\left(V^{(2)};V^{(1)}\right)}{\Delta\left(U^{(2)};V^{(1)}\right)\Delta\left(V^{(2)};U^{(1)}\right)}\cdot\frac{q_{\hat{z}_{1},\mathrm{R}}\left(U^{(2)}\right)q_{\hat{z}_{2},\mathrm{R}}\left(U^{(1)}\right)}{q_{\hat{z}_{1},\mathrm{R}}\left(V^{(2)}\right)q_{\hat{z}_{2},\mathrm{R}}\left(V^{(1)}\right)}.

We need to further rewrite the above expressions so that we can apply Proposition
2.11
later.
Denote

\mathfrak{h}(w;z):=\begin{cases}q_{z,\mathrm{R}}(w)/w^{N},&w\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{L}},\\
q_{z,\mathrm{L}}(w)/(w+1)^{L-N},&w\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{R}}.\end{cases}

It is easy to check that
\mathfrak{h}(w;z)
is analytic and nonzero for
w\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{L}}\cup\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{R}}
and for
z\in\mathbb{D}(\mathrm{r_{max}})
. Especially we have
\mathfrak{h}(w;0)=1
for all
w\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{L}}\cup\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{R}}
. See equation (5.5) in
[
Liu19
]
and the discussions below.

One can write (see equation (4.51) of
[
BL19
]
)

q^{\prime}_{z,\mathrm{R}}(v)=\frac{v^{N}}{J(v)\mathfrak{h}(v;z)},\quad v\in\mathcal{R}_{z,\mathrm{R}}.

and (see (4.49) of
[
BL19
]
)

q_{z,\mathrm{R}}(v^{\prime})=\frac{z^{\prime}-z}{q_{z,\mathrm{L}}(v^{\prime})}=\frac{z^{\prime}-z}{(v^{\prime}+1)^{L-N}\mathfrak{h}(v^{\prime};z)},\quad v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{z^{\prime},\mathrm{R}}.

Note that
\Delta\left(\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z}_{2},\mathrm{R}};\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z}_{1},\mathrm{R}}\right)=q_{\hat{z}_{1},\mathrm{R}}\left(\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z}_{2},\mathrm{R}}\right)
. After inserting all these formulas and simplifying the expression, we end up with

\begin{split}&J\left(W^{(1)}\right)J\left(W^{(2)}\right)\hat{f}_{1}\left(W^{(1)}\right)\hat{f}_{2}\left(W^{(2)}\right)\frac{\left(\Delta\left(W^{(1)}\right)\right)^{2}\left(\Delta\left(W^{(2)}\right)\right)^{2}}{\Delta\left(W^{(2)};W^{(1)}\right)}=\mathcal{K}(\hat{z}_{2},\hat{z}_{1})\cdot\frac{\hat{z}_{1}^{n}\hat{z}_{2}^{N-n}}{(\hat{z}_{2}-\hat{z}_{1})^{N}}\\
&\quad\cdot\left[\prod_{k=1}^{2}\frac{(\Delta(U^{(k)}))^{2}(\Delta(V^{(k)}))^{2}}{(\Delta(U^{(k)};V^{(k)}))^{2}}\cdot\frac{f_{k}(U^{(k)};s_{k})}{f_{k}(V^{(k)};s_{k})}\cdot\left(\mathfrak{h}(U^{(k)};\hat{z}_{k})\right)^{2}\cdot\left(\mathfrak{h}(V^{(k)};\hat{z}_{k})\right)^{2}\cdot J(U^{(k)})J(V^{(k)})\right]\\
&\quad\cdot\left[\frac{\Delta(U^{(2)};V^{(1)})\Delta(V^{(2)};U^{(1)})}{\Delta(U^{(2)};U^{(1)})\Delta(V^{(2)};V^{(1)})}\cdot\frac{(1-\hat{z}_{2}/\hat{z}_{1})^{n_{1}}(1-\hat{z}_{1}/\hat{z}_{2})^{n_{2}}}{\mathfrak{h}(U^{(2)};\hat{z}_{1})\mathfrak{h}(V^{(2)};\hat{z}_{1})\mathfrak{h}(U^{(1)};\hat{z}_{2})\mathfrak{h}(V^{(1)};\hat{z}_{2})}\right],\end{split}

(2.63)

where the functions
f_{k}(w;s_{k})=\hat{f}_{k}(w)w^{N}
,
k=1,2
, are defined in (
1.9
), and

\begin{split}\mathcal{K}(\hat{z}_{2},\hat{z}_{1})&=\frac{1}{\hat{z}_{1}^{n}}\prod_{v\in\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z}_{1},\mathrm{R}}}\frac{(v+1)^{-m}v^{n}e^{s_{1}v}}{\mathfrak{h}(v;\hat{z}_{1})}\cdot\frac{1}{\hat{z}_{2}^{N-n}}\prod_{v\in\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z}_{2},\mathrm{R}}}\frac{(v+1)^{-M+m+L-N}v^{N-n}e^{s_{2}v}}{\mathfrak{h}(v;\hat{z}_{2})/\mathfrak{h}(v;\hat{z}_{1})}\\
&=(-1)^{N(L-1)}\prod_{v\in\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z}_{1},\mathrm{R}}}\frac{(v+1)^{-m}e^{s_{1}v}}{\mathfrak{h}(v;\hat{z}_{1})}\prod_{u\in\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z}_{1},\mathrm{L}}}\frac{1}{u^{n}}\prod_{v\in\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z}_{2},\mathrm{R}}}\frac{(v+1)^{-M+m+L-N}e^{s_{2}v}}{\mathfrak{h}(v;\hat{z}_{2})/\mathfrak{h}(v;\hat{z}_{1})}\prod_{u\in\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z}_{2},\mathrm{L}}}\frac{1}{u^{N-n}}.\end{split}

(2.64)

We observe that
\mathcal{K}(\hat{z}_{2},\hat{z}_{1})
is analytic for both
\hat{z}_{2}\in\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{r_{max}}}
and
\hat{z}_{1}\in\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{r_{max}}}
since
\mathfrak{h}
is analytic and nonzero, and
z^{-1}\prod_{v\in\mathcal{R}_{z,\mathrm{R}}}v=(-1)^{L-1}\prod_{u\in\mathcal{R}_{z,\mathrm{L}}}u^{-1}
is analytic for
z\in\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{r_{max}}}
. Moreover, we have
\mathcal{K}(0,0)=1
.

As we mentioned before, there is an extra factor in the summand of (
2.62
) which comes from (
2.56
),

\hat{H}\left(W^{(1)};W^{(2)}\right)+z_{0}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{w_{i}^{(2)}}{w_{i}^{(1)}}\hat{H}\left(W^{(2)};W^{(1)}\right).

Here
\hat{H}
is defined in (
2.7
). Recall that
\{w_{i}^{(k)}:1\leq i\leq N\}=\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z}_{k},\mathrm{R}}\cup\{u_{i}^{(k)}:1\leq i\leq n_{k}\}\setminus\{v_{i}^{(k)}:1\leq i\leq n_{k}\}
. We write, for each
k,k^{\prime}\in\{1,2\}
,

\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(w_{i}^{(k)}-w_{i}^{(k^{\prime})}\right)=\sum_{i_{k}=1}^{n_{k}}\left(u_{i_{k}}^{(k)}-v_{i_{k}}^{(k)}\right)-\sum_{i^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}=1}^{n_{k^{\prime}}}\left(u_{i^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}}^{(k^{\prime})}-v_{i^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}}^{(k^{\prime})}\right)+\mathcal{S}_{1}(\hat{z}_{k})-\mathcal{S}_{1}(\hat{z}_{k^{\prime}})

and

\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left((w_{i}^{(k)})^{2}-(w_{i}^{(k^{\prime})})^{2}\right)=\sum_{i_{k}=1}^{n_{k}}\left((u_{i_{k}}^{(k)})^{2}-(v_{i_{k}}^{(k)})^{2}\right)-\sum_{i^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}=1}^{n_{k^{\prime}}}\left((u_{i^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}}^{(k^{\prime})})^{2}-(v_{i^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}}^{(k^{\prime})})^{2}\right)+\mathcal{S}_{2}(\hat{z}_{k})-\mathcal{S}_{2}(\hat{z}_{k^{\prime}}),

where

\mathcal{S}_{k}(\hat{z}):=\sum_{v\in\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z},\mathrm{R}}}v^{k},\quad k=1,2

(2.65)

is analytic in
\hat{z}\in\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{r_{max}}}
. Moreover, it is easy to see that
\mathcal{S}_{k}(0)=0
for both
k=1,2
. We also write

z_{0}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{w_{i}^{(2)}}{w_{i}^{(1)}}=\frac{\hat{z}_{1}}{\hat{z}_{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{w_{i}^{(2)}}{w_{i}^{(1)}}=\prod_{i_{1}=1}^{n_{1}}\frac{v_{i_{1}}^{(1)}}{u_{i_{1}}^{(1)}}\prod_{i_{2}=1}^{n_{2}}\frac{u_{i_{2}}^{(2)}}{v_{i_{2}}^{(2)}}\cdot\frac{\pi(\hat{z}_{2})}{\pi(\hat{z}_{1})},

(2.66)

where

\pi(\hat{z}):=\frac{1}{\hat{z}}\prod_{v\in\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z},\mathrm{R}}}v=\frac{(-1)^{L-1}}{\prod_{u\in\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z},\mathrm{L}}}u}

is analytic in
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{r_{max}}}
. Moreover, it is easy to see that
\pi(0)=(-1)^{N-1}
.

Combing the above calculations we have

\hat{H}\left(W^{(1)};W^{(2)}\right)+z_{0}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{w_{i}^{(2)}}{w_{i}^{(1)}}\hat{H}\left(W^{(1)};W^{(2)}\right)=\tilde{H}(U^{(1)},U^{(2)};V^{(1)},V^{(2)};\hat{z}_{1},\hat{z}_{2})

(2.67)

for some function
\tilde{H}
which is analytic for all
u_{i_{1}}^{(1)},u_{i_{2}}^{(2)}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{L}}
,
v_{i_{1}}^{(1)},v_{i_{2}}^{(2)}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{R}}\setminus\{0\}
,
1\leq i_{1}\leq n_{1},1\leq i_{2}\leq n_{2}
, and for
\hat{z}_{1},\hat{z}_{2}\in\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{r_{max}}}
. Moreover, we have

\tilde{H}(U^{(1)},U^{(2)};V^{(1)},V^{(2)};0,0)=H(U^{(1)},U^{(2)};V^{(1)},V^{(2)}),

(2.68)

where
H
is defined in (
1.10
).

Now we combine (
2.63
) and (
2.67
), and note (
2.62
). Note
\hat{z}_{1}/\hat{z}_{2}=z_{0}
. We have

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{(N!)^{2}}\mathcal{G}(z_{1},z_{0})\frac{(1-z_{0})^{N}}{z_{0}^{n}}\\
&=\mathcal{K}(\hat{z}_{2},\hat{z}_{1})\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}=0}^{N}\frac{(1-z_{0}^{-1})^{n_{1}}(1-z_{0})^{n_{2}}}{(n_{1}!)^{2}(n_{2}!)^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}U^{(2)}\in\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z}_{2},\mathrm{L}}^{n_{2}}\\
V^{(2)}\in\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z}_{2},\mathrm{R}}^{n_{2}}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}U^{(1)}\in\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z}_{1},\mathrm{L}}^{n_{1}}\\
V^{(1)}\in\mathcal{R}_{\hat{z}_{1},\mathrm{R}}^{n_{1}}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{C}(U^{(2)};U^{(1)})\mathcal{C}(V^{(2)};V^{(1)})\\
&\quad\cdot\left[\prod_{k=1}^{2}\frac{(\Delta(U^{(k)}))(\Delta(V^{(k)}))}{(\Delta(U^{(k)};V^{(k)}))^{2}}\cdot\frac{f_{k}(U^{(k)};s_{k})}{f_{k}(V^{(k)};s_{k})}\cdot\left(\mathfrak{h}(U^{(k)};\hat{z}_{k})\right)^{2}\cdot\left(\mathfrak{h}(V^{(k)};\hat{z}_{k})\right)^{2}\cdot J(U^{(k)})J(V^{(k)})\right]\\
&\quad\cdot\left[{\Delta(U^{(2)};V^{(1)})\Delta(V^{(2)};U^{(1)})}\cdot\frac{\tilde{H}(U^{(1)},U^{(2)};V^{(1)},V^{(2)};\hat{z}_{1},\hat{z}_{2})}{\mathfrak{h}(U^{(2)};\hat{z}_{1})\mathfrak{h}(V^{(2)};\hat{z}_{1})\mathfrak{h}(U^{(1)};\hat{z}_{2})\mathfrak{h}(V^{(1)};\hat{z}_{2})}\right].\end{split}

(2.69)

2.4.4
Completing the proof

Now we are ready to complete the proof. We will take
z_{1}\to 0
on both sides of (
2.69
). Recall that we have already proven that
\mathcal{G}(z_{1},z_{0})
is analytic for
(z_{1},z_{0})\in\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{r_{max}}}\times\mathbb{D}
and
\mathcal{G}(0,z_{0})
is well defined. For the right hand side, recall
\hat{z}_{2}=z_{1}
and
\hat{z}_{1}=z_{1}z_{0}
. When
z_{1}\to 0
, both
\hat{z}_{1}
and
\hat{z}_{2}
go to
0
. We also recall
\mathcal{K}(0,0)=1
.

For the summand over
U^{(2)},V^{(2)},U^{(1)},V^{(1)}
, it is a Cauchy type summation as we discussed in Proposition
2.11
. Our previous discussions on the functions
\mathfrak{h}
and
\tilde{H}
implies that this summand satisfies the analyticity assumption. The proof that
q(w)
dominates the corresponding factor in this summand is also similar to the previous case discussed in Section
2.4.2
. The only minor difference is that we have a factor
\prod_{i_{1}}v_{i_{1}^{(1)}}\prod_{i_{2}}(v_{i_{2}}^{(2)})^{-1}
in
\tilde{H}
but the proof does not change even with this factor. Hence we know that this summation is also analytic for
(z_{1},z_{0})\in\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{r_{max}}}\times\mathbb{D}
. Moreover, by inserting
z_{1}=0
in the equation, we obtain

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{(N!)^{2}}\mathcal{G}(0,z_{0})\frac{(1-z_{0})^{N}}{z_{0}^{n}}=\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}=0}^{N}\frac{(1-z_{0}^{-1})^{n_{1}}(1-z_{0})^{n_{2}}}{(n_{1}!)^{2}(n_{2}!)^{2}}\\
&\quad\prod_{i_{1}=1}^{n_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{1-{z_{0}}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{in}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}u^{(1)}_{i_{1}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}-\frac{{z_{0}}}{1-{z_{0}}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{out}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}u^{(1)}_{i_{1}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)\left(\frac{1}{1-{z_{0}}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{in}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}v^{(1)}_{i_{1}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}-\frac{{z_{0}}}{1-{z_{0}}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{out}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}v^{(1)}_{i_{1}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)\\
&\quad\cdot\prod_{i_{2}=1}^{n_{2}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{L}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}u^{(2)}_{i_{2}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{L}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}v^{(2)}_{i_{2}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\mathcal{C}(U^{(2)};U^{(1)})\mathcal{C}(V^{(2)};V^{(1)})\prod_{k=1}^{2}\frac{(\Delta(U^{(k)}))(\Delta(V^{(k)}))}{(\Delta(U^{(k)};V^{(k)}))^{2}}\cdot\frac{f_{k}(U^{(k)};s_{k})}{f_{k}(V^{(k)};s_{k})}\\
&\quad\cdot{\Delta(U^{(2)};V^{(1)})\Delta(V^{(2)};U^{(1)})}\cdot{H(U^{(1)},U^{(2)};V^{(1)},V^{(2)}).}\end{split}

(2.70)

Inserting it in (
2.59
) and replacing
n_{0},n_{1}
by
k_{1},k_{2}
, we obtain

\begin{split}&p(s_{1},s_{2};m,n,M,N)=\oint_{0}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\mathrm{z}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}\mathrm{(1-z)^{2}}}\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}\geq 0}\frac{1}{(k_{1}!k_{2}!)^{2}}\\
&\quad\prod_{i_{1}=1}^{k_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{in}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}u^{(1)}_{i_{1}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}-\frac{\mathrm{z}}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{out}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}u^{(1)}_{i_{1}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)\left(\frac{1}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{in}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}v^{(1)}_{i_{1}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}-\frac{\mathrm{z}}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{out}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}v^{(1)}_{i_{1}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)\\
&\quad\cdot\prod_{i_{2}=1}^{k_{2}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{L}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}u^{(2)}_{i_{2}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{R}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}v^{(2)}_{i_{2}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\cdot\left(1-\mathrm{z}\right)^{k_{2}}\left(1-\frac{1}{\mathrm{z}}\right)^{k_{1}}\cdot\frac{f_{1}(U^{(1)};s_{1})f_{2}(U^{(2)};s_{2})}{f_{1}(V^{(1)};s_{1})f_{2}(V^{(2)};s_{2})}\cdot H(U^{(1)},U^{(2)};V^{(1)},V^{(2)})\\
&\quad\cdot\prod_{\ell=1}^{2}\frac{\left(\Delta(U^{(\ell)})\right)^{2}\left(\Delta(V^{(\ell)})\right)^{2}}{\left(\Delta(U^{(\ell)};V^{(\ell)})\right)^{2}}\cdot\frac{\Delta(U^{(1)};V^{(2)})\Delta(V^{(1)};U^{(2)})}{\Delta(U^{(1)};U^{(2)})\Delta(V^{(1)};V^{(2)})}.\end{split}

(2.71)

Note that when
k_{1}=0
, the summand is analytic for
\mathrm{z}=0
hence the integral of
\mathrm{z}
vanishes. When
k_{2}=0
, there is no
u^{(2)}_{i_{2}}
or
v_{i_{2}}^{(2)}
variable, hence the
u_{i_{1}}^{(1)}
and
v_{i_{1}}^{(1)}
contours can be deformed to
\Sigma_{\mathrm{L}}
and
\Sigma_{\mathrm{R}}
respectively. As a result, the
\mathrm{z}
integral can be separately written as

\oint_{0}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\mathrm{z}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}\mathrm{(1-z)^{2}}}\left(1-\frac{1}{\mathrm{z}}\right)^{k_{1}}=\begin{cases}-1,&k_{1}=1,\\
0,&k_{1}=0,\text{or }k_{1}\geq 2.\end{cases}

However, it is direct to check that
H(U^{(1)},U^{(2)};V^{(1)},V^{(2)})=0
when
k_{1}=1
and
k_{2}=0
. Therefore the summand when
k_{2}=0
also vanishes. Thus we can replace the sum
\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}\geq 0}
by
\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}\geq 1}
, and
arrive at the formula (
1.7
).

3
Asymptotic analysis and proof of Theorem
1.3

In this section, we will perform asymptotic analysis for the formulas obtained in Theorem
1.1
and prove Theorem
1.3
.
The main technical result of this section is as follows.

Proposition 3.1
.

Suppose
\alpha>0,\gamma\in(0,1)
are fixed constants. Assume that

\begin{split}M&=[\alpha N],\\
m&=[\gamma\alpha N+x_{1}\alpha^{2/3}(1+\sqrt{\alpha})^{2/3}N^{2/3}],\\
n&=[\gamma N+x_{2}\alpha^{-1/3}(1+\sqrt{\alpha})^{2/3}N^{2/3}],\\
t_{1}&=d((1,1),(m,n))+\mathrm{t}_{1}\cdot\alpha^{-1/6}(1+\sqrt{\alpha})^{4/3}N^{1/3},\\
t_{2}&=d((m+1,n),(M,N))+\mathrm{t}_{2}\cdot\alpha^{-1/6}(1+\sqrt{\alpha})^{4/3}N^{1/3},\\
t_{2}^{\prime}&=d((m,n+1),(M,N))+\mathrm{t}_{2}\cdot\alpha^{-1/6}(1+\sqrt{\alpha})^{4/3}N^{1/3},\end{split}

(3.1)

for some real numbers
x_{1},x_{2}
. Then

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}\left((m,n),(m+1,n)\in{\mathcal{G}}_{(1,1)}(M,N),L_{(1,1)}(m,n)\geq t_{1},L_{(m+1,n)}(M,N)\geq t_{2}\right)\\
&=\alpha^{1/3}(1+\sqrt{\alpha})^{-2/3}N^{-2/3}\int_{\mathrm{t}_{1}}^{\infty}\int_{\mathrm{t}_{2}}^{\infty}\mathrm{p}(\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{2},\mathrm{x}=x_{2}-x_{1};\gamma)\mathrm{d}{\mathrm{s}_{2}}\mathrm{d}{\mathrm{s}_{2}}+\mathcal{O}(N^{-1}(\log N)^{5}),\end{split}

(3.2)

and similarly

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}\left((m,n),(m,n+1)\in{\mathcal{G}}_{(1,1)}(M,N),L_{(1,1)}(m,n)\geq t_{1},L_{(m,n+1)}(M,N)\geq t^{\prime}_{2}\right)\\
&=\alpha^{-2/3}(1+\sqrt{\alpha})^{-2/3}N^{-2/3}\int_{\mathrm{t}_{1}}^{\infty}\int_{\mathrm{t}_{2}}^{\infty}\mathrm{p}(\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{2},\mathrm{x}=x_{2}-x_{1};\gamma)\mathrm{d}{\mathrm{s}_{2}}\mathrm{d}{\mathrm{s}_{1}}+\mathcal{O}(N^{-1}(\log N)^{5})\end{split}

(3.3)

as
N
becomes large, and the
\mathcal{O}(N^{-1}(\log N)^{5})
errors are uniformly for
x_{1},x_{2}
in any given compact set and for
\mathrm{t}_{1},\mathrm{t}_{2}
in any given set with a finite lower bound.

The proof of Proposition will be provided later in this section.
Below we prove Theorem
1.3
assuming Proposition
3.1
.

Recall that
\pi
is an up/left lattice path from
(m,n)
to
(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})
. See Figure
4
for an illustration. We first realize that there are different types of lattice points
(a,b)\in\pi
depending on whether
(a+1,b)
and
(a,b+1)
are on
\pi
or not. We call
(a,b)\in\pi
is a horizontal point if
(a,b+1)\notin\pi
, and a vertical point if
(a+1,b)\notin\pi
. Note there are outer corners which are both horizontal and vertical points, and inner corners which are neither horizontal nor vertical points. We also note that an exit point
\mathbf{p}
must be a horizontal point
\mathbf{p}=(a,b)
with
\mathbf{p}_{+}=(a,b+1)
, or a vertical point
\mathbf{p}=(a,b)
with
\mathbf{p}_{+}=(a+1,b)
. We write

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}\left(\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathcal{G}}_{(1,1)}(M,N)\text{ intersects $\pi$, and exits $\pi$ at some point $\mathbf{p}=(a,b)$,}\\
\mbox{and }L_{(1,1)}(\mathbf{p})\geq t_{1}=d((1,1),\mathbf{p})+\mathrm{t}_{1}\cdot\alpha^{-1/6}(1+\sqrt{\alpha})^{4/3}N^{1/3},\\
\mbox{and }L_{\mathbf{p}_{+}}(M,N)\geq t_{2}=d(\mathbf{p}_{+},(M,N))+\mathrm{t}_{2}\cdot\alpha^{-1/6}(1+\sqrt{\alpha})^{4/3}N^{1/3}\\
\end{array}\right)\\
&=\sum_{(a,b)\in\pi\text{ is a vertical point }}\mathbb{P}\left(\begin{array}[]{l}(a,b)\in{\mathcal{G}}_{(1,1)}(M,N)\text{ and }(a+1,b)\in{\mathcal{G}}_{(1,1)}{(M,N)},\\
\mbox{and }L_{(1,1)}(a,b)\geq d((1,1),(a,b))+\mathrm{t}_{1}\cdot\alpha^{-1/6}(1+\sqrt{\alpha})^{4/3}N^{1/3},\\
\mbox{and }L_{(a+1,b)}(M,N)\geq d((a+1,b),(M,N))+\mathrm{t}_{2}\cdot\alpha^{-1/6}(1+\sqrt{\alpha})^{4/3}N^{1/3}\\
\end{array}\right)\\
&+\sum_{(a,b)\in\pi\text{ is a horizontal point }}\mathbb{P}\left(\begin{array}[]{l}(a,b)\in{\mathcal{G}}_{(1,1)}(M,N)\text{ and }(a,b+1)\in{\mathcal{G}}_{(1,1)}{(M,N)},\\
\mbox{and }L_{(1,1)}(a,b)\geq d((1,1),(a,b))+\mathrm{t}_{1}\cdot\alpha^{-1/6}(1+\sqrt{\alpha})^{4/3}N^{1/3},\\
\mbox{and }L_{(a,b+1)}(M,N)\geq d((a,b+1),(M,N))+\mathrm{t}_{2}\cdot\alpha^{-1/6}(1+\sqrt{\alpha})^{4/3}N^{1/3}\\
\end{array}\right).\end{split}

(3.4)

(m,n)
(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})
scaled
\mathrm{x}

Figure 4:
An illustration of the sum (
3.4
). The square-shaped points are vertical points, and the round-shaped points are horizontal points. The sum can be viewed as a Riemann sum along the axis
\mathrm{x}
, where the horizontal points contribute to the spring parts and the vertical points contribute to the thick part.

Now we apply Proposition
3.1
and view the right hand side of (
3.4
) as a Riemann sum of the quantity
\int_{\mathrm{t}_{1}}^{\infty}\int_{\mathrm{t}_{2}}^{\infty}\mathrm{p}(\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{2},\mathrm{x};\gamma)\mathrm{d}{\mathrm{s}_{2}}\mathrm{d}{\mathrm{s}_{1}}
over an interval
\mathrm{x}\in[x_{2}-x_{1},x^{\prime}_{2}-x^{\prime}_{1}]
, plus an error terms
\mathcal{O}(N^{-1}(\log N)^{5})\times\mathcal{O}(N^{2/3})=\mathcal{O}(N^{-1/3}(\log N)^{5})
. See Figure
4
for an illustration. It is easy to see from the definition that
\int_{\mathrm{t}_{1}}^{\infty}\int_{\mathrm{t}_{2}}^{\infty}\mathrm{p}(\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{2},\mathrm{x};\gamma)\mathrm{d}{\mathrm{s}_{2}}\mathrm{d}{\mathrm{s}_{1}}
is continuous in
\mathrm{x}
. Thus the Riemman sum converges to the desired integral in (
1.16
), and we complete the proof of Theorem
1.3
.

The remaining part of this section is the proof of Proposition
3.1
. We first realize that (
3.3
) and (
3.2
) are equivalent. In fact, if we switch rows and columns and replace
\alpha
by
\alpha^{-1}
in the equation (
3.3
), we obtain (
3.2
) with
-\mathrm{x}
instead of
\mathrm{x}
appearing on the right hand side. Note that
\mathrm{p}(\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{2},\mathrm{x};\gamma)=\mathrm{p}(\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{2},-\mathrm{x};\gamma)
, see Remark
1.6
. We hence obtain the equivalence of (
3.3
) and (
3.2
). It remains to prove one equation (
3.2
).

Using Theorem
1.1
, we write the left hand side of (
3.2
) as

\mathbb{P}\left(\begin{array}[]{l}(m,n),(m+1,n)\in{\mathcal{G}}_{(1,1)}{(M,N)},\\
\mbox{and }L_{(1,1)}(m,n)\geq t_{1},\\
\mbox{and }L_{(m+1,n)}(M,N)\geq t_{2}\\
\end{array}\right)=\oint_{0}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\mathrm{z}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}\mathrm{(1-z)^{2}}}\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}\geq 1}\frac{1}{(k_{1}!k_{2}!)^{2}}\hat{T}_{k_{1},k_{2}}(\mathrm{z};t_{1},t_{2};m,n,M,N),

(3.5)

where

\begin{split}&\hat{T}_{k_{1},k_{2}}(\mathrm{z};t_{1},t_{2};m,n,M,N)\\
&=\int_{t_{1}}^{\infty}\int_{t_{2}}^{\infty}T_{k_{1},k_{2}}(\mathrm{z};s_{1},s_{2};m,n,M,N)\mathrm{d}s_{2}\mathrm{d}s_{1}\\
&=\prod_{i_{1}=1}^{k_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{in}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}u^{(1)}_{i_{1}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}-\frac{\mathrm{z}}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{out}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}u^{(1)}_{i_{1}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)\left(\frac{1}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{in}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}v^{(1)}_{i_{1}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}-\frac{\mathrm{z}}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{out}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}v^{(1)}_{i_{1}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)\\
&\quad\cdot\prod_{i_{2}=1}^{k_{2}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{L}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}u^{(2)}_{i_{2}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{R}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}v^{(2)}_{i_{2}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\cdot\left(1-\mathrm{z}\right)^{k_{2}}\left(1-\frac{1}{\mathrm{z}}\right)^{k_{1}}\cdot\frac{f_{1}(U^{(1)};t_{1})f_{2}(U^{(2)};t_{2})}{f_{1}(V^{(1)};t_{1})f_{2}(V^{(2)};t_{2})}\cdot\frac{1}{\prod_{\ell=1}^{2}\sum_{i_{\ell}=1}^{k_{\ell}}(u_{i_{\ell}}^{(\ell)}-v_{i_{\ell}}^{(\ell)})}\\
&\quad\cdot H(U^{(1)},U^{(2)};V^{(1)},V^{(2)})\cdot\prod_{\ell=1}^{2}\frac{\left(\Delta(U^{(\ell)})\right)^{2}\left(\Delta(V^{(\ell)})\right)^{2}}{\left(\Delta(U^{(\ell)};V^{(\ell)})\right)^{2}}\cdot\frac{\Delta(U^{(1)};V^{(2)})\Delta(V^{(1)};U^{(2)})}{\Delta(U^{(1)};U^{(2)})\Delta(V^{(1)};V^{(2)})},\end{split}

(3.6)

with the functions
f_{1}(w;t_{1})
and
f_{2}(w;t_{2})
defined in (
1.9
), and the function
H
defined by (
1.10
). We remark that in the above equation we evaluated the integral over
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
using the fact
\mathrm{Re}u_{i_{\ell}}^{(\ell)}<\mathrm{Re}v_{i_{\ell}}^{(\ell)}
due to the order of the contours.

Similarly, we can write

\begin{split}\int_{\mathrm{t}_{1}}^{\infty}\int_{\mathrm{t}_{2}}^{\infty}\mathrm{p}(\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{2},\mathrm{x};\gamma)\mathrm{d}{\mathrm{s}_{2}}\mathrm{d}{\mathrm{s}_{1}}=\oint_{0}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\mathrm{z}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}\mathrm{(1-z)^{2}}}\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}\geq 1}\frac{1}{(k_{1}!k_{2}!)^{2}}\mathrm{\hat{T}}_{k_{1},k_{2}}(\mathrm{z};\mathrm{t}_{1},\mathrm{t}_{2},\mathrm{x};\gamma)\end{split}

(3.7)

with

\begin{split}&\mathrm{\hat{T}}_{k_{1},k_{2}}(\mathrm{z};\mathrm{t}_{1},\mathrm{t}_{2},\mathrm{x};\gamma)=\int_{\mathrm{t}_{1}}^{\infty}\int_{\mathrm{t}_{2}}^{\infty}\mathrm{T}_{k_{1},k_{2}}(\mathrm{z};\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{2},\mathrm{x};\gamma)\mathrm{d}{\mathrm{s}_{2}}\mathrm{d}{\mathrm{s}_{1}}\\
&=\prod_{i_{1}=1}^{k_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Gamma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{in}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\xi^{(1)}_{i_{1}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}-\frac{\mathrm{z}}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Gamma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{out}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\xi^{(1)}_{i_{1}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)\left(\frac{1}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Gamma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{in}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\eta^{(1)}_{i_{1}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}-\frac{\mathrm{z}}{1-\mathrm{z}}\int_{\Gamma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{out}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\eta^{(1)}_{i_{1}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)\\
&\quad\cdot\prod_{i_{2}=1}^{k_{2}}\int_{\Gamma_{\mathrm{L}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\xi^{(2)}_{i_{2}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\int_{\Gamma_{\mathrm{R}}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\eta^{(2)}_{i_{2}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\cdot\left(1-\mathrm{z}\right)^{k_{2}}\left(1-\frac{1}{\mathrm{z}}\right)^{k_{1}}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{f}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(1)};\mathrm{t}_{1})\mathrm{f}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(2)};\mathrm{t}_{2})}{\mathrm{f}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(1)};\mathrm{t}_{1})\mathrm{f}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(2)};\mathrm{t}_{2})}\cdot\frac{1}{\prod_{\ell=1}^{2}\sum_{i_{\ell}=1}^{k_{\ell}}(\xi_{i_{\ell}}^{(\ell)}-\eta_{i_{\ell}}^{(\ell)})}\\
&\quad\cdot\mathrm{H}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(2)})\cdot\prod_{\ell=1}^{2}\frac{\left(\Delta(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(\ell)})\right)^{2}\left(\Delta(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(\ell)})\right)^{2}}{\left(\Delta(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(\ell)};\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(\ell)})\right)^{2}}\cdot\frac{\Delta(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(2)})\Delta(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(2)})}{\Delta(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(2)})\Delta(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(2)})},\end{split}

(3.8)

where the functions
\mathrm{f}_{1}(\zeta;\mathrm{t})
and
\mathrm{f}_{2}(\zeta;\mathrm{t})
are defined in (
1.24
), and the function
\mathrm{H}
is defined in (
1.25
). We remark that in the above calculations we exchanged the integrals and the summations. We need to justify that they are exchangeable. It is tedious but not hard to check that

\int_{t_{1}}^{\infty}\int_{t_{2}}^{\infty}\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}\geq 1}\frac{1}{(k_{1}!k_{2}!)^{2}}\left|T_{k_{1},k_{2}}(\mathrm{z};s_{1},s_{2};m,n,M,N)\right||\mathrm{d}s_{2}||\mathrm{d}s_{1}|<C(\mathrm{z})<\infty

(3.9)

and

\int_{\mathrm{t}_{1}}^{\infty}\int_{\mathrm{t}_{2}}^{\infty}\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}\geq 1}\frac{1}{(k_{1}!k_{2}!)^{2}}\left|\mathrm{T}_{k_{1},k_{2}}(\mathrm{z};\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{2},\mathrm{x};\gamma)\right||\mathrm{d}\mathrm{s}_{2}||\mathrm{d}\mathrm{s}_{1}|<\mathrm{C}(\mathrm{z})<\infty

(3.10)

for some constants
C(\mathrm{z})
and
\mathrm{C}(\mathrm{z})
which only depend on
\mathrm{z}
. Moreover,
C(\mathrm{z})
and
\mathrm{C}(\mathrm{z})
are both continuous in
\mathrm{z}
(except at
\mathrm{z}=0
or
-1
) hence they are uniformly bounded for
|\mathrm{z}|=
constant that lies in
(0,1)
. Here we omit the proof of these inequalities since it is similar to that of Lemma
3.3
. Using these inequalities we verify that the exchanges of integrals and summations are valid and equations (
3.5
) and (
3.7
) hold.

To proceed, we need to compare (
3.5
) and (
3.7
) term by term and estimate their difference. There is a need to see the dependence of the error on the parameters. We will fix the contour of
\mathrm{z}
to be a circle with fixed radius
|\mathrm{z}|\in(0,1)
. We also introduce the following notation.

Notation 3.2
.

we use the calligraphic font
\mathcal{C}
(or
\mathcal{C}_{i}
with some index
i
) to denote a positive constant term (independent of
N
) satisfying the following three conditions:

(1)

\mathcal{C}
is independent of
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
.

(2)

\mathcal{C}
is continuous in
\mathrm{z}
.

(3)

\mathcal{C}
is continuous in
\mathrm{t}_{1}
and
\mathrm{t}_{2}
, and decays exponentially as
\mathrm{t}_{1}\to\infty
or
\mathrm{t}_{2}\to\infty
.

Throughout this whole section, we will use
\mathcal{C}
as described in Notation
3.2
, and the regular
C
as a constant independent of the parameters.

We will show the following two lemmas in subsequent subsections.

Lemma 3.3
.

We have the estimate

\left|\mathrm{\hat{T}}_{k_{1},k_{2}}(\mathrm{z};\mathrm{t}_{1},\mathrm{t}_{2},\mathrm{x};\gamma)\right|\leq k_{1}^{k_{1}/2}k_{2}^{k_{2}/2}(k_{1}+k_{2})^{(k_{1}+k_{2})/2}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{k_{1}+k_{2}}

for all
k_{1},k_{2}\geq 1
, where
\mathcal{C}_{1}
is a positive constant as described in Notation
3.2
.

Lemma 3.4
.

With the same assumptions as in Proposition
3.1
, there is a constant
\mathcal{C}_{2}
as described in Notation
3.2
such that

\begin{split}&\left|N^{2/3}\hat{T}_{k_{1},k_{2}}(\mathrm{z};t_{1},t_{2};m,n,M,N)-\alpha^{1/3}(1+\sqrt{\alpha})^{-2/3}\mathrm{\hat{T}}_{k_{1},k_{2}}(\mathrm{z};\mathrm{t}_{1},\mathrm{t}_{2},\mathrm{x};\gamma)\right|\\
&\leq k_{1}^{k_{1}/2}k_{2}^{k_{2}/2}(k_{1}+k_{2})^{(k_{1}+k_{2})/2}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{k_{1}+k_{2}}N^{-1/3}(\log N)^{5}\end{split}

(3.11)

for all
k_{1},k_{2}\geq 1
as
N
becomes sufficiently large.

Now we use these two lemmas to prove (
3.2
). We first use and realize that the right hand side of (
3.7
) is uniformly bounded by

\begin{split}&\oint_{0}\left|\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\mathrm{z}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}\mathrm{(1-z)^{2}}}\right|\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}\geq 1}\frac{1}{(k_{1}!k_{2}!)^{2}}\left|\mathrm{\hat{T}}_{k_{1},k_{2}}(\mathrm{z};\mathrm{t}_{1},\mathrm{t}_{2},\mathrm{x};\gamma)\right|\\
&\leq\oint_{0}\left|\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\mathrm{z}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}\mathrm{(1-z)^{2}}}\right|\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}\geq 1}\frac{1}{(k_{1}!k_{2}!)^{2}}k_{1}^{k_{1}/2}k_{2}^{k_{2}/2}(k_{1}+k_{2})^{(k_{1}+k_{2})/2}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{k_{1}+k_{2}}<\infty,\end{split}

where the last inequality is due to the Stirling’s approximation formula
k!\approx k^{k}e^{-k}\sqrt{2\pi k}
for large
k
.

Similarly we know that

\begin{split}&\oint_{0}\left|\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\mathrm{z}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}\mathrm{(1-z)^{2}}}\right|\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}\geq 1}\frac{1}{(k_{1}!k_{2}!)^{2}}\left|N^{2/3}\hat{T}_{k_{1},k_{2}}(\mathrm{z};t_{1},t_{2};m,n,M,N)-\alpha^{1/3}(1+\sqrt{\alpha})^{-2/3}\mathrm{\hat{T}}_{k_{1},k_{2}}(\mathrm{z};\mathrm{t}_{1},\mathrm{t}_{2},\mathrm{x};\gamma)\right|\\
&\leq\oint_{0}\left|\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\mathrm{z}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}\mathrm{(1-z)^{2}}}\right|\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}\geq 1}\frac{1}{(k_{1}!k_{2}!)^{2}}k_{1}^{k_{1}/2}k_{2}^{k_{2}/2}(k_{1}+k_{2})^{(k_{1}+k_{2})/2}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{k_{1}+k_{2}}N^{-1/3}(\log N)^{5}<\infty\end{split}

for sufficiently large
N
.

Combining the above two estimates we also know the right hand side of (
3.5
) multiplied by
N^{2/3}
is also uniformly bounded by the sum of the above two bounds

\begin{split}&N^{2/3}\oint_{0}\left|\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\mathrm{z}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}\mathrm{(1-z)^{2}}}\right|\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}\geq 1}\frac{1}{(k_{1}!k_{2}!)^{2}}\left|\hat{T}_{k_{1},k_{2}}(\mathrm{z};t_{1},t_{2};m,n,M,N)\right|\\
&\leq\oint_{0}\left|\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\mathrm{z}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}\mathrm{(1-z)^{2}}}\right|\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}\geq 1}\frac{1}{(k_{1}!k_{2}!)^{2}}k_{1}^{k_{1}/2}k_{2}^{k_{2}/2}(k_{1}+k_{2})^{(k_{1}+k_{2})/2}\\
&\qquad\cdot\left(\alpha^{1/3}(1+\sqrt{\alpha})^{-2/3}\mathcal{C}_{1}^{k_{1}+k_{2}}+\mathcal{C}_{2}^{k_{1}+k_{2}}N^{-1/3}(\log N)^{5}\right)\\
&<\infty.\end{split}

The above estimates imply that we can rewrite, using (
3.5
) and (
3.7
),

\begin{split}&N^{2/3}\mathbb{P}\left(\begin{array}[]{l}(m,n),(m+1,n)\in{\mathcal{G}}_{(1,1)}{(M,N)},\\
\mbox{and }L_{(1,1)}(m,n)\geq t_{1},\\
\mbox{and }L_{(m+1,n)}(M,N)\geq t_{2}\\
\end{array}\right)-\alpha^{1/3}(1+\sqrt{\alpha})^{-2/3}\int_{\mathrm{t}_{1}}^{\infty}\int_{\mathrm{t}_{2}}^{\infty}\mathrm{p}(\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{2},\mathrm{x};\gamma)\mathrm{d}{\mathrm{s}_{2}}\mathrm{d}{\mathrm{s}_{1}}\\
&=\oint_{0}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\mathrm{z}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}\mathrm{(1-z)^{2}}}\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}\geq 1}\frac{1}{(k_{1}!k_{2}!)^{2}}\left(N^{2/3}\hat{T}_{k_{1},k_{2}}(\mathrm{z};t_{1},t_{2};m,n,M,N)-\alpha^{1/3}(1+\sqrt{\alpha})^{-2/3}\mathrm{\hat{T}}_{k_{1},k_{2}}(\mathrm{z};\mathrm{t}_{1},\mathrm{t}_{2},\mathrm{x};\gamma)\right),\end{split}

which is uniformly bounded by, using Lemma
3.4
,

\oint_{0}\left|\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\mathrm{z}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}\mathrm{(1-z)^{2}}}\right|\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}\geq 1}\frac{1}{(k_{1}!k_{2}!)^{2}}k_{1}^{k_{1}/2}k_{2}^{k_{2}/2}(k_{1}+k_{2})^{(k_{1}+k_{2})/2}\mathcal{C}_{2}^{k_{1}+k_{2}}N^{-1/3}(\log N)^{5}=\mathcal{O}(N^{-1/3}(\log N)^{5})

for sufficiently large
N
. Thus (
3.2
) holds.

It remains to prove the two lemmas
3.3
and
3.4
. Note that if we did not have the factors
\frac{1}{\prod_{\ell=1}^{2}\sum_{i_{\ell}=1}^{k_{\ell}}(u_{i_{\ell}}^{(\ell)}-v_{i_{\ell}}^{(\ell)})}
and

H(U^{(1)},U^{(2)};V^{(1)},V^{(2)})
in the integrand of
T_{k_{1},k_{2}}(\mathrm{z};t_{1},t_{2};m,n,M,N)
, and the factors
\frac{1}{\prod_{\ell=1}^{2}\sum_{i_{\ell}=1}^{k_{\ell}}(\xi_{i_{\ell}}^{(\ell)}-\eta_{i_{\ell}}^{(\ell)})}
and

\mathrm{H}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(2)})
in the integrand of
\mathrm{\hat{T}}_{k_{1},k_{2}}(\mathrm{z};\mathrm{t}_{1},\mathrm{t}_{2},\mathrm{x};\gamma)
, the right hand sides of both (
3.5
) and (
3.7
) could be viewed as expansions of Fredholm determinants. They have similar structures as the expansion of the two-time distribution formulas in TASEP, see
[
Liu19
, Proposition 2.10]
. Moreover, the two lemmas above are indeed analogous to Lemmas 7.1 and 7.2 in
[
Liu19
]
. So it is not surprising that we can modify the standard asymptotic analysis for Fredholm determinants to prove these two lemmas. However, we do need some tedious calculations to incorporate the extra factors, and much finer estimates in Lemmas
3.3
and
3.4
compared with the analogs in
[
Liu19
]
. Our proof will also be illustrative to prove similar statements in our follow-up papers.

We will prove the Lemma
3.3
and
3.4
in the following two subsections.

3.1
Proof of Lemma
3.3

In this subsection we prove Lemma
3.3
. Some estimates we use here will also appear in the proof of the lemmas
3.4
in the next subsection.

We first estimate the factor

\mathrm{B}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(2)}):=\prod_{\ell=1}^{2}\frac{\left(\Delta(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(\ell)})\right)^{2}\left(\Delta(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(\ell)})\right)^{2}}{\left(\Delta(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(\ell)};\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(\ell)})\right)^{2}}\cdot\frac{\Delta(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(2)})\Delta(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(2)})}{\Delta(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(2)})\Delta(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(2)})}.

Observe that this factor is the product of the following three Cauchy determinants up to a sign

\begin{split}\mathrm{B}_{1}&=\det\left[\frac{1}{\xi^{(1)}_{i_{1}}-\eta^{(1)}_{j_{1}}}\right]_{i_{1},j_{1}=1}^{k_{1}}=(-1)^{k_{1}(k_{1}-1)/2}\frac{\Delta(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(1)})\Delta(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(1)})}{\Delta(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(1)})},\\
\mathrm{B}_{2}&=\det\left[\frac{1}{\xi^{(2)}_{i_{2}}-\eta^{(2)}_{j_{2}}}\right]_{i_{2},j_{2}=1}^{k_{2}}=(-1)^{k_{2}(k_{2}-1)/2}\frac{\Delta(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(2)})\Delta(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(2)})}{\Delta(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(2)})},\\
\mathrm{B}_{3}&=\det\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc|ccc}&\vdots&&&\vdots&\\
\cdots&\displaystyle\frac{1}{\xi_{i_{1}}^{(1)}-\eta_{j_{1}}^{(1)}}&\cdots&\cdots&\displaystyle\frac{1}{\xi_{i_{1}}^{(1)}-\xi_{j_{2}}^{(2)}}&\cdots\\
&\vdots&&&\vdots&\\
\hline\cr&\vdots&&&\vdots&\\
\cdots&\displaystyle\frac{1}{\eta_{i_{2}}^{(2)}-\eta_{j_{1}}^{(1)}}&\cdots&\cdots&\displaystyle\frac{1}{\eta_{i_{2}}^{(2)}-\xi_{j_{2}}^{(2)}}&\cdots\\
&\vdots&&&\vdots&\end{array}\right]_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i_{1},j_{1}\leq k_{1}\\
1\leq i_{2},j_{2}\leq k_{2}\end{subarray}}\\
&=(-1)^{k_{1}(k_{1}-1)/2+k_{2}(k_{2}+1)/2}\frac{\Delta(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(1)})\Delta(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(1)})}{\Delta(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(1)})}\cdot\frac{\Delta(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(2)})\Delta(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(2)})}{\Delta(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(2)})}\cdot\frac{\Delta(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(2)})\Delta(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(2)})}{\Delta(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(2)})\Delta(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(2)})}.\end{split}

By applying the Hadamard’s inequality, we have

\left|\mathrm{B}_{1}\right|\leq\prod_{i_{1}=1}^{k_{1}}\sqrt{\sum_{j_{1}=1}^{k_{1}}\left|\xi^{(1)}_{i_{1}}-\eta^{(1)}_{j_{1}}\right|^{-2}}\leq k_{1}^{k_{1}/2}\prod_{i_{1}=1}^{k_{1}}\frac{1}{\mathrm{dist}(\xi_{i_{1}}^{(1)})},

where
\mathrm{dist}(\xi)
denotes the shortest distance from the point
\xi
to the contours
\Gamma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{out}},\Gamma_{\mathrm{L}},\Gamma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{in}},\Gamma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{out}},\Gamma_{\mathrm{R}},\Gamma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{in}}
except for the one contour which
\xi
belongs to. For example, if
\xi_{i_{1}}^{(1)}\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{out}}
, then
\mathrm{dist}(\xi_{i_{1}}^{(1)})
is the distance from
\xi_{i_{1}}^{(1)}
to
\Gamma_{\mathrm{L}}\cup\Gamma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{out}}
, where we ignored the contours
\Gamma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{out}},\Gamma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{in}},\Gamma_{\mathrm{R}},
and
\Gamma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{in}}
since
\Gamma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{out}}
is the contour
\xi_{i_{1}}^{(1)}
belongs to, and the other three contours are farther to the point
\xi_{i_{1}}^{(1)}
compared with
\Gamma_{\mathrm{L}}
and
\Gamma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{out}}
.

Similarly, we have

\mathrm{B}_{2}\leq k_{2}^{k_{2}/2}\prod_{i_{2}=1}^{k_{2}}\frac{1}{\mathrm{dist}(\eta_{i_{2}}^{(2)})},

and

\mathrm{B}_{3}\leq(k_{1}+k_{2})^{(k_{1}+k_{2})/2}\prod_{j_{1}=1}^{k_{1}}\frac{1}{\mathrm{dist}(\eta_{j_{1}}^{(1)})}\prod_{j_{2}=1}^{k_{2}}\frac{1}{\mathrm{dist}(\xi_{j_{2}}^{(2)})}.

We combine the above estimates and obtain

\begin{split}&\left|\mathrm{B}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(2)})\right|\\
&\leq k_{1}^{k_{1}/2}k_{2}^{k_{2}/2}(k_{1}+k_{2})^{(k_{1}+k_{2})/2}\prod_{i_{1}=1}^{k_{1}}\frac{1}{\mathrm{dist}(\xi_{i_{1}}^{(1)})}\prod_{i_{2}=1}^{k_{2}}\frac{1}{\mathrm{dist}(\eta_{i_{2}}^{(2)})}\prod_{j_{1}=1}^{k_{1}}\frac{1}{\mathrm{dist}(\eta_{j_{1}}^{(1)})}\prod_{j_{2}=1}^{k_{2}}\frac{1}{\mathrm{dist}(\xi_{j_{2}}^{(2)})}.\end{split}

(3.12)

Now we consider the factor
\mathrm{H}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(2)})=\frac{1}{12}\mathrm{S}_{1}^{4}+\frac{1}{4}\mathrm{S}_{2}^{2}-\frac{1}{3}\mathrm{S}_{1}\mathrm{S}_{3}
which is defined in (
1.25
). We use the trivial bounds

\begin{split}|\mathrm{S}_{\ell}|&=\left|\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{k_{1}}\left(\left(\xi_{i_{1}}^{(1)}\right)^{\ell}-\left(\eta_{i_{1}}^{(1)}\right)^{\ell}\right)-\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{k_{2}}\left(\left(\xi_{i_{2}}^{(2)}\right)^{\ell}-\left(\eta_{i_{2}}^{(2)}\right)^{\ell}\right)\right|\\
&\leq\prod_{i_{1}=1}^{k_{1}}\left(1+|\xi_{i_{1}}^{(1)}|^{\ell}\right)\left(1+|\eta_{i_{1}}^{(1)}|^{\ell}\right)\prod_{i_{2}=1}^{k_{2}}\left(1+|\xi_{i_{2}}^{(2)}|^{\ell}\right)\left(1+|\eta_{i_{2}}^{(2)}|^{\ell}\right)\\
&\leq\prod_{i_{1}=1}^{k_{1}}\mathrm{g}_{1}\left(|\xi_{i_{1}}^{(1)}|\right)\mathrm{g}_{1}\left(|\eta_{i_{1}}^{(1)}|\right)\prod_{i_{2}=1}^{k_{2}}\mathrm{g}_{1}\left(|\xi_{i_{2}}^{(2)}|\right)\mathrm{g}_{1}\left(|\eta_{i_{2}}^{(2)}|\right),\quad\ell=1,2,3,\end{split}

where
\mathrm{g}_{1}(y):=1+y+y^{2}+y^{3}
. Note that
\mathrm{g}_{1}^{2}(y)\leq\mathrm{g}_{1}^{4}(y)
for all
y\geq 0
. Thus

\begin{split}|\mathrm{H}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(2)})|&\leq\frac{1}{12}|\mathrm{S}_{1}^{4}|+\frac{1}{4}|\mathrm{S}_{2}^{2}|+\frac{1}{3}|\mathrm{S}_{1}\mathrm{S}_{3}|\\
&\leq\prod_{i_{1}=1}^{k_{1}}\mathrm{g}_{1}^{4}\left(|\xi_{i_{1}}^{(1)}|\right)\mathrm{g}_{1}^{4}\left(|\eta_{i_{1}}^{(1)}|\right)\prod_{i_{2}=1}^{k_{2}}\mathrm{g}_{1}^{4}\left(|\xi_{i_{2}}^{(2)}|\right)\mathrm{g}_{1}^{4}\left(|\eta_{i_{2}}^{(2)}|\right).\end{split}

(3.13)

Finally, we note that the locations of contours imply that
\mathrm{Re}(\xi_{i_{\ell}}^{(\ell)})<0
for
\xi_{i_{\ell}}^{(\ell)}\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{L}}\cup\Gamma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{out}}\cup\Gamma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{in}}
, and
\mathrm{Re}(\eta_{i_{\ell}}^{(\ell)})>0
for
\eta_{i_{\ell}}^{(\ell)}\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{R}}\cup\Gamma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{out}}\cup\Gamma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{in}}
. Thus we have a trivial bound

\begin{split}\left|\frac{1}{\prod_{\ell=1}^{2}\sum_{i_{\ell}=1}^{k_{\ell}}(\xi_{i_{\ell}}^{(\ell)}-\eta_{i_{\ell}}^{(\ell)})}\right|&\leq\frac{1}{\mathrm{Re}(\eta_{1}^{(1)}-\xi_{1}^{(1)})}\cdot\frac{1}{\mathrm{Re}(\eta_{1}^{(2)}-\xi_{1}^{(2)})}\leq\frac{1}{\mathrm{Re}(\eta_{1}^{(1)})}\cdot\frac{1}{\mathrm{Re}(\eta_{1}^{(2)})}\\
&\leq\left(1+\frac{1}{\mathrm{Re}(\eta_{1}^{(1)})}\right)\left(1+\frac{1}{\mathrm{Re}(-\xi_{1}^{(1)})}\right)\left(1+\frac{1}{\mathrm{Re}(\eta_{1}^{(2)})}\right)\left(1+\frac{1}{\mathrm{Re}(-\xi_{1}^{(2)})}\right)\\
&\leq\prod_{i_{1}=1}^{k_{1}}\mathrm{g}_{2}\left(\xi_{i_{1}}^{(1)}\right)\mathrm{g}_{2}\left(\eta_{i_{1}}^{(1)}\right)\prod_{i_{2}=1}^{k_{2}}\mathrm{g}_{2}\left(\xi_{i_{2}}^{(2)}\right)\mathrm{g}_{2}\left(\eta_{i_{2}}^{(2)}\right),\end{split}

(3.14)

where
\mathrm{g}_{2}(w):=1+|\mathrm{Re}(w)|^{-1}
for all
w\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}
.

Now we insert all the estimates (
3.12
), (
3.13
) and (
3.14
) in the equation (
3.8
) and obtain

\begin{split}&\left|\mathrm{\hat{T}}_{k_{1},k_{2}}(\mathrm{z};\mathrm{t}_{1},\mathrm{t}_{2},\mathrm{x};\gamma)\right|\leq k_{1}^{k_{1}/2}k_{2}^{k_{2}/2}(k_{1}+k_{2})^{(k_{1}+k_{2})/2}\\
&\quad\cdot\prod_{i_{1}=1}^{k_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{|1-\mathrm{z}|}\int_{\Gamma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{in}}}\frac{|\mathrm{d}\xi_{i_{1}}^{(1)}|}{2\pi}+\frac{\mathrm{|z|}}{|1-\mathrm{z}|}\int_{\Gamma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{out}}}\frac{|\mathrm{d}\xi_{i_{1}}^{(1)}|}{2\pi}\right)\left(\frac{1}{|1-\mathrm{z}|}\int_{\Gamma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{in}}}\frac{|\mathrm{d}\eta_{i_{1}}^{(1)}|}{2\pi}+\frac{\mathrm{|z|}}{|1-\mathrm{z}|}\int_{\Gamma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{out}}}\frac{|\mathrm{d}\eta_{i_{1}}^{(1)}|}{2\pi}\right)\\
&\quad\cdot\prod_{i_{2}=1}^{k_{2}}\int_{\Gamma_{\mathrm{L}}}\frac{|\mathrm{d}\xi_{i_{2}}^{(2)}|}{2\pi}\int_{\Gamma_{\mathrm{R}}}\frac{|\mathrm{d}\eta_{i_{2}}^{(2)}|}{2\pi}\cdot\left|1-\mathrm{z}\right|^{k_{2}}\left|1-\frac{1}{\mathrm{z}}\right|^{k_{1}}\cdot\prod_{i_{1}=1}^{k_{1}}\mathrm{g}\left(\xi_{i_{1}}^{(1)}\right)\mathrm{g}\left(\eta_{i_{1}}^{(1)}\right)\prod_{i_{2}=1}^{k_{2}}\mathrm{g}\left(\xi_{i_{2}}^{(2)}\right)\mathrm{g}\left(\eta_{i_{2}}^{(2)}\right)\\
&=k_{1}^{k_{1}/2}k_{2}^{k_{2}/2}(k_{1}+k_{2})^{(k_{1}+k_{2})/2}\left|1-\mathrm{z}\right|^{k_{2}}\left|1-\frac{1}{\mathrm{z}}\right|^{k_{1}}\mathcal{C}_{1,1}^{k_{1}}\mathcal{C}_{1,2}^{k_{2}}\\
&\leq k_{1}^{k_{1}/2}k_{2}^{k_{2}/2}(k_{1}+k_{2})^{(k_{1}+k_{2})/2}\left(\left|1-\frac{1}{\mathrm{z}}\right|\mathcal{C}_{1,1}+\left|1-\mathrm{z}\right|\mathcal{C}_{1,2}\right)^{k_{1}+k_{2}},\end{split}

(3.15)

where

\mathrm{g}(\zeta)=\begin{cases}|\mathrm{f}_{1}(\zeta;\mathrm{t}_{1})|\mathrm{g}^{4}_{1}(|\zeta|)\mathrm{g}_{2}(\zeta)/\mathrm{dist}(\zeta),&\zeta\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{out}}\cup\Gamma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{in}},\\
|\mathrm{f}_{1}(\zeta;\mathrm{t}_{1})^{-1}|\mathrm{g}^{4}_{1}(|\zeta|)\mathrm{g}_{2}(\zeta)/\mathrm{dist}(\zeta),&\zeta\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{out}}\cup\Gamma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{in}},\\
|\mathrm{f}_{2}(\zeta;\mathrm{t}_{2})|\mathrm{g}^{4}_{1}(|\zeta|)\mathrm{g}_{2}(\zeta)/\mathrm{dist}(\zeta),&\zeta\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{L}},\\
|\mathrm{f}_{2}(\zeta;\mathrm{t}_{2})^{-1}|\mathrm{g}^{4}_{1}(|\zeta|)\mathrm{g}_{2}(\zeta)/\mathrm{dist}(\zeta),&\zeta\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{R}},\\
\end{cases}

and

\begin{split}\mathcal{C}_{1,1}&=\left(\frac{1}{|1-\mathrm{z}|}\int_{\Gamma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{in}}}\frac{\mathrm{g}(\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi|}{2\pi}+\frac{\mathrm{|z|}}{|1-\mathrm{z}|}\int_{\Gamma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{out}}}\frac{\mathrm{g}(\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi|}{2\pi}\right)\left(\frac{1}{|1-\mathrm{z}|}\int_{\Gamma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{in}}}\frac{\mathrm{g}(\eta)|\mathrm{d}\eta|}{2\pi}+\frac{\mathrm{|z|}}{|1-\mathrm{z}|}\int_{\Gamma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{out}}}\frac{\mathrm{g}(\eta)|\mathrm{d}\eta|}{2\pi}\right),\\
\mathcal{C}_{1,2}&=\left(\int_{\Gamma_{\mathrm{L}}}\frac{\mathrm{g}(\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi|}{2\pi}\right)\left(\int_{\Gamma_{\mathrm{R}}}\frac{\mathrm{g}(\eta)|\mathrm{d}\eta|}{2\pi}\right).\end{split}

We used the fact that
\mathrm{g}(\zeta)
decays exponentially when
\zeta
goes to infinity along the integration contours since all other factors are of polynomial order,
\mathrm{dist}(\zeta)
is bounded below, and the dominating factor
|\mathrm{f}_{\ell}|
(or
|\mathrm{f}_{\ell}^{-1}|
) decays super exponentially. By checking the parameters appearing in
\mathrm{f}_{\ell}
(and hence in
\mathrm{g}
), we find that both
\mathcal{C}_{1,1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{1,2}
satisfy the conditions described in Notation
3.2
. Thus (
3.15
) implies Lemma
3.3
with
\mathcal{C}_{1}=\left|1-\frac{1}{\mathrm{z}}\right|\mathcal{C}_{1,1}+\left|1-\mathrm{z}\right|\mathcal{C}_{1,2}
.

3.2
Proof of Lemma
3.4

The proof of Lemma
3.4
is more tedious. We separate the argument into three parts. In the first part we illustrate the proof strategy and show that Lemma
3.4
can be reduced to two other lemmas. In the remaining two parts we prove these lemmas respectively.

3.2.1
Proof strategy

Although the quantities
\hat{T}_{k_{1},k_{2}}
and
\mathrm{\hat{T}}_{k_{1},k_{2}}
only depend on how the integration contours are nested, we choose these contours explicitly to simplify our argument. The idea is that we split each contour into two parts with one part making most of the contribution in integration and the other part contributing an exponentially small error only.

We first choose the six contours appearing in the terms
\mathrm{\hat{T}}_{k_{1},k_{2}}
. As we introduced before, we assume
\Gamma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{out}},\Gamma_{\mathrm{L}}
and
\Gamma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{in}}
, from right to left, are three simple contours in the left half plane from
e^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}/3}\infty
to
e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}/3}\infty
. Similarly,
\Gamma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{out}},\Gamma_{\mathrm{R}}
and
\Gamma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{in}}
, from left to right, are three simple contours in the right half plane from
e^{-\pi\mathrm{i}/3}\infty
to
e^{\pi\mathrm{i}/3}\infty
. For simplification, we assume that all these contours are symmetric about the real axis.

Each of the
\Gamma_{*}
contour above,
*\in\{\{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{out}\},\{\mathrm{L}\},\{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{in}\},\{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{out}\},\{\mathrm{R}\},\{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{in}\}\}
, can be split into two parts. One part is within the disk
\mathbb{D}(\log N)
, the disk of radius
\log N
with center
0
, and the other part is outside of this disk. We denote these two parts
\Gamma^{(N)}_{*}
and
\Gamma^{(\mathrm{err})}_{*}
. In other words, we have six contours within
\mathbb{D}(\log N)
:
\Gamma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{out}}^{(N)},\Gamma_{\mathrm{L}}^{(N)}
,
\Gamma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{in}}^{(N)}
,
\Gamma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{out}}^{(N)},\Gamma_{\mathrm{R}}^{(N)}
, and
\Gamma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{in}}^{(N)}
, and six contours outside of
\mathbb{D}(\log N)
:
\Gamma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{out}}^{(\mathrm{err})},\Gamma_{\mathrm{L}}^{(\mathrm{err})}
,
\Gamma_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{in}}^{(\mathrm{err})}
,
\Gamma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{out}}^{(\mathrm{err})},\Gamma_{\mathrm{R}}^{(\mathrm{err})}
, and
\Gamma_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{in}}^{(\mathrm{err})}
.

We now choose the six contours appearing in the terms
\hat{T}_{k_{1},k_{2}}
. We let them all intersect a neighborhood of the point

w_{c}:=-\frac{1}{1+\sqrt{\alpha}},

(3.16)

where
\alpha
is the constant in Proposition
3.1
.
We pick, for each
*\in\{\{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{out}\},\{\mathrm{L}\},\{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{in}\},\{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{out}\},\{\mathrm{R}\},\{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{in}\}\}
,
\Sigma_{*}
to be the union of two parts
\Sigma_{*}^{(N)}
and
\Sigma_{*}^{(\mathrm{err})}
. The part
\Sigma_{*}^{(N)}
lies in a neighborhood of
w_{c}
and satisfies

\Sigma_{*}^{(N)}=w_{c}+\alpha^{1/6}(1+\sqrt{\alpha})^{-4/3}N^{-1/3}\Gamma_{*}^{(N)},\quad*\in\{\{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{out}\},\{\mathrm{L}\},\{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{in}\},\{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{out}\},\{\mathrm{R}\},\{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{in}\}\}.

(3.17)

See the solid contours within the dashed circle in Figure
5
for an illustration.

Recall
f_{1}(w;t_{1})=(w+1)^{-m}w^{n}e^{t_{1}w}
and
f_{2}(w;t_{2})=(w+1)^{-M+m}w^{N-n}e^{t_{2}w}
with the parameters satisfying (
3.1
). A detailed calculation (see (
3.29
) and (
3.30
) for example) indicate that
f_{i}(w;t_{i})
behaves like a cubic-exponential function. More explicitly,
f_{i}(w;t_{i})
decays super-exponentially fast when
w
moves away from
w_{c}
along the contours
\Sigma_{*}^{(N)}
on the left, and grows super-exponentially fast along the contours
\Sigma_{*}^{(N)}
on the right. Moreover, if we denote
w_{*}^{ep}
and
\overline{w_{*}^{ep}}
the endpoints of
\Sigma_{*}^{(N)}
, using (
3.29
) and (
3.30
), we have
|f_{i}(w_{*}^{ep},t_{i})/f_{i}(w_{c};t_{i})|\leq e^{-c(\log N)^{3}}
when
w_{*}^{ep}
is on the left contours, and
|f_{i}(w_{*}^{ep};t_{i})/f_{i}(w_{c},t_{i})|\geq e^{c(\log N)^{3}}
when
w_{*}^{ep}
is on the right contours. Here
c
is some positive constant uniformly for
\mathrm{x}
in a compact interval and
\mathrm{t}_{1},\mathrm{t}_{2}
with a lower bound.

In the next step, we will define the contours
\Sigma_{*}^{(\mathrm{err})}
. Note that

f_{1}(w;t_{1})=e^{\gamma Nh(w)+\mathcal{O}(N^{2/3})},\quad f_{2}(w;t_{2})=e^{(1-\gamma)Nh(w)+\mathcal{O}(N^{2/3})},

where

h(w)=-\alpha\log(w+1)+\log w+(\sqrt{\alpha}+1)^{2}w.

(3.18)

It is standard to analyze
\mathrm{Re}h(w)
for
w\in\mathbb{C}
and extend the contours
\Sigma_{*}^{(N)}
to
\Sigma_{*}^{(\mathrm{err})}
such that

\max_{u\in\Sigma_{*}^{(\mathrm{err})}}|f_{i}(u;t_{i})|\leq\min_{u\in\Sigma_{*}^{(N)}}|f_{i}(u;t_{i})|,\qquad i=1,2,*\in\{\{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{out}\},\{\mathrm{L}\},\{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{in}\}\}

(3.19)

and

\min_{v\in\Sigma_{*}^{(\mathrm{err})}}|f_{i}(v;t_{i})|\geq\max_{v\in\Sigma_{*}^{(N)}}|f_{i}(v;t_{i})|,\qquad i=1,2,*\in\{\{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{out}\},\{\mathrm{R}\},\{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{in}\}\}

(3.20)

for sufficiently large
N
. See Figure
5
for an illustration and the figure caption for more explanation.

Figure 5:
Illustration of the contours when
\alpha=1
